at position
i^{\prime}
, namely
\pm u_{p}s_{l}
. The only remaining operations that affect row
l
are
x_{i^{\prime}}(w_{h})
during
\textsc{end}(c_{h})
for chords
c_{h}
that are head-to-tail with
c_{p}
. These operations add a same-sign multiple of the entry at
i^{\prime}
, namely
\pm s_{l}u_{p}w_{h}
, to the entry at
i^{\prime}+1
. Also in the case that
c_{p}
is a sticky child
y_{i^{\prime}}(u_{p})
occurs, now in
\textsc{start}(c_{\ast p})
rather than
c_{p}
, where
c_{\ast p}
is the ancestor at the top of the sticky chain ending in
c_{p}
. Due to the order of
y
operation in this
start
statement, row
l
cannot be modified by any other operations. In conclusion, the support of
M_{l}
is exactly
\{i^{\prime},i^{\prime}+1,i,i+1,j,j+1\}
, same as for the row
C_{l}
in the corresponding domino matrix. The six nonzero entries at these positions are respectively given by the following six expressions:

\pm s_{l}u_{p}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\pm s_{l}\left(1+u_{p}\sum_{j_{h}=i^{\prime}}w_{h}\right)\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;u_{l}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;1+u_{l}\sum_{j_{h}=i}w_{h}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\pm v_{l}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\pm v_{l}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{h}=j\\
h\geq l\end{subarray}}w_{h}

where the last sum is over
c_{l}
and its same-end descendants.
According to the domino form, these expressions should equal
(\varepsilon_{l}\alpha_{p},\varepsilon_{l}\beta_{p},\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},\gamma_{l},\delta_{l})
respectively. This is achieved by setting
\varepsilon_{l}=\pm s_{l}
and noting that the resulting
\alpha_{p}
and
\beta_{p}
are consistent with their definitions for the parent row
p
. The signs of the variables
\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},\gamma_{l},\delta_{l}
satisfy the domino sign rules, exactly as in the case of a top chord. The sign of
\varepsilon_{l}
is the parity of the number of
{\mathrm{inc}}
operations to the upper right of the entry at position
i^{\prime}+1
of row
l
, which is the number of chords ending between the starts of
c_{l}
and its parent
c_{p}
, which yields
(-1)^{\mathrm{beyond}(c_{l})}
as required by Definition
2.10
(4).

Sticky:

Finally, let
c_{l}=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
be a sticky child of some other chord
c_{p}=(i-1,i,j^{\prime},j^{\prime}+1)
where
p=l-1
. The effect of
\textsc{end}(c_{l})
and the descendants of
c_{l}
is similar to the nonsticky case. When we reach the start of
c_{l}
, the nonzero terms are at positions
i,i+1,j,j+1
of row
l
, since
(i+1){\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1=i
. The entry at position
i+1
is
1
as before, and the entry at position
i
is
s_{l}
with a positive sign in this case, because there is no other chord between the two starts, so no sign flip could occur. Since
c_{l}
is a sticky child, the next operations occur in
\textsc{start}(c_{\ast l})
at
c_{l}
’s sticky ancestor. There, the application of
y_{i-1}(u_{p})
creates a new nonzero entry
u_{p}s_{l}
at position
i-1
, and the subsequent application of
y_{i}(u_{l})
adds
u_{l}
to the existing
s_{l}
at position
i
. Other
y
operations in the sticky chain, before or after these two, do not affect row
l
since they can be seen to add a zero to some entry. In the case that the row is not further modified by the algorithm, the three entries at positions
i-1,i,i+1
should be, respectively, according to the domino form:

\varepsilon_{l}\alpha_{p}=s_{l}u_{p}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\varepsilon_{l}\beta_{p}+\alpha_{l}=s_{l}+u_{l}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\beta_{l}=1

This follows readily from setting
\varepsilon_{l}=s_{l}
,
\alpha_{l}=u_{l}
,
\beta_{l}=1
,
\alpha_{p}=u_{p}
, and
\beta_{p}=1
, which is consistent which our choices for
c_{p}
in cases handled above. The only remaining case where row
l
is further modified is when
c_{p}
is at the top of the sticky chain, and it is head-to-tail with other chords
c_{h}
ending at
(i-1,i)
. The
x_{i-1}(w_{h})
operations at these ends add
s_{l}u_{p}w_{h}
terms to the middle entry at
i
. These terms also result from the above definition of
\beta_{p}
, which contains
u_{p}w_{h}
terms in this head-to-tail case.

So far, we have shown that there exist
\{\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},\gamma_{l},\delta_{l},\varepsilon_{l}\}_{l=1}^{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{5k}
such that the output of the algorithm
M=\textsc{construct-matrix}(D,\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\}_{l=1}^{k})
is equal to the domino matrix
C
, and these
5k
real variables satisfy the sign rules in Definition
2.10
(1)-(4). It remains to verify that they also satisfy the two remaining
2\times 2
sign rules. For verifying Definition
2.10
(5), let
c_{l}
be a same-end child of
c_{p}
. We have seen that
\delta_{l}=\pm v_{l}\sum_{h}w_{h}
summing over
c_{l}
and its descendants, and
\gamma_{l}=\pm v_{l}
with the same sign, and similarly for
c_{p}
. Hence,

\delta_{l}/\gamma_{l}\;=\;w_{l}+\sum_{h}w_{h}\;<\;w_{p}+w_{l}+\sum_{h}w_{h}\;=\;\delta_{p}/\gamma_{p}

where the possibly-empty sum is over same-end descendants
c_{h}
of
c_{l}
. Similarly, we verify Definition
2.10
(6), for
c_{l}
and
c_{r}
head-to-tail. Recall
\alpha_{r}=u_{r}
and
\beta_{r}=1+u_{r}\sum_{h}w_{h}
summing over all
c_{h}
ending at the start of
c_{r}
. Hence,

\beta_{r}/\alpha_{r}\;=\;1/u_{r}+w_{l}+\sum_{h}w_{h}\;>\;w_{l}+\sum_{h}w_{h}\;=\;\delta_{l}/\gamma_{l}

as required. In conclusion, the output of
construct-matrix
has the domino form and satisfies the sign rules.
∎

The above proof gives formulas for the
5k
domino variables in terms of the
4k
algorithm parameters.
One may invert these formulas, and recover the parameters from the domino variables as follows.

Lemma 3.22
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram, and
C\in\mathcal{DM}_{n,k}
the corresponding domino matrix.
Let
\{\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},\gamma_{l},\delta_{l},\varepsilon_{l}\}_{1\leq l\leq k}
be a real assignment to
C
that satisfies the domino sign rules. Define the parameters
\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\}_{1\leq l\leq k}
recursively as follows.

u_{l}=\left(\frac{\beta_{l}}{\alpha_{l}}-\sum_{j_{h}=i_{l}}w_{h}\right)^{-1}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;v_{l}=\left|\frac{\gamma_{l}}{\alpha_{l}}\right|u_{l}\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;w_{l}=\left(\frac{\delta_{l}}{\gamma_{l}}-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j_{h}=j\\
h>l\end{subarray}}w_{h}\right)\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;s_{l}=\left|\frac{\varepsilon_{l}}{\alpha_{l}}\right|u_{l}

Here, we always handle next the leftmost lowest chord so-far unhandled. The first sum is over chords
c_{h}
head-to-tail with
c_{l}
, and the second sum is over same-end descendants
c_{h}
of
c_{l}
, excluding
c_{l}
itself.

Then, these
4k
parameters are positive, and their assignment to
\textsc{construct-matrix}(D)
yields the domino matrix
C
up to rescaling each set
(\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},\gamma_{l},\delta_{l},\varepsilon_{l})
by a positive factor for every
l
.

Proof.

First, by induction, for every
c_{l}
, the ratio
\delta_{l}/\gamma_{l}
equals the sum
\sum_{h}w_{h}
over
c_{l}
and its same-end descendants. Hence
u_{l}
is positive by the sign rule
\beta_{l}/\alpha_{l}>\delta_{h}/\gamma_{h}
, and
s_{l}
is positive by the sign rule
\delta_{l}/\gamma_{l}>\delta_{h}/\gamma_{h}
. Clearly,
v_{l}
and
s_{l}
are positive as well. It is now straightforward that the formulas
(\ast)
in the proof of Proposition
3.21
recover the variables
\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},\gamma_{l},\delta_{l},\varepsilon_{l}
and their signs, up to a common positive factor.
∎

3.3
The Generated Cell

The algorithm in Section
3.2
takes a chord diagram
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
and generates a matrix
C\in\mathcal{DM}_{n,k}
, shown to have the domino form corresponding to
D
as defined in Section
2.2
. By our definitions in Section
2.4
, the chord diagram
D
also directly corresponds to a decorated permutation
\pi\in\mathcal{DP}_{n,k}
. This permutation
\pi
corresponds in turn to a positroid cell
S_{D}\in\mathcal{BCFW}_{n,k}
in a unique and standard manner, see Definitions
2.21
and
2.28
and the references in Section
2.3
. Besides, the generated matrix
C
, for any assignment of
4k
positive numbers, lies in a particular positroid cell
S
, as observed in Remark
3.19
. If
\sigma
is the decorated permutation of
S
, then our goal is to show that
\sigma=\pi
, and therefore
S=S_{D}
. To that end, we first turn to analyze Algorithm
3.13
and describe its output in terms of decorated permutations.

Definition 3.23
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram. Consider the following construction running in parallel to the algorithm
construct-matrix
on
D
:

1.

Start with the one-element permutation
\sigma:\{n\}\to\{n\}
.

2.

Whenever
{\mathrm{pre}}_{m}
is applied, augment
\sigma
by the fixed point,
\sigma(m)=m
.

3.

Whenever
{\mathrm{inc}}_{i+1;l}
is applied, augment
\sigma
by the white fixed point,
\sigma(i+1)=\overline{i+1}
.

4.

Whenever
x_{j}(t)
is applied, update
\sigma
to be
\sigma\cdot(j\penalty\ \penalty\ j{\pluscirc}1)
.

5.

Whenever
y_{j}(t)
is applied, update
\sigma
to be
(j\penalty\ \penalty\ j{\pluscirc}1)\cdot\sigma
.

The resulting decorated permutation
\sigma\in S_{n}
is called the
algorithmic permutation
of
D
.

Remark 3.24
.

At any point of this process, we have a permutation
\sigma:N\to N
, for the current index set
N
maintained by the algorithm. Thus, the adjacent transpositions
(j\penalty\ \penalty\ j{\pluscirc}1)
are with respect to the current state of the index set
N
at the moment of application. The apparent distinction between black and white fixed points will never be important, due to transpositions applied later at these points.

Remark 3.25
.

One can also describe how the composed operation
\overleftrightarrow{\iota}_{i,l,r}
acts on permutations. It corresponds to adding a white fixed point at
i
, and then multiplying by the cycle
(i\penalty\ (i{{\pluscirc}1})\penalty\ \cdots\penalty\ (i{{\pluscirc}r}))
from the right and by
(i\penalty\ (i{{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1})\penalty\ \cdots\penalty\ (i{{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}l}))
from the left. For example, the upper embedding
\overleftrightarrow{\;\textsc{upp}\;}_{1}=\overleftrightarrow{\iota}_{1,3,1}

corresponds to
(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)
from the left and
(1\penalty\ 2)
from the right.

We also give the following, equivalent but more explicit, definition for the algorithmic permutation of a given chord diagram. For stating this definition, recall that the chords in a diagram are always noncrossing. Thus, for head-to-tail chords, the end of the first is said to come before the start of the second one, even though they are both in the same segment. Similarly, if we order the chords increasingly by their ends, then same-end chords are ordered with decreasing starts.

Definition 3.26
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
and
C=\textsc{construct-matrix}(D,\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\})
. Then the
algorithmic permutation
corresponding to
D
and
C
is the following composition of 2-cycles and 3-cycles:

\sigma\;=\;(p_{1}\penalty\ q_{1})\penalty\ (p_{2}\penalty\ q_{2})\cdots(p_{2k}\penalty\ q_{2k})\,\cdot\,(i_{k}+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j_{k}\penalty\ \penalty\ j_{k}+1)\cdots(i_{1}+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j_{1}\penalty\ \penalty\ j_{1}+1)\;\in\;S_{n}

where

•

The terms
(i_{l}+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j_{l}\penalty\ \penalty\ j_{l}+1)
correspond to the
k
chords
c^{\prime}_{l}=(i_{l},i_{l}+1,j_{l},j_{l}+1)
, where
c^{\prime}_{1},\dots,c^{\prime}_{k}
are indexed increasingly by the occurrence of their ends along
D
.

•

The terms
(p_{m}\penalty\ q_{m})
correspond to the sequence of
endpoints
, both starts and ends, in order of occurrence along the diagram
D
, and determined as follows:

–

If the
m
th endpoint is a start of some chord
c_{l}=(i_{l},i_{l}+1,j_{l},j_{l}+1)
then
(p_{m}\penalty\ q_{m})=(i_{l}\penalty\ \penalty\ i_{l\ast}+1)
, using the notation
c_{l\ast}
for a maximal sticky descendant of
c_{l}
, and as usual possibly
c_{l}=c_{l\ast}
.

–

If the
m
th endpoint is a end of some chord
c_{l}
then this term swaps the following elements:

*

p_{m}=i_{l}+1
, i.e., the second marker at the
start
of
c_{l}
.

*

q_{m}=\begin{cases}n&\text{if }c_{l}\text{ is a top chord,}\\
i_{h}+1&\text{if }c_{l}\text{ is a child of another chord }c_{h}.\end{cases}

Example 3.27
.

For
D=\left([14],\,\left((1,2,11,12),(3,4,6,7),(8,9,10,11)\right)\right)
of Examples
2.3
and
3.16
:

\sigma\;=\;(1\penalty\ 2)\penalty\ (3\penalty\ 4)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 4)\penalty\ (8\penalty\ 9)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 9)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 14)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 11\penalty\ 12)\penalty\ (9\penalty\ 10\penalty\ 11)\penalty\ (4\penalty\ 6\penalty\ 7)

This permutation coincides with Example
2.26
, as we later prove in general.

Example 3.28
.

For
D=\left([8],\,\left((1,2,6,7),(2,3,4,5),(4,5,6,7)\right)\right)
of Example
3.17
:

\sigma\;=\;(1\penalty\ 3)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 3)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 3)\penalty\ (4\penalty\ 5)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 5)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 8)\penalty\ (2\penalty\ 6\penalty\ 7)\penalty\ (5\penalty\ 6\penalty\ 7)\penalty\ (3\penalty\ 4\penalty\ 5)

Note that the leftmost transposition is
(1\penalty\ 3)
rather than
(1\penalty\ 2)
, because the chord has a sticky child.

Remark 3.29
.

If there are several top chords
c_{(1)},\dots,c_{(g)}
in
D
, indexed left to right, then the product in Definition
3.26
admits a useful factorization, grouping together the descendants of each top chord:

\sigma\;=\;\tau_{1}\cdot\tau_{2}\cdots\tau_{g}\,\cdot\,\rho_{g}\cdot\rho_{g-1}\cdots\rho_{1}

where
\tau_{h}
is the product over transpositions
(p_{m}\penalty\ q_{m})
that arise from starts and ends of
c_{(h)}
and its descendants, and
\rho_{h}
is the product of 3-cycles that arise from their ends.

Lemma 3.30
.

Definitions
3.23
and
3.26
are equivalent.

Proof.

The computation is straightforward. The contribution of the
x_{j}(t)
applications happens at the end of every chord, and thus according to the reverse order of these ends, which is
c^{\prime}_{k},\dots,c^{\prime}_{1}
as in Definition
3.26
. At each end of a chord
c_{l}^{\prime}=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
, the algorithm applies
x_{i+1}(v_{l})
and then
x_{j}(w_{l})
. These operations right-multiply
\sigma
by
(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j)(j\penalty\ \penalty\ j+1)=(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j\penalty\ \penalty\ j+1)
, noting that these three indices are adjacent in
N
at that moment. Repeating for all chord ends, this yields the 3-cycles in the right half of the algorithmic permutation
\sigma
of Definition
3.26
.

For the 2-cycles, first assume that there are no sticky chords in
D
. In this case, the applications of
y_{j}(t)
happen at every end and start of a chord according to their reverse order of occurrence along the diagram
D
. This is indeed the order used in the definition of the terms
(p_{m}\penalty\ q_{m})
in Definition
3.26
. At every start of a chord
c_{l}=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
, an application of
y_{i}(u_{l})
contributes the left-factor
(i\penalty\ \penalty\ i+1)
. At every end the contribution of
y_{(i+1){\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}(s_{l})
gives the left-factor
(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ n)
if
c_{l}
is a top chord, and
(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ i^{\prime}+1)
otherwise where
c_{h}=(i^{\prime},i^{\prime}+1,j^{\prime},j^{\prime}+1)
is
c_{l}
’s parent. This gives the product of 2-cycles in the left half of the algorithmic permutation in Definition
3.26
.

What if there are sticky chains in
D
? In this case the
y_{i}(u_{l})
applications at starts are applied in the opposite order of their occurrence when going back on the diagram. Suppose that
(c_{l},\dots,c_{l+h})
is a maximal descending sticky chain in
D
. Then we apply the operations
y_{i+h}(u_{l+h})\circ\dots\circ y_{i+1}(u_{l+1})\circ y_{i}(u_{l})
. This yields the product of transpositions

(i+h\penalty\ \penalty\ i+h+1)\penalty\ \cdots\penalty\ (i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ i+2)\penalty\ (i\penalty\ \penalty\ i+1)\;=\;(i\penalty\ \penalty\ i+h+1)\penalty\ (i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ i+h+1)\penalty\ \cdots\penalty\ (i+h\penalty\ \penalty\ i+h+1)

where the verification of this equality is routine. Now the 2-cycles are listed in order of occurrence, though the contribution of every start is
(i\penalty\ \penalty\ i^{\prime}+1)
where
c_{l\ast}=c_{l+h}
is its maximal sticky descendant. In conclusion, the construction described in the Definition
3.23
yields the algorithmic permutation
\sigma
of
D
as in Definition
3.26
.
∎

Note that Lemma
3.30
does not yet claim that the matrices generated by
construct-matrix
lie in the positroid cell corresponding to the decorated permutation
\sigma
. As a first step, we show that they lie in some
4k
-dimensional cell.

Lemma 3.31
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram, and let
S
be the positroid cell that arises from
\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D,\,\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\}_{l=1}^{k}\right)
. Every point in
S
is obtained from a unique choice of the
4k
positive variables. Hence,
S
is
4k
-dimensional.

In the proof of lemma
3.31
, we need the following useful lemma, which serves us for other purposes later in the paper. It is stated here and proven below, at the end of this Section
3.3
. We note that
\binom{[n]}{k}
is the set of
k
-element subsets of
[n]
.

Lemma 3.32
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram with a top chord
c=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
, and let
S\subseteq\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}
be all the points representable by a domino matrix of the form corresponding to
D
. Then,

•

For every
V\in S
, the Plücker coordinate
P_{I}(V)\neq 0
only if
I
intersects
A=\{i,i+1,j,j+1,n\}
.

•

For every
p\in A
, there exists
I\in\tbinom{[n]}{k}
such that
I\cap A=\{p\}
and
P_{I}(V)\neq 0
for every
V\in S
.

•

In particular, no
V\in S
contains a nonzero vector supported on a proper subset of
A
.

Proof of Lemma
3.31
.

The proof goes by establishing an inverse map. We proceed by induction on
k
, the number of chords. For a chord diagram
D_{a}
, we prove that that given an output of
construct-matrix
(D_{a})
in the corresponding positroid cell
S_{a}
, all the real parameters of the
x_{i}
and
y_{i}
operations in the algorithm can be uniquely determined. The claim is obviously true when
k=1
.

Suppose we have shown the claim for less than
k
chords.
If
D_{a}
has at least two top chords, denote by
(i,i+1)
the end of the first top chord
c_{1}
, and let
D_{b}
be the subdiagram made of chords which end no later than
(i,i+1),
so that all its chords are
c_{1}
and its descendants, and the marker set is
[i+1]\cup\{n\}
.
Let
D_{c}
be the subdiagram made of the other chords, with markers
[n]\setminus[i-1].

Suppose that
D_{b}
has
k^{\prime}
chords.

Let
C
be a domino representative of
V\in S_{a}.

C
can be written as

C=\begin{pmatrix}C^{\prime}\\
C^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix},

where
C^{\prime}
is the submatrix which consists of the upper
k^{\prime}
rows and
C^{\prime\prime}
is the submatrix which consists of the remaining rows. Denote by
V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime}
the row spans of
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
respectively.
Algorithm
3.13
is structured so that the construction of
C^{\prime}
is independent of the construction of
C^{\prime\prime}
which precedes it, as can be immediately seen from its recursive form, Algorithm
3.20
.
In other words,

C^{\prime}\;=\;{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\cdots{\mathrm{pre}}_{i+2}\;\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D_{b},\,\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k^{\prime}]}\right).

Similarly,

C^{\prime\prime}
is almost untouched by the construction of
C^{\prime}.
They are only affected when operations of the form
x_{i}(w_{h})
are performed in
end
(c_{h})
for
c_{h}
which ends at
(i,i+1),

h\in[k^{\prime}]
.
If
w
is the sum of all those
w_{h},
then

C^{\prime\prime}\;=\;{\mathrm{pre}}_{i-1}\cdots{\mathrm{pre}}_{1}\;x_{i}(w)\;\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D_{c},\,\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k]\setminus[k^{\prime}]}\right)

We claim that
V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime}
are uniquely determined from
V.

Indeed, if some linear combination of rows, which involves at least one of the
k-k^{\prime}
rows nontrivially lies in
V^{\prime},
then since all vectors in
V^{\prime}
have zero entries between
i+2
and
n-1,
there must be some nontrivial linear combination
v
of rows from
V^{\prime\prime}
which has zeros in these places as well. But all vectors in
V^{\prime\prime}
have zeros at entries
1,\ldots,i-1,
thus, this linear combination must be supported on
\{i,i+1,n\}.
Lemma
3.32
applied to
c_{1}
tell us that there is a set
I\in\binom{[n]\setminus\{i,i+1,n\}}{k}
with
P_{I}(V)\neq 0.
But if we take a representative matrix
M
for
V
which contains
v
as one of the rows, expanding
\det(M^{I})
with respect to the row of
v
shows that
P_{I}(V)
must be zero, which is a contradiction. A similar claim holds for
V^{\prime\prime}.

Applying the induction hypothesis on
V^{\prime}
allows us to uniquely calculate all
4k^{\prime}
positive parameters
\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k^{\prime}]}.
It also allows us to calculate the parameter
w
in
x_{i}(w)
above, since it is a sum of some of these
w_{h}
variables.
Applying the induction hypothesis on
x_{i}(-w).V^{\prime\prime}
allows us to uniquely calculate all parameters
\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k]\setminus[k^{\prime}]}.

The remaining case is that
D_{a}
has a single top chord
c_{1}
.
We may assume it is a long chord, starting at
(1,2)
and ending at
(n-2,n-1),
or otherwise we can erase zero columns, and recover the algorithm variables for the smaller problem, which does satisfy these assumptions.
Let
D_{b}
be the subdiagram made of
c_{1}
’s descendants with index set
[n]\setminus[1].

Take
V\in S_{a},
and let
C
be a domino representative of
V
.
We can write

C=\begin{pmatrix}C_{1}\\
C^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}.

The submatrix
C^{\prime}
obtained from
C
by erasing the first row is not a domino matrix for
S_{b}.
But it equals

{\mathrm{pre}}_{n}\mathrm{rescale}_{1}(u_{1})\;x_{1}(1)\;\mathrm{rotate}_{k-1}\;\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D_{b},\,\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k]\setminus[1]}\right)

Here,
\mathrm{rotate}_{k}
is the circular shift that takes the last column, puts it first, relabels it as
1
, and multiplies it by
(-1)^{k-1}
in order to preserve positivity.
\mathrm{rescale}_{1}(t)
multiplies the first column by
t
.

Both
\mathrm{span}(C_{1})
and
V^{\prime}
are uniquely determined by
V,
by an argument similar to that used to distinguish
V^{\prime}
and
V^{\prime\prime}
in the previous case. Thus,
C_{1}
is the unique vector in
\mathrm{span}(C_{1})
whose second entry is
1.
The variables
\{s_{1},u_{1},v_{1}\}
are read from its
n
th,
1
st and
(n-2)
th coordinates respectively.
w_{1}
cannot be determined yet, since there may applications of
x_{n-2}(w_{l})
for same-end descendants
c_{l}
of
c_{1}.

Similarly, starting from
V^{\prime},
projecting on the first
n-1
columns and undoing the reversible
\mathrm{rescale}_{1}(u_{1}),\penalty\ x_{1}(1),\penalty\ \mathrm{rotate}_{k-1}
operations we obtain a vector space in
S_{b}
which has a domino representation

\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D_{b},\,\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k]\setminus[1]}\right).

By induction this representation is unique, and we can determine its parameters.
Now, if we define
w
to be the sum of
w_{l},
over same-end descendants
c_{l}
of
c_{1},
a simple calculation reveals that
w_{1}
is precisely the ratio between the
(n-1)
th entry of
x_{n-2}(-w).C_{1}
and its
(n-2)
th entry. Thus, all parameters are now found, as required.
∎

Example 3.33
.

Recall the two chord diagrams considered in Examples
3.16
-
3.17
.
Consider first the subdiagrams which contain only
c_{2},c_{3}.

The matrix of the subdiagram

(\{2,\ldots,14\},((3,4,6,7),(8,9,10,11))),
is

\begin{array}[]{*{13}{c}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}2&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}3&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}4&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}5&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}6&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}7&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}8&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}9&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}10&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}11&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}12&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}13&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}14\\
\hline\cr 0&u_{2}&1&0&v_{2}&w_{2}v_{2}&0&0&0&0&0&0&-s_{2}\\
0&0&0&0&0&0&u_{3}&1&v_{3}&w_{3}v_{3}&0&0&s_{3}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}\vskip 5.0pt

and the matrix of the subdiagram
(\{2,\ldots,8\},((2,3,4,5),(4,5,6,7)))
is

\begin{array}[]{*{7}{c}}\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}2&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}3&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}4&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}5&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}6&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}7&\color[rgb]{0.75,0.75,0.75}8\\
\hline\cr u_{2}&1&v_{2}&v_{2}w_{2}&0&0&-s_{2}\\
0&0&u_{3}&1+w_{2}u_{3}&v_{3}&w_{3}v_{3}&s_{3}\\
\hline\cr\end{array}\vskip 5.0pt

respectively.
In both cases it is easy to see that each row vector is uniquely determined, after fixing its second entry to be
1,
from the two dimensional vector space.
In the first case the construction by the algorithm of each row is independent of the other. In the second the construction of the top row is independent of the bottom row. After performing
x_{4}(-w_{2})
on the bottom row, where
w_{2}
is determined from the first row, and throwing zeros, we are left with the row vector which is the output of the generating algorithm to the chord diagram made of
c_{3}.

If we compare the matrices considered in this example, to the matrices constructed in Examples
3.16
-
3.17
, which correspond to adding
c_{1}
we first observe that indeed the row which corresponds to
c_{1}
is uniquely determined in both cases. We also see that the submatrices made of the rows which correspond to
c_{2},c_{3}
are indeed related to the matrices considered in this example by first shifting the last column to be the first, and fixing the sign. Then acting with
x_{1}(1),
scaling the first column by
u_{1}
and adding a zero column as a last column.

Remark 3.34
.

The nonzero entries of a matrix in domino form can be written, after one fixes some nonzero entry in each row to be
1
as rational functions in the Plücker coordinates. This can be seen by undoing Algorithm
3.13
, every time we undo a
x_{i}
or
y_{i}
operation we use
[
Lam14
, Proposition 7.10]
to write its parameter using the Plücker coordinates of the matrix at the time of the action, and to update the new Plücker coordinate in terms of the previous ones. We omit the details.

In the beginning of this section Section
3.3
, we have defined the algorithmic permutation
\sigma
of
D
, made of
4k
transpositions that record steps along
construct-matrix
. By Lemma
3.31
, the positroid cell
S
generated by the same
\textsc{construct-matrix}(D)
attains its largest possible dimension,
4k
. By the discussion in Summary
3.11
, we can deduce that these two algorithmic outcomes bear the standard correspondence between a decorated permutation and a positroid cell.

Corollary 3.35
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram. The decorated permutation of the positroid cell
S
that arises from
construct-matrix(
D
)
is the algorithmic permutation
\sigma
of
D
.

Remark 3.36
.

Our approach for showing that the BCFW cells are
4k
-dimensional is directly from the matrix construction and self-contained. Since we also show that the decorated permutations of these cells are given by other equivalent combinatorial descriptions, and coincide with BCFW cells defined elsewhere in the literature, we could also deduce this fact differently, e.g. as these permutations correspond to reduced plabic graphs with
4k+1
faces. Our direct approach in Lemmas
3.31
and
3.32
will serve us well in Section
7
.

We now give the proof of Lemma
3.32
.

Proof of Lemma
3.32
.

The first part is straightforward. Consider the domino form
C
of
V.
Then by expanding
\det(C^{I})
with respect to the row which corresponds to
c
we see that this determinant vanishes unless
I
intersects the support of this row, which is precisely
A.

For the proof of the second part, we first introduce the following terminology. A sequence of chords
(c,c^{\prime},c^{\prime\prime\prime},\dots)
is a
Loch Ness Monster
if all consecutive pairs of chords are head-to-tail. For example, the sequence
(c_{2},c_{3},c_{4},c_{6})
in Example
2.31
is a Loch Ness Monster.

We prove the second part of the lemma by constructing a set
I
of size
k
, consists of one element for each chord, such that
\det(C^{I})\neq 0
. The set
I
has the form
I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup I_{3}\cup\{p\},
where
I_{1}\subseteq[i-1],\penalty\ I_{2}\subseteq\{i+2,\ldots,j-1\},
and
I_{3}\subseteq\{j+2,\ldots,n-1\}
are defined as follows.

1.

For
I_{1}
we take the set of first markers of all chords that start before
c
.

2.

I_{2}
is the set of second markers for all sticky descendants
c_{h}
of
c
, together with the first markers of all other descendant of
c
. Recall that the first two markers are the start of the chord, and not the markers inherited from its parent, which may come earlier.

3.

We construct
I_{3}
as follows.
Denote by
c_{1}
the first chord which starts after
c
ends.

•

If
c_{1}
starts at
(j^{\prime},j^{\prime}+1)
for
j^{\prime}>j+1
we take for
I_{3}
the set of first markers of each chord which starts
c
ends.

•

If
c_{1}
starts at
(j+1,j+2),
then we act as we did for
I_{2},
namely, for
c_{1}
and any other chord in a maximal sticky chain from
c_{1}
we add to
I_{3}
the second marker, and for all the remaining chords which start afterwards we choose the first marker.

•

Finally, if
c_{1}
starts at
(j,j+1),
we write
c_{0}=c
and we initiate
I_{3}
to be the empty set. Write
c_{2},c_{3},\ldots,c_{h}
for a maximal Loch Ness Monster which starts at
c_{0}.

Put
j_{0}=j
and
(j_{h},j_{h}+1)
for the end of
c_{h}.
Observe that each
c_{l}

must be a top chord.

For each
l\in[h]
first we add
j_{l}
to
I_{3}.

Then, for any descendant in a sticky chain from
c_{l}
, add the second marker. For any other chord which descends from
c_{l}
we add the first marker.

Denote by
\tilde{c}
the first chord which starts after
c_{h}
ends.
\tilde{c}
is a top chord, and we denote its start by
(\tilde{j},\tilde{j}+1).

\tilde{j}>j_{h},
since
\tilde{c}
does not belong to the Loch Ness Monster. If
\tilde{j}>j_{h}+1,
add
\tilde{j}
to
I_{3},
as well as the first marker of every chord that comes after it.

If
\tilde{j}=j_{h}+1
then for each sticky descendant
\tilde{c}
of it, take the second marker, while for the remaining chords take the first marker.

Note that all added entries are different, since chords start at different markers, and the third marker of a chord
c_{l}
can never be the first or second marker of any other chord, except
c_{l+1}.
Therefore, the size of
I
is
k
, and is easily seen not to intersect
A
.
Moreover, if
l\leq h
then
j_{l}+1\notin I
by construction.

We now change the matrix
C,
by applying row operations, to another matrix
\bar{C}
which is more convenient to analyze, and
\det(C^{I})\neq 0
if and only if
\det(\bar{C}^{I})\neq 0.

We define
\bar{C}
in two steps.

•

First apply row operations that cancel the domino inherited by the parent, and make the first marker of every chord
c^{\prime}
be the first nonzero position in the row of
c^{\prime}.

This is done by going parent-to-child, for each chord
c_{l}
we subtract
\varepsilon_{l}
times its parent’s chord, which cancels the domino inherited to that chord by its parent. These operations result in adding nonzero entries, but only to the right of the starting domino of each chord’s column. Moreover, the first domino of each row is untouched, and rows that correspond to top chords, like
c,
are not affected.

•

Then for every chord
c^{\prime}\in\{c,c_{1},\ldots,c_{h}\}
or
c^{\prime}=\tilde{c},
and
\tilde{j}=j_{l}+1,
we act iteratively on the sticky chain which descends from
c^{\prime},
in parent-to-child order. For every parent we subtract a multiple of its row from its sticky child in order to cancel the first domino entry of the latter.
The second domino entries remain unchanged during this process.

These actions may add nonzero entries at positions which are after the end of each chord. They may also add nonzero entries before the start of a chord, but in a limited way:
If
c^{\prime}=c
they may add nonzero entries at column
i.

If
c^{\prime}=c_{l},
they may add nonzero entries at column
j_{l-1}.

If
c^{\prime}=\tilde{c},
the may add nonzero entries at column
j_{h}.

The rows of
\{c,c_{1},\ldots,c_{h},\tilde{c}\}
and non-sticky descendants do not change.

Consider the matrix
\bar{C}^{I}.

Its restriction to the first
|I_{1}|
rows and columns is upper triangular with nonzero diagonal. Thus, it is nonsingular precisely if the matrix
\tilde{C}=\bar{C}^{I\setminus I_{1}}_{[k]\setminus[|I_{1}|]},
obtained from it by erasing the first
|I_{1}|
rows and columns is nonsingular.

The first row of this matrix corresponds to
c.
In this row there is a single nonzero entry, column indexed
p.
Thus,
\tilde{C}
is nonsingular precisely if the matrix
C^{\prime},
obtained from
\tilde{C}
by erasing the first row and the column indexed
p
is nonsingular.

Now, by the construction of
\bar{C}
and of
I_{2},
which misses
i,i+1

the restriction of
C^{\prime}
to its first
|I_{2}|
rows and columns is again upper triangular, hence
C^{\prime}
is nonsingular precisely if
C^{(1)},
obtained from
C^{\prime}
by erasing these rows and columns is nonsingular.

We iteratively construct
C^{(l+1)}
from
C^{(l)}
which is nonsingular precisely if
C^{(l)}
is nonsingular in a similar fashion. We first note that in
C^{(l)}
the row which corresponds to
c_{l}
has nonzero entry only in the location which corresponds to column
j_{l}
. Indeed
c_{l}=(j_{j-1},j_{j-1}+1,j_{l},j_{l}+1,n)
, as remarked above
j_{l}+1\notin I,
and
\{j_{j-1},j_{j-1}+1,n\}
either do not belong to
I,
or were removed from the column set in previous steps. Erasing the row which corresponds to
c_{l}
and column
j_{l}
we obtain a matrix whose restriction to the first
\mathrm{below}(c_{l})
rows and columns is upper triangular with nonzero diagonal, where we remind that
\mathrm{below}(c_{l})
is the number of chords which descend from
c_{l}.
Erasing also these rows and columns we obtain
C^{(l+1)}.

Continuing like this, we end at
C^{(h+1)}.

By the same reasoning this matrix is upper triangular with nonzero diagonal, hence invertible. As needed.

For the third part of the lemma, suppose that there is a vector in
V
spanned by
\{\mathrm{e}_{h}\}_{h\in A\setminus\{p\}}.
Take
I\in\binom{[n]}{k}
with
I\cap A=\{p\}
and
P_{I}(V)\neq 0.
Its existence implies that the support of every nontrivial linear combination of the elements of
V
must contain an element out of
A\setminus\{p\}.

∎

3.4
Agreement of Permutations

Recall Definition
2.25
of
\pi
, the decorated permutation of
[n]
associated directly to a chord diagram
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
, which was used in Definition
2.28
of the BCFW positroid cell of
D
. On the other hand,
D
gives rise to a positroid cell via the algorithm
\textsc{construct-matrix}(D)
, and by Corollary
3.35
the decorated permutation of that cell is the algorithmic permutation
\sigma
of
D
, from Definition
3.26
. The next two lemmas show that
\pi
and
\sigma
admit an identical inductive characterization. It follows that
\sigma=\pi
and the algorithm
construct-matrix
indeed produces the BCFW cell of
D
.

Lemma 3.37
.

Let
D_{a}\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
. The permutation

\sigma_{a}
satisfies the following properties, and is uniquely determined by them.

1.

If
k=1
and
c_{1}=(i,i+1,l,l+1)
then
\sigma_{a}=(i\penalty\ (i+1)\penalty\ l\penalty\ (l+1)\penalty\ n).

2.

If
D_{a}
contains a top chord
c_{j}=(i,i+1,l,l+1)
, then let
D_{c}
be the subdiagram made of
c_{j}
and the chords that start after it, including its descendants, with index set
N_{c}=[n]\setminus[i-1],
and let
D_{b}
be the subdiagram made of all the remaining chords, with index set
N_{b}=[i-1]\cup\{i_{j\ast}+1,l,n\},
where
(i_{j\ast},i_{j\ast}+1)
is the start of the last descendant of
c_{j}
in a sticky chain, and we relabel markers so that
i
is relabeled as
i_{j\ast}+1
and
i+1
as
l
, so that chord which ends at
(i,i+1)
in
D_{a}
ends in
D_{b}
at
(i_{j\ast}+1,l)
while every chord that ends at
(i-1,i)
in
D_{a}
ends at
(i-1,i_{j\ast}+1)
in
D_{b}
.
Then
\sigma_{a}=\sigma_{b}\sigma_{c},
where the permutations are extended to
[n]
by adding black fixed points outside of
N_{b},N_{c}
.

3.

Suppose that
D_{a}
is obtained from
D_{b}
by a
combinatorial upper embedding
: adding a new long
top chord
c_{1}
which starts at
(1,2)
and ends at
(n-2,n-1).
Then

\sigma_{a}=(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)\sigma_{b}(1\penalty\ 2),

where
\sigma_{b}
is the decorated permutation of
D_{b},
whose index set is
[n]\setminus\{1\}.

Proof.

The first item is straightforward. For the second item, note that by Remark
3.29
the algorithmic permutation can be written as

\sigma_{a}\;=\;\tau_{b}\,\tau_{c}\,\rho_{c}\,\rho_{b},

If in
D_{b}
all chords end before
i,
then the two permutations commute and the effect of the replacements
i\to i_{j\ast}+1,\penalty\ i+1\to l
in
D_{b}
is trivial, and the claim holds.

If in
D_{b}
there are some chords which end at
(i-1,i)
but no chord ends at
(i,i+1)
then
\rho_{c},\tau_{c}
commute, since their periods do not contain common indices: in
\rho_{c}
appear only elements of the form
i_{h}+1,l_{h},l_{h}+1
for chords
c_{h}=(i_{h},i_{h}+1,l_{h},l_{h}+1)
in
D_{c}
, and these are always larger than the corresponding terms for
D_{b}.

Also most transpositions of
\tau_{c}
are of indices which do not appear in
D_{b}.
The only exclusion is the leftmost transposition in
\tau_{c}
which is
(i\penalty\ i_{j\ast}+1).
It has a common index only with triplets of
D_{b}
of the form
(p+1\penalty\ i-1\penalty\ i).

Commuting to such elements gives

(i\penalty\ i_{j\ast}+1)(p+1\penalty\ i-1\penalty\ i)=(p+1\penalty\ i-1\penalty\ i_{j\ast}+1)(i\penalty\ i_{j\ast}+1).

Thus, the commutation of
(i,i_{j\ast}+1)
with
\rho_{b}
has the prescribed effect. Since
i
does not appear in
\tau_{b}
the claim also follows in this case.

The final case is when there are chords in
D_{b}
which end at
(i,i+1)
and possibly also chords which ends at
(i-1,i).
Note that the latter appears in
\rho_{b}
to the right of the former.
The only element in
\rho_{c}
which shares an index with elements of
\rho_{b}
is
((i+1)\penalty\ l\penalty\ (l+1))
which does not commute only with elements of the form
((p+1)\penalty\ i\penalty\ (i+1)).

Commuting such elements results in replacing each appearance of
i+1
in
\rho_{b}
by
l,
since

((i+1)\penalty\ l\penalty\ (l+1))((p+1)\penalty\ i\penalty\ (i+1))=((p+1\penalty\ i\penalty\ l)((i+1)\penalty\ l\penalty\ (l+1)).

After moving
\rho_{c}
to the right of
\rho_{b}
the only term of
\tau_{c}
which does not commute with elements in its right is again
(i\penalty\ i_{j\ast}+1)
which does not commute with elements of the form
((p+1)\penalty\ (i-1)\penalty\ i)
or
((p+1)\penalty\ i\penalty\ l).
Again the effect of commuting it with them is
i\to i_{j\ast}+1
in the terms of
b.

Since again
i,i+1
do not appear in
\tau_{b}
also in this case the result follows.

For the third item, we can write

\sigma_{a}=(1\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))\tau_{b}(2\penalty\ n)(2\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1))\rho_{b},

where
\tau_{b},\rho_{b}
are as in the previous item.
The required permutation
\sigma_{b}
is obtained from
\tau_{b}\rho_{b}
by replacing every term of the form
(2\penalty\ (i_{h}+1)),
for children
c_{h}=(i_{h},i_{h}+1,l_{h},l_{h}+1)
of
c_{1}
, by
(2\penalty\ n).

We first move
(1\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))
to the right. If in
D_{b}
no chord starts at
2
then
i_{1\ast}+1=2
and
(1\penalty\ 2)
commutes with
\tau_{b}.

Otherwise
\tau_{b}
is of the form

(2\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))(3\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))\cdots(i_{1\ast}\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))\sigma^{\prime},

\sigma^{\prime}
does not contain
1
but it contains
(2\penalty\ (i_{h}+1))
for every child
c_{h}=(i_{h},i_{h}+1,l_{h},l_{h}+1)
of
c_{1}.

Commuting with
(1\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))
we obtain that

(1\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))\tau_{b}=\tilde{\tau}_{b}(1\penalty\ 2)

where
\tilde{\tau}_{b}
is obtained from
\tau_{b}
by replacing every appearance of
2
by
1,
where we have used

(1\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))(2\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))=(2\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))(1\penalty\ 2)\penalty\

to commute with
(2\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))(3\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))\cdots(i_{1\ast}\penalty\ (i_{1\ast}+1))
and

(1\penalty\ 2)(2\penalty\ (i_{h}+1))=(1\penalty\ (i_{h}+1))(1\penalty\ 2)

to commute with
\sigma^{\prime}.

Then we commute it with
(2\penalty\ n)(2\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1))=(2\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)
and obtain

(1\penalty\ 2)(2\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)=(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)(1\penalty\ 2),

and then further commute
(1\penalty\ 2)
to the right, which is possible since
\rho_{b}
does not contain
1,2
in the indices on which it acts nontrivially.

We now wish to commute
(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)
to the left of
\tilde{\tau}_{b}.
Among the indices
1,n-2,n-1,n
only
1
can appear in
\tilde{\tau}_{b},
and only in
(1\penalty\ (i_{h}+1))
terms.
Since

(1\penalty\ (i_{h}+1))(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)=(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)((i_{h}+1)\penalty\ n),

the result follows.

The fact that the permutation is uniquely determined by these properties follows from a simple induction on the number of chords.
∎

Lemma 3.38
.

\pi_{a}
satisfies the following properties, and is uniquely determined by them.

1.

If
k=1
then
\pi_{a}=(a_{1}\penalty\ (a_{1}+1)\penalty\ b_{1}\penalty\ (b_{1}+1)\penalty\ n).

2.

If
D_{a}
contains a top chord
c_{h}=(i,i+1,l,l+1),
let
D_{c}
be the subdiagram made of
c_{h},
and the chords which start after it, including its descendants, on the markers set of is
N_{c}=[n]\setminus[i-1].
Let
D_{b}
be the subdiagram made of all the remaining chords, with index set
N_{b}=[i-1]\cup\{i_{h\ast}+1,l,n\},
where
(i_{h\ast},i_{h\ast}+1)
is the end of the last chord in the sticky chain from
c_{h},
and we relabel the markers so that a chord that ends at
(i,i+1)
in
D_{a}
ends in
D_{b}
at
(i_{h\ast}+1,l)
while every chord that ends at
(i-1,i)
in
D_{a}
ends at
(i-1,i_{h\ast}+1)
in
D_{c}
.
Then
\pi_{a}=\pi_{b}\pi_{c},
where the permutations are extended to
[n]
by adding black fixed points.

3.

Suppose that
D_{a}
is obtained from
D_{b}
by a combinatorial upper embedding: adding a new long chord which starts at
(1,2)
and ends at
(n-2,n-1).
Then

\pi_{a}=(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)\pi_{b}(1\penalty\ 2),

where
\pi_{b}
is the decorated permutation of
D_{b},
whose markers set is
[n]\setminus\{1\}.

Proof.

The first item is immediate from the definition of
T,U,V,W.

The third item is also immediate:
\pi_{b}
and
\pi_{c}
are just the partial products made of the
5
-cycles which correspond to the chords of
D_{b},\penalty\ D_{c}
respectively.

For the first item, by definition we have

\pi_{a}=(T_{1}\penalty\ U_{1}\penalty\ V_{1}\penalty\ W_{1}\penalty\ n)\pi_{b},

and it is easily seen that
(T_{1}\penalty\ U_{1}\penalty\ V_{1}\penalty\ W_{1}\penalty\ n)=(1\penalty\ R\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)
where
R=2
if the next chord starts at
(j,j+1)
for
j>2,
and otherwise
R=U_{2}.

In the former case,
1,2
do not appear in
\pi_{b}
and we can split

(1\penalty\ 2\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)=(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)(1\penalty\ 2),

and commute
(1\penalty\ 2)
to the rightmost position.
In the latter case,
1
does not appear in
\pi_{b},
and
2
appears there exactly once - in the leftmost term
(2\penalty\ R_{2}).

We split

(1\penalty\ R_{2}\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)=(1\penalty\ (n-2)\penalty\ (n-1)\penalty\ n)(1\penalty\ R_{2}).

Since
(1\penalty\ R_{2})(2\penalty\ R_{2})=(2\penalty\ R_{2})(1\penalty\ 2),
we can again commute
(1\penalty\ 2)
to the rightmost position. As needed.

Simple induction shows that these properties determine the permutation uniquely.
∎

Corollary 3.39
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram. The decorated permutation
\pi
of
D
is equal to the algorithmic permutation
\sigma
of
D
.

Proof.

This is immediate from Lemmas
3.38
and
3.37
. Both permutations are determined by the same recursion.
∎

3.5
Another Algorithm

We describe a variant of Algorithms
3.13
and
3.20
, which is useful for analyzing the effect of extending a chord diagram by adding chords to the right of the given ones. The variant algorithm iterates the top chords in opposite order, increasing in their occurrence along
D
, rather than decreasing. The descendants of each chord are scanned in decreasing order as before.

Algorithm 3.40
.

Given a chord diagram
D
, The algorithm

construct-matrix-rightwards

is the following variation of
construct-matrix
from Algorithm
3.13
. The variables
M,N,K,\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\}^{k}_{l=1}
are as before. The statements
start
,
fill
and the subroutine
sub-construct-matrix
are as in Algorithms
3.13
and
3.20
and not restated here. Denote the top chords of
D
by
c_{(1)},\dots,c_{(g)}
in
increasing
order, from first to last. The notation
c_{1},\dots,c_{l},\dots,c_{k}
is reserved for the usual startwise increasing ordering.

construct-matrix-rightwards
(chord diagram
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
,  variables
s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\in\mathbb{R}
for
l\in[k]
)

initialize:
Let
N=\{n\}
,
K=\emptyset
, and an empty matrix
M\in\mathrm{Mat}_{K\times N}

for
every
c_{(h)}=(i_{h},i_{h}+1,j_{h},j_{h}+1)=c_{l}

in

(c_{(1)},\dots,c_{(g)})

do

for

m

in

(j_{h-1}+2,j_{h-1}+3,\dots,i_{h},i_{h}+1,j_{h}+1)

do

fill
(
m
)
// where
j_{0}=-1

end-right
(
c_{(h)}
): apply
y_{i_{h}+1}(v_{l})\circ x_{j_{h}+1}(s_{l})\circ x_{j_{h}}(w_{l})\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{j_{h};l}
to
M

add
j_{h}
to
N
, and add
l
to
K

sub-construct-matrix
(
children
(
c_{(h)}
),
i_{h}+2
,
j_{h}
)
// ordered last to first

start
(
c_{(h)}
)

for

m

in

(j_{g}+2,\dots,n-1)

do

fill
(
m
)

return

M

The analysis of
construct-matrix-rightwards
is similar to the original variant. Let
S^{\prime}
be the positroid cell constructed by this algorithm. Its dimension is again at most
4k
. As in Definition
3.26
and Remark
3.29
for the case of
construct-matrix
, the chord diagram
D
is associated via the left-to-right algorithm with
another algorithmic permutation
,

\sigma^{\prime}\;=\;\tau_{g}^{\prime}\cdot\tau_{g-1}^{\prime}\cdots\tau_{1}^{\prime}\,\cdot\,\rho_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\rho_{2}^{\prime}\cdots\rho_{g}^{\prime}

where
c_{(1)},\dots,c_{(g)}
are the top chords in increasing order, and the factors
\tau_{f}^{\prime}
and
\rho^{\prime}_{f}
are the following products of 2-cycles and 3-cycles corresponding to a chord
c_{(f)}=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
:

•

\tau_{f}^{\prime}
is the same as
\tau_{f}
except for the rightmost transposition, which is
(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j)
instead of
(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ n)
.

•

\rho_{f}^{\prime}
is the same as
\rho_{f}
except for the leftmost 3-cycle, which is
(j\penalty\ \penalty\ j+1\penalty\ \penalty\ n)
instead of
(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j\penalty\ \penalty\ j+1)
.

The following two lemmas are analogous to Proposition
3.21
and
Corollary
3.35

of the original algorithm, and their proofs are omitted.

Lemma 3.41
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram, and let
C\in\mathcal{DM}_{n,k}
be the domino matrix that corresponds to
D
. Then

\textsc{construct-matrix-rightwards}(D)
has the same form of
C
, and it satisfies the domino sign rules.

Lemma 3.42
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram. The decorated permutation of the positroid cell
S^{\prime}
that arises from
construct-matrix-rightwards(
D
)
is the other algorithmic permutation
\sigma^{\prime}
of
D
.

In order to show that the two algorithms parameterize the same positroid cell, we prove that their decorated permutations agree.

Lemma 3.43
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram. Let
S
the positroid cell arising from
construct-matrix
(D)
, and associated with the algorithmic permutation
\sigma
, and let
S^{\prime}
be the positroid cell arising from
construct-matrix-rightwards
(D)
, and associated with the other algorithmic permutation
\sigma^{\prime}
. Then
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
and hence
S=S^{\prime}
.

Proof.

The two algorithmic permutations are

\displaystyle\sigma\;

\displaystyle=\;\tau_{1}\cdot\tau_{2}\cdots\tau_{g}\,\cdot\,\rho_{g}\cdot\rho_{g-1}\cdots\rho_{1}

\displaystyle\sigma^{\prime}\;

\displaystyle=\;\tau_{g}^{\prime}\cdot\tau_{g-1}^{\prime}\cdots\tau_{1}^{\prime}\,\cdot\,\rho_{1}^{\prime}\cdot\rho_{2}^{\prime}\cdots\rho_{g}^{\prime}

We claim first that for every top chord
c_{(f)}

\tau_{f}^{\prime}\cdot\rho_{f}^{\prime}\;=\;\tau_{f}\cdot\rho_{f}

Indeed, if
c_{(f)}=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
then these factors differ by

\displaystyle\tau_{f}\;

\displaystyle=\;\cdots(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ n)

\displaystyle\rho_{f}\;

\displaystyle=\;(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j\penalty\ \penalty\ j+1)\cdots

\displaystyle\tau_{f}^{\prime}\;

\displaystyle=\;\cdots(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j)

\displaystyle\rho_{f}^{\prime}\;

\displaystyle=\;(j\penalty\ \penalty\ j+1\penalty\ \penalty\ n)\cdots

Our claim follows from the identity

(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ n)\penalty\ (i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j\penalty\ \penalty\ j+1)\;=\;(i+1\penalty\ \penalty\ j)\penalty\ (j\penalty\ \penalty\ j+1\penalty\ \penalty\ n)

We apply this claim to the factors of the first top chord
c_{1}=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
in
\sigma^{\prime}
, and obtain

\sigma^{\prime}\;=\;\tau_{g}^{\prime}\cdots\tau_{2}^{\prime}\cdot\tau_{1}\,\cdot\,\rho_{1}\cdot\rho_{2}^{\prime}\cdots\rho_{g}^{\prime}

The factor
\rho_{1}
commutes with all
\rho_{f}^{\prime}
for
f\geq 2
as they do not share any elements other than fixed points. Indeed, the least element in
\rho_{2}
is either
j_{2}
the third marker of
c_{2}
or the second marker of one of its descendants, and these are strictly greater than
j+1
the fourth marker of
c_{1}
. Similarly,
\tau_{1}
commutes with all
\tau_{f}^{\prime}
for
f\geq 2
. Indeed, all the transpositions in
\tau_{f}^{\prime}
are in the range
j
to
n-1
, while the rightmost transposition in
\tau_{1}
swaps its second marker
i+1
with
n
, and other transposition only involve starts of
c_{1}
and its descendants which are smaller than
j
. In conclusion,

\sigma^{\prime}\;=\;\tau_{1}\cdot\tau_{g}^{\prime}\cdots\tau_{2}^{\prime}\,\cdot\,\rho_{2}^{\prime}\cdots\rho_{g}^{\prime}\cdot\rho_{1}

The lemma follows by iterating for all top chords
c_{(2)},\dots,c_{(g)}
until we obtain
\sigma
.
∎

Remark 3.44
.

One could also deduce Lemma
3.42
from
\sigma=\sigma^{\prime}
, based on plabic graphs (Summary
3.11
). To sketch the argument, since both corresponding plabic graphs have
4k+1
faces, which is minimal for a
4k
-dimensional cell, they are reduced and correspond to the same positroid cell
S=S^{\prime}
.

3.6
The Domino Theorem

We conclude Section
3
by stating key results and corollaries that play a role in later sections. As noted in Remark
3.19
, we often denote by
\textsc{construct-matrix}(D)
the set of all points in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
which are obtained from that algorithm, by taking the row-spans of the outputs
\textsc{construct-matrix}(D,\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\}_{l=1}^{k})
where the parameters vary over
(0,\infty)^{4k}
.
We have seen in Section
3.2
that the algorithm generates domino matrices. By the analysis of Section
3.3
it indeed gives a positroid cell, shown in Section
3.4
to have the decorated permutation of
D
as in Definition
2.25
. An equivalent variant of this algorithm has been given in Section
3.5
. We record these constructions of BCFW cells in the following corollary.

Corollary 3.45
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram. The BCFW positroid cell that corresponds to
D
is equal to
\textsc{construct-matrix}(D)
and to
\textsc{construct-matrix-rightwards}(D)
.

Proof.

By Lemma
3.31
and its Corollary
3.35
, Corollary
3.39
, Definition
2.28
, and Lemma
3.43
.
∎

It follows that BCFW cells have domino representations, as formulated in the following theorem. This result is stated above in Theorem
1.6
, and was conjectured by Karp, Williams, Zhang, and Thomas
[
KWZ20
, Conjecture A.7]
.

Theorem 3.46
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram and
S
its BCFW positroid cell. Every point in
S
has a representative matrix in the domino form of
D
that satisfies the domino sign rules, and every such matrix represents a point in
S
. The domino matrix representation of a point is unique up to rescaling each row by a positive number.

Proof.

By Corollary
3.45
, the BCFW cell that corresponds to
D
is generated by Algorithm
3.13
, where the existence of a unique preimage to each
V\in S
with respect to
construct-matrix
is guaranteed by Lemma
3.31
. The output has the domino form of
D
and satisfies the sign rules by Proposition
3.21
. Every assignment to the domino matrix
C
is obtained from some input to
\textsc{construct-matrix}(D)
by Lemma
3.22
, and hence represents a point
V\in S
. As also noted in that lemma, different domino matrices that represent
\textsc{construct-matrix}(D)
for the same input to the algorithm are equivalent up to positively rescaling each set
(\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},\gamma_{l},\delta_{l},\varepsilon_{l})
, which amounts to rescaling each row of the representative matrix of the form
C
given by Proposition
3.21
.
∎

The analysis of the BCFW triangulation in the coming sections uses the recursive nature of its algorithmic constructions. It critically depends on which matrix operations generate a BCFW cell
S
of some chord diagram
D
from a BCFW cell
S^{\prime}
of some subdiagram
D^{\prime}
of
D
. The relation between
D
and
D^{\prime}
comes in three different flavors, stated in Corollaries
3.47
,
3.48
and
3.49
below.

In these corollaries, we always consider a chord diagram
D=(N,\mathcal{C})
and a subdiagram
D^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
with a subset
N^{\prime}\subset N
of the markers and a subset
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}\subset\mathcal{C}
of the chords. In fact,
N^{\prime}
is taken to be a cyclic interval of
N
in these cases, and the chords
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
are exactly those whose four markers are in
N^{\prime}
. The specific assumptions on the excluded chords
\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
differ between the three corollaries. In each case, we relate the respective BCFW positroid cells
S
and
S^{\prime}
.

We always assume a common largest marker
n=\max N=\max N^{\prime}
. The matrix operation
x_{i}=x_{i}(t)
is always carried out with a positive variable
t\in(0,\infty)
, and similarly for
y_{i}
. Writing
y_{i{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}
implies that
i
is contained in the index set when the operation is applied at its current precedent. In all diagrams and subdiagrams, the two markers of a chord’s start or end are assumed to be consecutive numbers, and hence denoted
(i,i{+}1)
rather than
(i,i{\pluscirc}1)
.

Corollary 3.47

(left extension)
.

Let
D^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
be a subdiagram of
D=(N,\mathcal{C})

with an index set
N^{\prime}=\{i\in N:i\geq m\}
for some
m\in N
, such that
\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{C^{\prime}}=\{(i,i+1,j,j+1)\in\mathcal{C}:j\leq m\}
.
Then the BCFW cell of
D
is generated from the BCFW cell of
D^{\prime}
by a sequence of operations from

\left\{{\mathrm{pre}}_{i}:1\leq i<m\right\}\;\cup\;\left\{{\mathrm{inc}}_{i}:1\leq i<m\right\}\;\cup\;\left\{x_{i}:1\leq i\leq m\right\}\;\cup\;\left\{y_{i{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}:1\leq i<m\right\}.

Corollary 3.48

(right extension)
.

Let
D^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
be a subdiagram of
D=(N,\mathcal{C})
with an index set
N^{\prime}=\{i\in N:i\leq m\}\cup\{n\}
for some
m\in N
, such that
\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{C^{\prime}}=\{(i,i+1,j,j+1)\in\mathcal{C}:i\geq m-1\}
.
Then the BCFW cell of
D
is generated from the BCFW cell of
D^{\prime}
by a sequence of operations from

\left\{{\mathrm{pre}}_{i}:m<i<n\right\}\;\cup\;\left\{{\mathrm{inc}}_{i}:m<i<n\right\}\;\cup\;\left\{x_{i}:m<i<n\right\}\;\cup\;\left\{y_{i{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}:m\leq i<n\right\}.

Corollary 3.49

(top extension)
.

Let
D^{\prime}=(N^{\prime},\mathcal{C}^{\prime})
be a subdiagram of
D=(N,\mathcal{C})
with an index set
N^{\prime}=N\setminus\{m\}
where
m=\min N
, such that
\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{C^{\prime}}=\{(m,m+1,n{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2,n{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1)\}
.
Then the BCFW cell of
D
is generated from the BCFW cell of
D^{\prime}
by an upper embedding, whose sequence of operations is

y_{n{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2}\;\circ\;y_{n{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\;\circ\;y_{n}\;\circ\;x_{m}\;\circ\;{\mathrm{inc}}_{m}.

Example 3.50
.

We demonstrate these three corollaries, using subdiagrams of the chord diagram
D
drawn in Example
2.31
.

•

Corollary
3.47
example: Let
D^{\prime}
be the subdiagram with markers
\{6,\dots,18\}
and chords
\{c_{4},\dots,c_{8}\}
. Then, a sequence of operations from
S^{\prime}
to
S
as claimed is given by Algorithm
3.13
:

y_{3{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ y_{2{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ{\mathrm{pre}}_{1}\circ y_{3{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ x_{4}\circ x_{3}\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{3}\circ y_{5{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ{\mathrm{pre}}_{4}\circ y_{5{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ x_{6}\circ x_{5}\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{5}\circ y_{2{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ x_{6}\circ x_{2}\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{2}

•

Corollary
3.48
example: Let
D^{\prime}
be the subdiagram of
D
with markers
\{1,\dots,11,18\}
and chords
\{c_{1},\dots,c_{5}\}
. Then, a sequence of operations from
S^{\prime}
to
S
as claimed is given by Algorithm
3.40
:

y_{12{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ y_{11{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ y_{13}\circ{\mathrm{pre}}_{13}\circ{\mathrm{pre}}_{15}\circ y_{14{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ x_{16}\circ x_{14}\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{14}\circ{}

{}\circ y_{12{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ x_{16}\circ x_{12}\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{12}\circ y_{16{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}\circ x_{17}\circ x_{16}\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{16}\circ{\mathrm{pre}}_{17}

•

Corollary
3.49
example: Let
D^{\prime}=(\{10,\dots,18\},\{c_{6},c_{7},c_{8}\})
and
D^{\prime\prime}=(\{11,\dots,18\},\{c_{7},c_{8}\})
. Then, the upper embedding from
S^{\prime\prime}
to
S^{\prime}
is:

y_{16}\circ y_{17}\circ y_{18}\circ x_{10}\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{10}

Proof of Corollaries
3.47
,
3.48
and
3.49
.

For all three claims, we consider a sequence of matrix operations that constructs the BCFW cell
S
of
D
. This sequence splits into a prefix that constructs the BCFW cell
S^{\prime}
of
D^{\prime}
, and a suffix that only contains operations in the given ranges. Thus, by applying the prefix of the sequence we obtain
S
, and then we generate
S^{\prime}
from
S
as required.

For Corollary
3.47
we use Algorithm
3.13
. This algorithm scans the diagram
D
right to left, such that all starts, ends, and markers in
D^{\prime}
are handled before those not in
D^{\prime}
. Hence, if we stop it at the right moment, then we have ran the algorithm for
D^{\prime}
and have generated
S^{\prime}
. The exact ranges for the remaining operations are straightforward to verify from the details of the algorithm.

Corollary
3.48
is shown similarly using Algorithm
3.40
, which handles top chords from left to right, each one together with its descendants. Hence it first generates
S^{\prime}
, and then
S
by a sequence of operations in the given ranges. For later reference, we mention additional properties of this sequence in case of a single top chord
(i,i+1,j,j+1)
in
\mathcal{C}\setminus\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
. It starts with
{\mathrm{pre}}
operations followed by
x_{j+1}\circ x_{j}\circ y_{i+1}\circ{\mathrm{inc}}_{j}
. Afterwards, there are no
y_{j{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}
or
y_{j}
, and only in the case
i=m-1
there is one
y_{m-1}
.

Finally, Corollary
3.49
is based on the fact that a cell with a single top chord is obtained by an upper embedding. This follows from Remark
3.25
and the last case of Lemma
3.37
. More explicitly, these operations can be shown to produce the cell as in the end of the proof of Lemma
3.31
.
∎

We now pause the discussion of the BCFW positroid cells and their algorithmic and domino representations for the next three section, where we turn to discuss their images under the amplituhedron map. We later return to this topic in Section
7
, which builds upon the techniques of this section in order to give a detailed description of the codimension-one boundaries of the BCFW positroid cells. The reader interested in that topic may safely skip from here to Section
7
.

4
Twistors and Functionaries

The points of the amplituhedron
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,m}(Z)
are
k
-dimensional spaces in the Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m}
. Hence, they may be described by representative
k\times(k+m)
full rank matrices, or by the Plücker coordinates of that Grassmannian. Arkani-Hamed and Trnka
[
AT14
]
introduced yet another set of coordinates for the amplituhedron, the
twistor coordinates
, which take into account the positive matrix
Z
. These coordinates were used by Arkani-Hamed, Thomas and Trnka
[
ATT18
]
to develop a combinatorial and topological picture of the amplituhedron. Parisi, Sherman-Bennett and Williams
[
PSW21
]
used them to characterize the
Z
-images of a large family of positroid cells giving triangulations of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,2}
.

4.1
Definitions

We start with some definitions and basic results stated for general
m
. Later, we focus on the case
m=4
relevant to this paper, even where our treatment extends to other values of
m
. As before, our definitions for
\{1,\dots,n\}
extend to general index sets
N\subset\mathbb{N}
.

Let
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{n\times(k+m)}
be a matrix with positive
(k+m)\times(k+m)
minors where
k+m\leq n
as in the definition of the amplituhedron
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,m}(Z)
. Denote the rows of
Z
by
Z_{1},\dots,Z_{n}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+m}
. For a point
Y\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m}
we also denote by
Y
a representative matrix in
\mathrm{Mat}_{k\times(k+m)}
when its choice factors out. When this matrix is written as
Y=\widetilde{Z}(C)=CZ
, the convention is that both
C
and
Z
have nonnegative determinants in their maximal minors, and in cases that determinants might be negative we explicitly say so. Let
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k}\in\mathbb{R}^{k+m}
denote the rows of
Y
. The determinant of a square matrix
M
is denoted by
\langle M\rangle
.

Definition 4.1
.

Consider a matrix
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times(k+m)}
and a representative matrix
Y\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m}
. For every set
I=\{i_{1},\dots,i_{m}\}
, such that
1\leq i_{1}<i_{2}<\dots<i_{m}\leq n
, the
I
th
twistor coordinate
of
Y
is the determinant of the
(k+m)\times(k+m)
matrix whose rows are
Y_{1},\dots,Y_{k},Z_{i_{1}},\dots,Z_{i_{m}}
. We write it using any of the following notations:

\langle Y\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle\;=\;\langle Y_{1}\dots Y_{k}\penalty\ Z_{i_{1}}\dots Z_{i_{m}}\rangle\;=\;\langle Y\penalty\ Z_{i_{1}}\dots Z_{i_{m}}\rangle\;=\;\langle Y\penalty\ i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{m}\rangle_{Z}

When
Y
or
Z
are fixed and understood from the context, we omit one or both of them and write, for example,
\langle i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{m}\rangle
. In the case
k=0
, the twistor
\langle i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{m}\rangle
is simply the determinant of the corresponding minor of
Z
. We rarely also calculate twistor coordinates for matrices
Y
which are not of full rank, so that they do not represent a point in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m}.
In this case the twistor is just
0.

Remark 4.2
.

Although the
\tbinom{n}{m}
different twistors correspond to unordered subsets
I\in\tbinom{[n]}{m}
, the order of indices in a twistor
\langle Y\penalty\ i_{1}i_{2}\dots i_{m}\rangle_{Z}
is important, by the above definition as a determinant. In the forthcoming, we occasionally use the freedom to write indices not in order, for example
\langle 1375\rangle=-\langle 1357\rangle
. We also write twistors with repeating indices, for example
\langle 2466\rangle=0
.

The twistor coordinates
\langle Y\penalty\ Z_{i}\penalty\ Z_{j}\rangle
are instrumental in the study of triangulations of the
m=2
amplituhedron
[
PSW21
]
. It turns out that in triangulations of the
m=4
amplituhedron, sums of products of twistors serve an important function. We hence give them the following name.

Definition 4.3
.

Let
0\leq m\leq n
. A
functionary
is a homogeneous polynomial in the
\tbinom{n}{m}
twistors
\langle YZ_{I}\rangle
. In more detail, a functionary is a real function of
Y\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m}
and
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{n\times(k+m)}
of the form

F\left(\langle YZ_{I}\rangle:{I\in\tbinom{[n]}{m}}\right)\;:=\;F\left.\left((z_{I})_{I\in\tbinom{[n]}{m}}\right)\right|_{z_{I}=\langle YZ_{I}\rangle}

where
F
is a homogeneous polynomial over
\mathbb{R}
of
degree

d
in the variables
\{z_{I}:I\in\tbinom{[n]}{m}\}
, defined for all applicable
k\in\{0,\dots,n-m\}
.

A functionary is also denoted
F(\langle Y\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle:I\in\scalebox{0.8}{$\binom{N}{m}$})

if all the twistors that appear in
F
are supported on a subset
N\subset[n]
. We denote a polynomial by
F(z_{I})
and a functionary by
F(\langle Y\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle)
when the range of
I
is clear from the context, and sometimes we abuse notation and refer to both of them by
F
.

Example 4.4
.

Here is a functionary of degree 3:

F\;=\;\langle Y1234\rangle\,\langle Y1256\rangle\,\langle Y3489\rangle\;-\;\langle Y1346\rangle\,\langle Y1259\rangle\,\langle Y2348\rangle

Definition 4.5
.

A functionary is
pure
if the multisets of indices occurring in all monomials are the same. This multiset is called the
type
of the functionary. The
multiplicity
of an index
i
is denoted
d_{i}(F)
.

Example 4.6
.

The functionary from Example
4.4
is pure of type
112233445689
. Its multiplicities are
(d_{1}(F),\dots,d_{9}(F))=(2,2,2,2,1,1,0,1,1)
. The functionary
F^{\prime}=\langle 1345\rangle+\tfrac{1}{8}\langle 2345\rangle
is not pure.

Definition 4.7
.

For
Y
and
Z
of
m=4
, we use a special shorthand for the following two-term pure quadratic functionary:

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i\penalty\ i^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;l\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}_{Y,Z}\;\;=\;\;\left\langle Y\penalty\ i\penalty\ j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\penalty\ l\right\rangle_{Z}\,\left\langle Y\penalty\ i^{\prime}\penalty\ h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\penalty\ l\right\rangle_{Z}\;-\;\left\langle Y\penalty\ i^{\prime}\penalty\ j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\penalty\ l\right\rangle_{Z}\,\left\langle Y\penalty\ i\penalty\ h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\penalty\ l\right\rangle_{Z}

where
Y
and
Z
are omitted from this notation whenever possible.

Example 4.8
.

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,1\,2\;{\big|}\;4\,5\;{\big|}\;7\,8\;{\big|}\;9\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}\;=\;\langle 1459\rangle\,\langle 2789\rangle-\langle 2459\rangle\,\langle 1789\rangle

Remark 4.9
.

This functionary and similar ones have appeared in the literature before, for example in
[
Lam14
]
and in
[
AM23
]
. It is most often applied with
i^{\prime}=i+1
,
j^{\prime}=j+1
, and
h^{\prime}=h+1
.

Lemma 4.10
.

The following identity is straightforward from the Plücker relations.

\displaystyle{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i\penalty\ i^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;l\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}\;\;=\;\;-\,{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i\penalty\ i^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;l\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\;\;=\;\;

\displaystyle{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;i\penalty\ i^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;l\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}\;\;=\;\;-\,{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;i\penalty\ i^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;l\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}

\displaystyle\;\;=\;\;

\displaystyle{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;i\penalty\ i^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;l\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}\;\;=\;\;-\,{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j\penalty\ j^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;i\penalty\ i^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;h\penalty\ h^{\prime}\;{\big|}\;l\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}

We conclude this introduction to the amplituhedron’s coordinates by recalling some well-known properties of twistors. First, we expand a twistor coordinate of a point in the amplituhedron in terms of determinants in
Z
and Plücker coordinates of its preimage.

Lemma 4.11

(Lemma 3.6,
[
PSW21
]
)
.

Consider a matrix
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}_{n\times(k+m)}
and two representative matrices
C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
and
Y=CZ\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m}
. For every
I\in\binom{[n]}{m}
, the
I
th twistor coordinate is given by

\langle Y\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle\;\;=\sum_{J\in\binom{[n]}{k}}\langle C^{J}\rangle\,\langle Z_{J}\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle\;\;=\sum_{J\in\binom{[n]\setminus I}{k}}s(J,I)\,\langle C^{J}\rangle\,\langle Z_{I\cup J}\rangle

where

s(J,I)\;=\;(-1)^{\displaystyle\;|\{(i,j):i\in I,\,j\in J,\,i<j\}|}
.

We remark that this expansion is based on the Cauchy-Binet formula. The determinants of
C
are simply the Plücker coordinates
\langle C^{J}\rangle=P_{J}(C)
in the Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
. Note that
I
,
J
and
I\cup J
are unordered sets, and hence the rows of
Z_{I}
,
Z_{J}
and
Z_{I\cup J}
are taken in increasing order. In the typical use case,
C
and
Z
are nonnegative matrices, so the sign of the
J
th term is
s(J,I)
.

In the rest of Section
4
we focus on the case
m=4
for the sake of simplicity. The following definition concerns twistors made of consecutive pairs.

Definition 4.12
.

Let
\widetilde{Z}:\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
as before. The twistor coordinates of the form
\langle i\penalty\ i+1\penalty\ j\penalty\ j+1\rangle
or
\langle 1\penalty\ i\penalty\ i+1\penalty\ n\rangle
are named
boundary twistors
.

We later show that the points in the amplituhedron
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
where a boundary twistor vanishes form the topological boundary of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
, as previously conjectured, see e.g.
[
ATT18
]
. The next lemma presents a well-known fact, that boundary twistors have a constant sign on the amplituhedron.

Lemma 4.13

(e.g.
[
ATT18
]
)
.

Let
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{n\times(k+4)}
.
For every
C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}^{\geq}

1.

\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle\geq 0
for every set of four indices of the form
I=\{i,i+1,j,j+1\}
.

2.

(-1)^{k}\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle\geq 0
for every set of four indices of the form
I=\{1,i,i+1,n\}
.

with equality if and only if the space
C
contains a nonzero vector supported on the four indices
I
.

Example 4.14
.

In
\mathcal{A}_{8,3,4}
the twistors
\langle 1234\rangle
and
\langle 2367\rangle
are nonnegative, and
\langle 1348\rangle
is nonpositive.

Remark 4.15
.

As noted above, by writing
\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle
we slightly abuse notation, as this requires considering a specific nonnegative representative matrix
C\in\mathrm{Mat}^{\geq}_{k\times n}
for
C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}^{\geq}
, and interpreting its image
\widetilde{Z}(C)=CZ\in\mathrm{Mat}_{k,k+m}
as the corresponding representative matrix of
\widetilde{Z}(C)\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m}
. Here, for example, the lemma holds regardless of the choice of
C
, though it is crucial to avoid a nonpositive representative matrix
C\in\mathrm{Mat}^{\leq}_{k\times n}
. We continue this abuse of notation in the rest of this section, where it is also crucial to consider the same representative
C
in all the twistors that occur in a certain functionary or claim.

Proof of Lemma
4.13
.

Both types of inequality follow from Lemma
4.11
, noting that all the terms in the sum have the same sign as stated. There is equality
\langle i\penalty\ i{\pluscirc}1\penalty\ j\penalty\ j{\pluscirc}1\rangle=0
exactly when all these terms vanish, which means that the Plücker coordinates
\langle C^{J}\rangle=0
for every
k
columns
J\subseteq[n]\setminus\{i,i{\pluscirc}1,j,j{\pluscirc}1\}
. Equivalently, these
n-4
columns of
C
are not of full rank
k
. Since
\mathrm{rank}\,C=k
, this condition amounts to the existence of a nonzero linear combination of
C
’s rows supported on
\{i,i{\pluscirc}1,j,j{\pluscirc}1\}
.
∎

For general
I
, it depends on the choice of
Z
which points
C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}^{\geq}
satisfy
\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle=0
. However, Lemma
4.13
shows that for some twistors this only depends on the preimage point
C
regardless of
Z
. We denote these points as follows.

Definition 4.16
.

For
n\geq k\geq 1
, let

\widetilde{S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}}={\widetilde{S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}}}^{Z}_{n,k,4}=\left\{C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}:\,\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ \text{is not of full rank, or }\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{\{i,i{\pluscirc}1,j,j{\pluscirc}1\}}\rangle=0\text{ for some }i\text{ and }j\right\}.

Write
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}={S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}}_{n,k,4}=\widetilde{S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}}\cap\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}.
Equivalently, using Lemma
4.13
,

\displaystyle S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle=\left\{C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}^{\geq}\;:\;\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{\{i,i{\pluscirc}1,j,j{\pluscirc}1\}}\rangle=0\text{ for some }i\text{ and }j\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}^{\geq}\;:\;\text{There exist }i\text{ and }j\text{ such that }\langle C^{J}\rangle=0\penalty\ \text{for every }J\subseteq[n]\setminus\{i,i{\pluscirc}1,j,j{\pluscirc}1\}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}^{\geq}\;:\;C\cap\mathrm{span}\left\{e_{i},e_{i{\pluscirc}1},e_{j},e_{j{\pluscirc}1}\right\}\neq\{0\}\text{ for some }i\text{ and }j\right\}

where
e_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
is the
i
th unit vector, and we consider only
i,j
such that
\{i,i{\pluscirc}1,j,j{\pluscirc}1\}
are four different elements.

Remark 4.17
.

Note that by Lemma
4.11
,

\left\{C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}\;:\;C\cap\mathrm{span}\left\{e_{i},e_{i{\pluscirc}1},e_{j},e_{j{\pluscirc}1}\right\}\neq\{0\}\text{ for some }i\text{ and }j\right\}\subseteq\widetilde{S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}}.

It follows from the definitions that
\widetilde{Z}(C)
is not of full rank if and only if
C
contains a vector which maps to
0
under multiplication by
Z.

It follows from the characterization by Plücker coordinates above, that for every
n
and
k
the set
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
is a union of positroid cells, is closed and is
Z
-independent
, unlike
\widetilde{S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}}
which may depend on
Z
.
Also by the Plücker characterization, for every positroid cell
S\subseteq S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
there is a boundary twistor
\langle i\penalty\ i{\pluscirc}1\penalty\ j\penalty\ j{\pluscirc}1\rangle
that vanishes on
\widetilde{Z}(S)
.

We later show that
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
is the preimage of the boundary of the amplituhedron
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
.

4.2
Promotion of Functionaries

We turn to analyze how twistors, and thereby functionaries, transform under the matrix operations
{\mathrm{pre}}
,
{\mathrm{inc}}
,
x
,
y
, and
\overleftrightarrow{\iota}
, defined in Section
3.1
.
Some proofs in this section are technical, and the reader may benefit from skipping them on the first reading and returning to them after seeing the applications in Section
4.3
.

We start with the embedding
{\mathrm{pre}}_{i}:\mathrm{Gr}_{k,N}\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k,N\cup\{i\}}
, which adds a column of zeros at some new index
i\not\in N
.

Lemma 4.18
.

Let
C\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,N}
and let
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{(N\cup\{i\})\times[k+4]}
where
i\notin N
and
k\geq 0
. For every
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\}\subseteq N
,

\langle\widetilde{Z}_{N}(C)\penalty\ (Z_{N})_{I}\rangle\;=\;\langle\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{i}C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle

Here we use the notation
\widetilde{Z}_{N}:\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,N}\to\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,k+4}
for the map induced from right multiplication
C\mapsto CZ_{N}
, where
Z_{N}\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{N\times[k+4]}
is obtained from
Z
by deleting the
i
th row. Later, we write
\widetilde{Z}(C)
instead of
\widetilde{Z}_{N}(C)
if there is no ambiguity.

Proof.

The lemma follows from
({\mathrm{pre}}_{i}C)Z=CZ_{N}
and
Z_{I}=(Z_{N})_{I}
.
∎

This lemma and similar ones are used to track the signs of functionaries under the matrix operations. We first define an abbreviated notation for a functionary having a fixed sign at a point.

Definition 4.19
.

Let
C\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k,N}
for a finite
N\subset\mathbb{N}
and
k\geq 0
, and let
F(z_{I}:I\in\scalebox{0.8}{$\tbinom{N}{4}$})
be a homogeneous polynomial. If
F\left(\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle\right)
has the same sign for every
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{N\times[k+4]}
then we denote this sign by

\SIGN F\left(\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle\right)\;\in\;\{-1,+1\}

and say that the functionary
F
has a fixed sign at
C
.
Otherwise, if the sign depends on
Z
, then we say that
F
does not have a fixed sign at
C
. Our usage of the notation
\SIGN F
entails that
F
has a fixed sign.
Note that this definition
Z
is not a given matrix, and its dimensions are understood from the context.

Lemma 4.20
.

Let
C\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,N}
and
i\not\in N
, and let
F(z_{I}:I\in\scalebox{0.8}{$\tbinom{N}{4}$})
be a homogeneous polynomial.
If
F(\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle)
has a fixed sign, then

\SIGN F(\langle\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{i}C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle)\;=\;\SIGN F(\langle\widetilde{Z}(C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle)

Proof.

Immediate from Lemma
4.18
.
∎

The next matrix operation is
{\mathrm{inc}}_{i}:\mathrm{Gr}_{k,N}\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k+1,N\cup\{i\}}
, which adds a unit vector at a new coordinate
i\not\in N
. For a representative matrix
C\in\mathrm{Mat}^{\geq}_{[k]\times N}
, without loss of generality, we insert the
i
th unit vector as the last row. The resulting representative matrix is
{\mathrm{inc}}_{i;k+1}\,C\in\mathrm{Mat}^{\geq}_{[k+1]\times(N\cup\{i\})}
where the columns after
i
are negated.

The next lemma analyzes how the twistor coordinates of
\widetilde{Z}(C)
translate to those of
\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{inc}}_{i}\,C)
. This requires a certain “projection” of
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{(N\cup\{i\})\times[k+4+1]}
to another positive matrix
Z_{\neg i}^{\neg j}\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{N\times[k+4]}
.

Lemma 4.21
.

Let
C\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,N}
and
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{(N\cup\{i\})\times[k+5]}
where
i\notin N
and
k\geq 0
, and let
j\in[k+5]
such that
Z_{i}^{j}\neq 0
. For every
I=\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\}\subseteq N
,

\langle{\widetilde{Z}_{\neg i}^{\neg j}}(C)\penalty\ (Z_{\neg i}^{\neg j})_{I}\rangle\;=\;(-1)^{|I\cap[i]|}\;\langle\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{inc}}_{i}\,C)\penalty\ Z_{I}\rangle

where the matrix
Z_{\neg i}^{\neg j}\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{N\times([k+5]\setminus\{j\})}
is defined by

(Z_{\neg i}^{\neg j})_{p}^{q}\;=\;(-1)^{\delta[p<i]+\delta[q<j]}\left(Z_{p}^{q}-\frac{Z_{i}^{q}Z_{p}^{j}}{Z_{i}^{j}}\right)\,(Z_{i}^{j})^{\delta[q=j{\pluscirc}1]}

Proof.

First, we interpret the formula defining
Z_{\neg i}^{\neg j}
. As
Z_{i}^{j}\neq 0
, we subtract multiples of
Z_{i}
from all other rows
Z_{p}
to cancel
Z_{p}^{j}
and make this column vanish for
p\in N
, and then erase the column
j
and the row
i
altogether. The sign in the beginning means that we negate all rows before
i
, and again negate all columns before
j
. Since
p\in N
and
q\in[k+5]\setminus\{j\}
the
i
th row and
j
th column of
Z
are actually deleted. The last factor multiplies the arbitrarily chosen column
j{\pluscirc}1
by the removed entry
Z_{i}^{j}
. As an illustration, if
Z_{i}=(1,0,0,\dots,0)
then we just delete the
i
th row and 1st column, and negate the rows before
i
. This example may be regarded as a generic case, since
\mathrm{Gr}_{k+1,k+5}
can always be rotated by composing a suitable
(k+5)\times(k+5)
matrix on
Z
, to turn
Z_{i}
into a unit vector. As before,
\widetilde{Z}_{\neg i}^{\neg j}
denotes the induced map from
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,N}^{\geq}
to
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
.

Examine the maximal determinants in the resulting matrix
Z_{\neg i}^{\neg j}
. Let
J\in\tbinom{N}{k+4}
and compare the determinants
\langle(Z_{\neg i}^{\neg j})_{J}\rangle
and
\langle Z_{J\cup\{i\}}\rangle
. The factors
(-1)^{\delta[p<i]+\delta[q<j]}
contributes
(-1)^{j+|J\cap[i]|}
, while the column multiplied by
(Z_{i}^{j})^{\delta[q=j{\pluscirc}1]}
contributes
Z_{i}^{j}
. The remaining matrix
(Z_{p}^{q}-Z_{i}^{q}Z_{p}^{j}/Z_{i}^{j})
is exactly the
(i,j)
minor of
Z_{J\cup\{i\}}
after subtraction of
Z_{i}
