In the previous proof, the former relied on Lemma
5.2
, while here it relies on the definition of
S^{\diamondsuit}_{D}
and Remark
4.17
.
We only list the changes.

We aim to reconstruct
C\in S^{\diamondsuit}
from
Y=\widetilde{Z}(C).
In the course of calculating the row
C_{i},
which corresponds to the chord
c_{i}=(j,j+1,l,l+1),
we first need to check that the twistor
\langle j,j+1,l,l+1\rangle\neq 0.

In the current setting, the non-vanishing of
\langle j,j+1,l,l+1\rangle
is a consequence of
S^{\diamondsuit}\cap\widetilde{S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}}=\emptyset.

The only other assumption we need to check is that
v,Z_{j},Z_{j+1},Z_{l},Z_{l+1}
are linearly independent, where
v=Z_{n}
if
c_{i}
is a top chord, and otherwise
v=t_{1}Z_{h}+t_{2}Z_{h+1},
using the domino
C_{i}
inherits from the parent.
In the case of a top chord, there is no change in the proof. In the other case, the argument used to show these five vectors are linearly independent can only fail if either
v=0
or
v
is proportional to
Z_{j},
where the latter option might happen only if
c_{i}
is a sticky child. In both situations, using Remark
4.17
,
\langle j,j+1,l,l+1\rangle=0,
which implies
C\in\widetilde{S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}}
, contradicting our assumptions.

We note that, by construction, all rows that are calculated throughout this process belong to the vector space represented by
C.
By the assumption
C
is
D
-domino matrix in the sense of Definition
2.5
, but may violate the sign rules of Definition
2.10
.

The uniqueness of the preimage follows from Lemma
4.35
as well, as was the case in Proposition
5.1
. The lemma implies that each of the rows we have constructed is unique up to scaling. On the other hand, if two non-equivalent
C_{1},C_{2}\in S^{\diamondsuit}
have the image
Y
, they must have some non-equivalent row, contradicting the aforementioned uniqueness up to scaling. The possible violation of sign rules does not matter for this argument, and since
S^{\diamondsuit}
was defined directly by the domino form without reference to positroid cells, we do not require anything parallel to Theorem
3.46
.

Finally, the inverse map is smooth since the resulting preimage is given as the functionary-valued matrix
C_{D}(Y,Z)
.
∎

We end this section with a corollary of Lemma
5.2
and Theorem
1.3
about the signs of different twistors on a BCFW cell. It is stated with the index set
\{1,\dots,n\}
for convenience.

Lemma 5.7
.

Let
S\in\mathcal{BCFW}_{n,k}
be a BCFW cell.

1.

Let
i,j\in[n]
such that
i+1<j
and
|\{i,i+1,j,j{{\pluscirc}1}\}|=4.
Then the boundary twistor
\langle i,i+1,j,j{{\pluscirc}1}\rangle
has a fixed sign on
\widetilde{Z}(S),
which is
+1
unless
j=n
and
k
is odd.

2.

If
c_{l}=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
is a top chord and
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\}=\{i,i+1,j,j+1,n\}\setminus\{p\}
,
then the twistor

\langle Y\penalty\ Z_{i_{1}}\penalty\ Z_{i_{2}}\penalty\ Z_{i_{3}}\penalty\ Z_{i_{4}}\rangle
has a fixed sign on
\widetilde{Z}(S)
, which is
+1
if
p=n
,
(-1)^{k-l}
if
p=i
,
(-1)^{k-l+1}
if
p=i+1
,
(-1)^{k-l-\mathrm{below}(c_{l})}
if
p=j
, and
(-1)^{k-l-\mathrm{below}(c_{l})+1}
if
p=j+1
.

Proof.

For the first item, by Remark
4.17
and Lemma
4.13
, it is enough to show that
S
is not contained in
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}},
where
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
is defined in Definition
4.16
. But, by Remark
4.17
again, if
S
were contained in
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}},
then some twistor
\langle i,i{\pluscirc}1,j,j{\pluscirc}1\rangle
would have vanished identically on
S.
This would imply that
\widetilde{Z}(S)
is contained in a codimension one subspace of
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4},
but then
\widetilde{Z}
cannot be injective on
S,
in contradiction to Theorem
1.3
.

The second item is an immediate consequence of the proof of Lemma
5.2
, where the signs appear in the calculation of Lemma
5.2
,(
1
) and we use that
\mathrm{behind}(c_{l})=k-l
.
∎

6
Separation

In this section we prove Theorem
1.4
. We find for each pair
(S_{a},S_{b})
of BCFW cells a separating functionary, meaning a functionary that has, for every positive
Z,
fixed and opposite signs on
\widetilde{Z}(S_{a})
and
\widetilde{Z}(S_{b}).

Finding the separating functionary involves analyzing the corresponding chord diagrams,
D_{a}
and
D_{b}.
We first analyze cases where the two cells are separated by a twistor. We show that such a twistor exists if
D_{a}
and
D_{b}
have rightmost top chords with different ends. If the rightmost top chords of
D_{a}
and
D_{b}
have same ends and different starts, then we find a quadratic functionary that separates the cells. Finally, if the two diagrams have the same rightmost top chord then we use induction.

Both in finding a quadratic separator, and in the induction step, we crucially rely on the results of Section
4
and Corollaries
3.47
-
3.49
. We show a separation for simpler subdiagrams, explicitly or by induction, and analyze their evolution under the extension steps of Corollaries
3.47
-
3.49
.

The first proposition shows that certain twistors have certain fixed signs on some BCFW cells. It is used below for separation of cells by twistors.

Proposition 6.1
.

Let
Z
be a positive
N\times(k+4)
matrix, and
i=\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1
, where
\overline{N}=\max N
.
Then

\langle j,j{\pluscirc}1,\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2,\overline{N}\rangle\;\geq\;0

for every point of
\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{i}(\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,N\setminus\{i\}})),
and every
j<\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}3
.

Moreover, this twistor is strictly positive on
\widetilde{Z}(S)
if
S
is a BCFW cell whose corresponding chord diagram does not include a chord ending at
(\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2,\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1).

If
S
is a BCFW cell whose chord diagram contains a chord ending at
(\overline{N}{{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2},\overline{N}{{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1})
then there exists
j<\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}3
for which

\langle j,j{{\pluscirc}1},\overline{N}{{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2},{\overline{N}}\rangle\;<\;0

for every point of
\widetilde{Z}(S),
for all
Z.

Proof.

The first claim is known; see for example the closely related
[
BH19
, Lemma 4.3]
. It follows immediately from the Cauchy-Binet formula in Lemma
4.11
by noting that in this case nonzero terms in the summation must have
i\notin J,
and therefore
s(J,I)=1.

For the ’More-over’ part, note that
S={\mathrm{pre}}_{i}(S^{\prime}),
for
S^{\prime}
a BCFW cell on the marker set
N\setminus\{i\}.
On the image of
S^{\prime}
in the amplituhedron
\langle j,j{\pluscirc}1,\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2,\overline{N}\rangle>0,
by Lemma
5.7
,(
1
) applied to
S^{\prime}
. Note that here
{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}
is calculated with respect to
N
.
Applying
{\mathrm{pre}}_{i}
and using Lemma
4.20
we obtain the result. When the chord diagram contains a chord that ends at
(\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2,\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1)
, there is a unique such chord that is also a top chord. It is supported on
\{j,j{\pluscirc}1,\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2,\overline{N}{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1,\overline{N}\}.
Applying Lemma
5.7
,(
2
) establishes the claim in this case.
∎

The next proposition shows that certain quadratic functionaries have certain fixed signs on certain BCFW cells. It is used below for separating cells by quadratics.

Proposition 6.2
.

Let
Z
be a positive
n\times(k+4)
matrix. Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram with a top chord
c
whose markers are
(i,i+1,n-2,n-1)
. Then, at every point of
\widetilde{Z}(S_{D}),
the functionaries

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i,i+1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}<0

for every
j\in[n-4]\setminus\{i\}.

Remark 6.3
.

In Proposition
6.2
it is equivalent to say that at every point of
\widetilde{Z}(S_{D})
,

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j,j+1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}>0

for every
j\in[n-4]\setminus\{i\}
. The two statements are equivalent by the Plücker’s relations, see Lemma
4.10
. This alternative writing is more natural when
j<i
.

Proof.

We first consider
j>i.

Denote by
D_{c}
the sub diagram of
D
which contains the descendants of
c
and whose index set is
\{i+1,\ldots,n\}.
By Corollaries
3.47
and
3.49
,
S_{D}
can be constructed as follows.

•

Apply the upper embedding
\overleftrightarrow{\;\textsc{upp}\;}_{i}

to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\times S_{c}.

•

Apply to the resulting cell a sequence of operations from:

–

{\mathrm{pre}}_{h},\penalty\ {\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
for
h<i

–

x_{h}(t),\penalty\ t>0,\penalty\ h\leq i

–

y_{h}(t)
,
t>0,

h<i-1
or
h=n

By Lemma
5.2
,(
1
) the twistors
\langle i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\rangle\neq 0
for all
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\}\subset\{i,i+1,n-2,n-1,n\}
on
\widetilde{Z}(S_{D})
. By Lemma
5.7
,(
1
) all boundary twistors are nonzero.
Thus we can use Corollary
4.44
to deduce that after applying the upper embedding,

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i,i+1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}<0

for every
j\in\{i+1,\ldots,n-4\}.

The iterations of
{\mathrm{pre}}_{h}
do not change the negativity, by Lemma
4.20
.
For iterations of
{\mathrm{inc}}_{h},
we have
I\cap[h]=\emptyset.
By Lemma
4.22
again the sign remains.
Finally, for steps of the form
x_{h},y_{h},
our constraint on
h
guarantees that the form or sign of the functionary do not change, by Lemma
4.26
.
This is clear for all such steps except
x_{i},y_{n}
, however, in these cases the observation in the end of Lemma
4.20
holds.
When
y_{n}
is applied the index set must have some index lower than
i
, by Algorithm
3.13
, and therefore
n{\pluscirc}1<i
would not be in the functionary.
For applications of
x_{i}
we use the fact that
{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i,i+1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}
has a representation in which every twistor which contains
i
also contains
i+1
.
Since all indices from
F
are larger than
i
, by the description of upper promotion in Proposition
4.39
, the index
i
may appear in a twistor in
F^{\prime}
only if it comes from a substitution of a twistor in
F
containing
n
or
n-1
, and in these cases the twistor of
F^{\prime}
containing
i
also contains
i+1
.

We turn to the case
j<i.
By Corollary
3.48
,
S_{D}
can also be constructed as follows.
Let
D_{b}
be the sub diagram of
D
made of chords whose support is contained in
[i+1]\cup\{n\}.
Note that
i\leq n-4.

Let
S_{b}
be the corresponding positroid cell, then
S_{D}
can be constructed from
S_{b}
by

•

Apply to
S_{b}

{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1},\penalty\ {\mathrm{inc}}_{n-2},
followed by
y_{i+1},\penalty\ x_{n-2}
and
x_{n-1}.

•

Apply to the resulting cells a sequence of operations from:

–

{\mathrm{pre}}_{h},{\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
where
h\in\{i+2,\ldots,n-3\}

–

x_{h}
where
h\in\{i+2,\ldots,n-1\}

–

y_{h}
where
h\in\{i,\ldots,n-4\}

As noted in the proof of Corollary
3.48
, this sequence contains a single
y_{i}(u)
operation.

By Proposition
6.1
after applying
{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}
all twistors
\langle j,j+1,i+1,n\rangle
for
j<i
are positive. If
D
differs from
D_{b}
by exactly one lowest chord, then only the first step and one additional
y_{i}
operation are performed. The combination of these operations is precisely the lower embedding
\overleftrightarrow{\;\textsc{low}\;}_{n-2}(t_{1},t_{2},s_{1},s_{2}).

By Lemma
5.2
,(
1
) the twistors
\langle i_{1}i_{2}i_{3}i_{4}\rangle\neq 0
for all
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\}\subset\{i,i+1,n-2,n-1,n\}.

Thus, we can employ Corollary
4.45
and deduce that the twistor
\langle j,j+1,i+1,n\rangle
is promoted to the functionary

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j,j+1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle},

which is positive.

In the general case the situation is rather similar, but the treatment requires some modification. Write
c=c_{l}.

First note that after the first step, every point of the positroid cell

S^{\prime}=x_{n-1}(s_{2})x_{n-2}(s_{1})y_{i+1}(t_{1}){\mathrm{inc}}_{n-2}{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}(S_{b})

has a matrix representative
C
whose
l
th has non zero entries only in positions
i+1,n-2,n-1,n.
Therefore, by the Cauchy-Binet formula of Lemma
4.11
the twistor
\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle
is always negative. Indeed, each contribution to it uses the entry
C_{l}^{i+1}\neq 0,
and a minor of the submatrix
C_{[l-1]}^{[i]}{=(C_{a}^{b})_{a\in[l-1],b\in[i]}}
of
C,
and all of these contributions appear with the same minus sign. Moreover, at least one term is non zero, the minor
C_{[l-1]}^{I}
for
I
the set of first domino entries of each chord, just as in Lemma
3.32
. No operation from the series of operations of the second step affects the form of this twistor, by Lemmas
4.20
,
4.22
,
4.26
. Its sign changes by
-1
for every
{\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
operation, since
|I\cap[h]|=|\{i\}|=1.

Second, note that most operations do not affect the twistors
\langle j,j+1,i+1,n\rangle
. These include all
{\mathrm{pre}}_{h}
and
x_{h}

operations, and all
y_{h}
operations, except for the single
y_{i}(u)
operation, by Lemmas
4.20
and
4.26
respectively. Each application of
{\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
preserves the form of the twistor, but changes the sign by
-1,
since
|I\cap[h]|=|\{j,j+1,i+1\}|=3,
by Lemma
4.22
.

Let
k^{\prime}
be the number of applications of
{\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
during the process, which come before the application of
y_{i}(u),
where we include
{\mathrm{inc}}_{n-2}
in the count. Then just before we apply
y_{i}(u),

\mathrm{sgn}(\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle)=\mathrm{sgn}(\langle j,j+1,i+1,n\rangle)=(-1)^{k^{\prime}}.

By Lemma
4.26
, the application of
y_{i}(u)
promotes
\langle j,j+1,i+1,n\rangle
to

\langle\widetilde{Z}(C),Z_{j},Z_{j+1},(Z_{i+1}+uZ_{i}),Z_{n}\rangle=\langle\widetilde{Z}(C),Z_{j},Z_{j+1},Z_{i+1},Z_{n}\rangle+u\langle\widetilde{Z}(C),Z_{j},Z_{j+1},Z_{i},Z_{n},\rangle

(1)

where matrix argument
C
is the matrix obtained after applying
y_{i}(u).
Moreover, this expression has the same sign
(-1)^{k^{\prime}},
for every positive
Z
.

Now,
u
can actually be read from
\widetilde{Z}(C).

Indeed, before applying
y_{i}(u),
the
l
th row of the matrix only had non zero entries at positions
i+1,n-2,n-1,n.
Therefore, after applying
y_{i}(u),\penalty\ C_{l}
has non zero entries at positions
i,i+1,n-2,n-1,n
and we can write

u=\frac{C_{l}^{i}}{C_{l}^{i+1}}.

We also know that
\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle\neq 0.
Thus, we can apply Lemma
4.35
to
\widetilde{Z}(C)
and the linearly independent vectors
Z_{i},Z_{i+1},Z_{n-2},Z_{n-1},Z_{n}
to deduce

u=-\frac{\langle i+1,n-2,n-1,n\rangle}{\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle}.

Multiplying (
1
) by
\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle
and substituting for
u
we obtain that

\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle\langle\widetilde{Z}(C),Z_{j},Z_{j+1},Z_{i+1},Z_{n}\rangle-\langle i+1,n-2,n-1,n\rangle\langle\widetilde{Z}(C),Z_{j},Z_{j+1},Z_{i},Z_{n}\rangle

has sign
(-1)^{k^{\prime}+k^{\prime}}=1.

This expression is precisely the functionary

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}.

By applying Plücker’s relations, Lemma
4.10
, we see that this functionary equals

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j,j+1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle},

which must therefore be positive.
Finally, the additional operations of the second step of the construction of
S_{D}
from
S_{b}
do not change the form of this functionary, by Lemmas
4.20
,
4.22
and
4.26
. Only
{\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
operations may affect the sign, but as above,
{\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
alters the sign of each of the four twistors forming the functionary
{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}
, hence does not affect the sign of the functionary, which remains positive.
As claimed.
∎

Remark 6.4
.

The argument in the second part of the above proof can be applied to more general twistors, and hence to general functionaries, with index set
[i+1]\cup\{n\}
. A twistor of the form
\langle i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\rangle
with
i_{1}\ldots,i_{4}\in[i]\cup\{n\}
keeps its original form. It also keeps its original sign if
n\notin\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\},
and it changes sign to
(-1)^{k^{\prime}},
where
k^{\prime}
is as in the above proof, times the original sign if
n\in\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\}
.
If one of the indices, say
i_{4},
equals
i+1,
then the twistor is promoted to the functionary

\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle\langle i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i+1\rangle-\langle i+1,n-2,n-1,n\rangle\langle i_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i\rangle,

which has the same sign if
n\in\{i_{1},i_{2},i_{3}\},
or
(-1)^{k^{\prime}}
times the same sign otherwise. Thus, the effect of the construction of the second step on a functionary is precisely the lower promotion from Proposition
4.39
.
In all cases the resulting functionary is pure. Moreover, as in Remark
4.38
, a pure functionary
F
with a fixed sign
s
and multiplicities
d_{j}(F)
as in Definition
4.5
, is promoted to another pure functionary
F^{\prime}
with a fixed sign. The multiplicity of each index
i,n-2,n-1,n
increases by
d_{i+1}(F)
. The sign
s
changes by
(-1)^{d_{i+1}(F)+d_{n}(F)}
.

We are now in position to prove the main theorem, Theorem
1.4
.

Proof of Theorem
1.4
.

We show that for every two different BCFW cells of a certain
n
, even with different values of
k
, there is a
pure separating functionary
. A pure separating functionary for a pair of positroid cells is a pure functionary having fixed and opposite signs on the two images of cells (not necessarily in the same amplituhedron), independently of the positive matrices
Z_{a},Z_{b}
used for projection to the appropriate amplituhedra.

We proceed by induction on
n
. The case where
n=4
and
k=0
is trivially true, since there is a single cell. Assuming the claim for all
n^{\prime}<n
, we consider two different BCFW cells,

S_{a},S_{b}\;\in\;\bigcup_{k=0}^{n-4}\mathcal{BCFW}_{n,k}

Let
D_{a}
and
D_{b}
denote the corresponding chord diagrams. We consider several cases.

(A) One Diagram is Trivial

Suppose that
D_{b}
is the diagram with no chords. Then all twistors, which are the determinants of
4\times 4
minors of
Z_{b}
, are positive on the image of
S_{b}
.
Since
D_{a}\neq D_{b}
, the other diagram
D_{a}
has at least one chord, and hence at least one top chord. Let
c=(i,i+1,j,j+1)
be the last top chord. Then by Lemma
5.7
(
2
), the twistor

\langle i,i+1,j,n\rangle
is always negative on the image of
S_{a}
.

(B) Common Unused Markers

If both
D_{a}
and
D_{b}
do not have a chord in the segments next to some marker
h\in\{1,\dots,n-1\}
, then
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
have a common zero column at
h
. It follows that
S_{a}={\mathrm{pre}}_{h}(S_{a^{\prime}})
and
S_{b}={\mathrm{pre}}_{h}(S_{b^{\prime}})
for some BCFW cells
S_{a^{\prime}}\neq S_{b^{\prime}}
with
n-1
columns. By the induction on
n
, there is a separating functionary between
S_{a^{\prime}}
and
S_{b^{\prime}}
. Applying Lemma
4.20
, this functionary is promoted to a separating functionary between
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
.

(C) Different Last Used Marker

Suppose that exactly one of
D_{a}
and
D_{b}
has a top chord of the form
(i,i+1,n-1,n-2)
. Then the other cell must have a zero column at its
n-1
coordinate. In this case, the twistor
\langle i,i+1,n-2,n\rangle
separates them, by Proposition
6.1
.

(D) Different Last Chords

Suppose that
D_{a}
and
D_{b}
have last top chords
(i,i+1,n-2,n-1)
and
(j,j+1,n-2,n-1)
respectively, for
i\neq j
. Then Proposition
6.2
and Remark
6.3
imply that the sign of the functionary
{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i,i+1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}
differs between the images of
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
. Note that the separating functionaries in cases (A)-(D) are indeed pure.

(E) Different Last Subdiagrams

The remaining case is
i=j
, meaning that both
D_{a}
and
D_{b}
have the same last top chord
c=(i,i+1,n-2,n-1)
.
Here induction may produce higher degree functionaries.
Since
D_{a}\neq D_{b}
, they either differ in the chords descendent from
c
, or in those before
c
.
We first consider the former case, and case (F) below will be the latter. The analysis here resembles the first case in the proof of Proposition
6.2
. Let
D_{a^{\prime}}
and
D_{b^{\prime}}
be the respective subdiagrams of
D_{a}
and
D_{b}
, induced on the marker set
\{i+1,\dots,n\}
, and let
S_{a^{\prime}}
and
S_{b^{\prime}}
be the corresponding cells. Note that
D_{a^{\prime}}
and
D_{b^{\prime}}
may consist of a different number of chords, and one of them may even be empty.

By induction on
k
, there is a pure separating functionary
F
for the pair of positroid cells
S_{a^{\prime}}
and
S_{b^{\prime}}
. Here it is crucial that our construction of separating functionaries works for pairs of BCFW cells of the same
n
also when the values of
k
are different.
It follows from Corollary
3.49
and Corollary
3.47
that
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
can be constructed from
S_{a^{\prime}}
and
S_{b^{\prime}}
by the following procedure.

•

Apply the upper embedding
\overleftrightarrow{\;\textsc{upp}\;}_{i}

to both
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\times S_{a^{\prime}}
and
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\times S_{b^{\prime}}
.

•

Apply to the resulting cells two possibly different sequences of operations from:

–

{\mathrm{pre}}_{h},\penalty\ {\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
for
h<i

–

x_{h}(t),\penalty\ t>0,\penalty\ h\leq i

–

y_{h}(t)
,
t>0,

h<i-1
or
h=n

As in the proof of Proposition
6.2
, by Lemma
5.2
,(
1
) all twistors
\langle i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\rangle\neq 0
for
\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\}\subset\{i,i+1,n-2,n-1,n\}
on both
\widetilde{Z}_{a}(S_{a})
and
\widetilde{Z}_{b}(S_{b})
. Thus we can apply Proposition
4.42
to deduce that after the first step, the pure functionary
F
is promoted to a pure functionary
F^{\prime},
and that
F^{\prime}
separates the two cells constructed after in the first step. Importantly, Since all indices from
F
are larger than
i
, by the description of upper promotion in Proposition
4.39
, the index
i
may appear in a twistor in
F^{\prime}
only if it comes from a substitution of
a twistor in
F
containing
n
or
n-1
, and in these cases the twistor of
F^{\prime}
containing
i
also contains
i+1
.

Applications of
{\mathrm{pre}}_{h}
do not change the form or sign of the functionary, by Lemma
4.20
. They also maintain the special property that every twistor which includes the index
i
contains also
i+1.

A similar claim holds for iterations of
{\mathrm{inc}}_{h},
where this time Lemma
4.22
is called, and we use
I\cap[h]=\emptyset.

Using Lemma
4.26
and the conditions on
h,
also steps of
x_{h},\penalty\ y_{h}
do not affect the form of the promoted functionary, and do not change the signs of the functionary, besides possibly
x_{i}
and
y_{n}
. However, in these cases the observation in the end of Lemma
4.20
holds.
For
x_{i}
, the special property that every twistor in
F
which contains
i
also contains
i+1
guarantees that the functionary is again unaffected by promotion by Lemma
4.26
.
For
y_{n}
, by Algorithm
3.13
, whenever it is applied we must have
\min(N)<i
, and so
n{\pluscirc}1=\min(N)
does not appear in any twistor in
F^{\prime}
, and the functionary is unaffected by promotion as well by Lemma
4.26
.
Thus, the separating functionary is promoted in all cases to the same functionary, and its sign is still a witness for separation.

(F) Same Last Subdiagram

Finally, suppose that the subdiagram which descends from
c
is the same for both
D_{a}
and
D_{b}.
The analysis of this case resembles that of the second case of Proposition
6.2
. Let
D_{a^{\prime}}
and
D_{b^{\prime}}
be the respective chord diagrams obtained by erasing
c
and its descendants. Their marker set is
\{1,\dots,i,i+1,n\}
, and
D_{a^{\prime}}\neq D_{b^{\prime}}
since
D_{a}\neq D_{b}
, so at least one of them contains chords. By induction, there is a pure separating functionary
F
between the corresponding images of
S_{a^{\prime}}
and
S_{b^{\prime}}.
This time we apply Corollary
3.48
to construct the cells
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
from
S_{a^{\prime}}
and
S_{b^{\prime}}
respectively.

•

Apply to both cells
{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1},\penalty\ {\mathrm{inc}}_{n-2},
followed by
y_{i+1},\penalty\ x_{n-2}
and
x_{n-1}.

•

Apply to each of the two resulting cells the same sequence of operations from:

–

{\mathrm{pre}}_{h},{\mathrm{inc}}_{h}
where
h\in\{i+2,\ldots,n-3\}

–

x_{h}
where
h\in\{i+2,\ldots,n-1\}

–

y_{h}
where
h\in\{i,\ldots,n-4\}

As noted in the proof of Corollary
3.48
, this sequence contains a single
y_{i}(t)
operation.

In case the second step comprises only of the single
y_{i}(t)
operation, we are in the context of the lower promotion of Proposition
4.43
, and it follows from that proposition that
F
is promoted to a pure functionary
F^{\prime}
which separates the two cells.
In the general case, arguing as in the analogous part in the proof of Proposition
6.2
, and as elaborated in Remark
6.4
, the separating pure functionary
F
is promoted to a pure functionary
F^{\prime}
which separates
S_{a}
from
S_{b},
for all
Z_{a}
,
Z_{b}
.

In conclusion, the cases (A)-(F) cover all possible configurations of two different chord diagrams, and the theorem follows.
∎

Remark 6.5
.

In case (E), the functionary
F^{\prime}
is obtained from
F
by the substitution
for the upper promotion in Proposition
4.39
.
Similarly, in case (F), the functionary
F^{\prime}
is obtained from
F
by the substitution for the lower promotion in Proposition
4.39
.
Suppose that
d_{j}(F)
are the multiplicities of indices in
F
, as in Definition
4.5
, and
s
is its sign on the cell. In case (E), the multiplicities of
\{i,i+1,n-2,n\}
increase by
d_{n-1}(F)
, and those of
\{i,i+1,n-2,n-1\}
increase again by
d_{n}(F)
by Remark
4.38
.
The sign changes by
(-1)^{d_{n-1}(F)}
by Proposition
4.42
.
In case (F), the sign and type change as in Remark
6.4
.

This gives a straightforward recursive algorithm to compute a separating functionary between every two given cells, corresponding to two chord diagrams with
k_{1},k_{2}\in\{0,\dots,n-4\}
. We provide an implementation of this function in
[
ELT22
]
, and its complete output for
n=6,7,8,9,10
.

7
Boundaries of BCFW Cells

In this section we complete the characterization of the BCFW cells via explicit inequalities on its domino entries and some of their
2\times 2
minors. We moreover characterize the codimension one boundaries of those cells.
We show that many boundary components are shared by pairs of cells. We characterize those, as well as those which are not. The techniques used in this section rely on the machinery developed in Section
3
, and this section can be viewed as a continuation of it. The results of this section will be used in Section
8
to prove Theorem
1.5
.

From here until the end of this section, for a chord diagram
D,
denote the corresponding BCFW cell by
S_{D}
and the corresponding decorated permutation by
\pi_{D}
.

7.1
Representation by Inequalities

Recall Definition
2.10
. The following corollary is a simple consequence of Proposition
3.21
.

Corollary 7.1
.

The domino matrix
C
of every point in a BCFW cell
S\in\mathcal{BCFW}_{n,k}
satisfies the following constraints.

1.

The entries of the starting domino of each row are positive.

2.

The entries of the ending domino of
C_{i}
have sign
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}.

3.

The
n
th entry, if
c_{i}
is a top chord, is of sign
(-1)^{k-i}.

4.

If
C_{i}
is not a top chord, then the constant which multiplies the domino entries inherited to
C_{i}
from its parent is of sign
(-1)^{\mathrm{beyond}(c_{i})}.

5.

If
c_{j}
is a descendant of
c_{i}
which ends at the same domino
(l,l+1)
then the determinant of the
2\times 2
minor
C^{l,l+1}_{i,j}
is of sign
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}.

6.

If
c_{j}
starts in the domino
(l,l+1)
in which
c_{i}
ends, then the determinant of the
2\times 2
minor
C^{l,l+1}_{i,j}
is of sign
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}.

Indeed, the first four items are as in Definition
2.10
. The last two are obtained by rewriting the last two items in that definition using the previous items.

Recall Definitions
2.5
,
2.10
.

Notation 7.2

(Domino Variables)
.

For
i=1,\ldots,k
let

\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=(-1)^{\text{behind}(c_{i})}\varepsilon_{i},

if
c_{i}
is a top level chord, and otherwise

\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}(-1)^{\mathrm{beyond}(c_{i})}\varepsilon_{i}.

Let

\hat{\gamma}_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\gamma_{i},\penalty\ \hat{\delta}_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\delta_{i}.

Note that if
c_{i}
is a sticky child of
c_{p}
and
(h,h+1)
is the start of
c_{i}
then
C_{i}^{h}=\alpha_{i}+\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}\beta_{p}.

If
c_{j}
is a same-end descendant of
c_{p},
and the end of both is
(l,l+1)
write

\eta_{pj}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{p})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}\det(C_{pj}^{l,l+1})={(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{p})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}(\gamma_{p}\delta_{j}-\gamma_{j}\delta_{p})}.

If
(c_{l},c_{r})
are head-to-tail chords, whose common markers are
(h,h+1)
put

\theta_{lr}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{l})}\det(C_{lr}^{h,h+1}){=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{l})}(\gamma_{l}\beta_{r}-\delta_{l}\alpha_{r})}

Write
Var^{1}=Var^{1}_{D}
for the set of all elements
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i},\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\hat{\gamma}_{i},\hat{\delta}_{i},\penalty\ i=1,\ldots,k.

Denote by
\widetilde{Var}=\widetilde{Var}_{D}
the set

Var^{1}_{D}\cup\{\eta_{pj}:\penalty\ \text{$c_{j}$ is a same-end descendant of $c_{p}$}\}\cup\{\theta_{lr}:\penalty\ \text{$(c_{l},c_{r})$ are head-to-tail chords}\}.

Finally, let
Var=Var_{D}
be the subset of
\widetilde{Var}
defined by

•

\eta_{pj}
appears only if
p
is the parent of
j.
In this case
\hat{\delta}_{p}
and
\hat{\gamma}_{j}
do not appear.

•

\theta_{lr}
appears only if
c_{l}
and
c_{r}
are siblings. In this case
\hat{\gamma}_{l}
and
\beta_{r}
do not appear.

•

All other variables of types
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i},\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\hat{\gamma}_{i},\hat{\delta}_{i}
belong to
Var.

For simplicity, when
D
is fixed, or clear from context, we denote
Var=Var_{D},

Var^{1}=Var^{1}_{D},

\widetilde{Var}=\widetilde{Var}_{D}
. The domino variables are always associated with some domino form of a chord diagram
D
, but we leave this implicit in the notation as well, and let the reader deduce
D
by context, again for simplicity of notation.

By Corollary
7.1
all the variables defined above are positive.
In this section and the next one it is more convenient to work with these domino variables and not the usual ones.

Proposition 7.3
.

Let
C
be a domino matrix in the sense of Definition
2.5
for a chord diagram
D.
For every
I\in\binom{[n]}{k},\penalty\ \det(C^{I})
is a polynomial with nonnegative coefficients in the variables of
\widetilde{Var}_{D},
which is nonzero precisely if the Plücker coordinate
P_{I}
is nonvanishing on
S_{D}.

Thus, if in addition
C
satisfies the inequalities of Definition
2.10
, or equivalently the inequalities of Corollary
7.1
, then
C
represents a point in
S_{D}.

Proof.

Let
I=\{i_{1},\ldots,i_{k}\}\in\binom{[n]}{k}
be an arbitrary set. Expand
\det(C^{I})
to obtain

\sum_{f:[k]\to I,\penalty\ \text{a bijection}}(-1)^{s(f)}\prod_{i=1}^{k}C_{i}^{f(i)},

where

s(f)=|\{(i,j)|i<j\text{ and }f(i)>f(j)\}.

Further expand in terms of the domino variables
\alpha_{i},\beta_{i},\gamma_{i},\delta_{i},\varepsilon_{i}
to obtain

\det(C^{I})=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}f:[k]\to I,\penalty\ \text{a bijection},\\
\mathcal{M}_{f}\neq\emptyset\end{subarray}}(-1)^{s(f)}\sum_{M\in\mathcal{M}_{f}}M,

(2)

where
\mathcal{M}_{f}
is the collection of monomials in the domino variables which appear in the expansion of
\prod_{i=1}^{k}C_{i}^{f(i)}.
The set
\mathcal{M}_{f}
may include more than one element only if for some
i

c_{i}
is a sticky child of
c_{p},
and
f(i)
is its first marker. In this case
C_{i}^{f(i)}=\alpha_{i}+\varepsilon_{i}\beta_{p}.

We first simplify the sub (
2
) by noting a collection of cancelling pairs.
To this end define an involution
\varphi
on
\sqcup_{f}\mathcal{M}_{f}
as follows.
Let
M
be a monomial, and let
i<j\in[k]
be the smallest indices, if such indices can be found, which satisfy

1.

c_{i},c_{j}
are either siblings or a parent and a child,

2.

f(i),f(j)
are the first two markers of
c_{h},
the parent of
c_{j}
(which may be
c_{i}
).

3.

M
is divisible by
\alpha_{h}\beta_{h}.

In case such
i,j
can be found, define
f^{\prime}:[k]\to I
to be the bijection which agrees with
f
everywhere on
[k],
except at
i,j
in which
f^{\prime}(i)=f(j),\penalty\ f^{\prime}(j)=f(i).

We note that
M\in\mathcal{M}_{f^{\prime}}
and define
\varphi(f,M)=(f^{\prime},M).

If there is no such pair, define
\varphi(f,M)=(f,M).

\varphi
is clearly an involution.
The meaning of the third condition above is that the contribution of
c_{i},c_{j}
to
M
comes from the terms inherited by the parent of
c_{j}.

Observe that
s(f)=s(f^{\prime})+1.
Thus if we change the order of summation in (
2
), to first sum over
\varphi
-equivalence classes, and then summing the terms
(-1)^{s(f)}M
in each equivalence class, we see that the sum equals the sum over fixed point of
\varphi,
that is over monomials which do not contain
\alpha_{h}\beta_{h}
for any
h.
Denote by
\mathcal{M}
this collection of monomials which are fixed by
\varphi.

We can thus write

\det(C^{I})=\sum_{(f,M)\in\mathcal{M}}(-1)^{s(f)}M.

(3)

The next step is to collect the summands of (
3
) in a clever way, to obtain a positive expression, at the expense of the need to introduce the variables
\eta_{ij},\theta_{ij}
.
Let
\mathcal{D}
be the collection of pairs
d=(h,h+1)
which appear in
D,
either as starts or ends of chords. Given a bijection
f:[k]\to I,
we say that two chords
c_{i},c_{j}

f
-share

d,
if both contain
d
as their start or end, and
\{f(i),f(j)\}=\{h,h+1\}.

Define an involution
\varphi_{d}
on
\mathcal{M},
for each
d=(h,h+1)\in\mathcal{D}
as follows. Consider
(f,M)\in\mathcal{M}.
If there are two chords
c_{i},c_{j}
which
f
-share
d,
we define
f^{\prime}
by
f^{\prime}(i)=f(j),\penalty\ f^{\prime}(j)=f(i)
and
f(l)=f^{\prime}(l)
otherwise.
Note that for any
d
there is at most one such pair
\{i,j\},
and that since
f
is a bijection, any
c_{i},
for
i\in[k],
can
f-
share at most one
d.

There is a natural bijection between
\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{M}_{f}
and
\mathcal{M}\cap\mathcal{M}_{f^{\prime}}.
In case
d
is the common end of
c_{i}
and
c_{j},
and, without loss of generality
f(i)<f(j)
then in terms of minors this bijection is just the multiplication by
\frac{\gamma_{j}\delta_{i}}{\gamma_{i}\delta_{j}}.
In case
d
is the end of
c_{i}
and the start of
c_{j},
then it is the multiplication by
\frac{\alpha_{j}\delta_{i}}{\beta_{j}\gamma_{i}},
if
f(i)<f(j)
and
\frac{\beta_{j}\gamma_{i}}{\alpha_{j}\delta_{i}}
otherwise.
We define
\varphi_{d}(f,M)
as
(f^{\prime},M^{\prime}),
where
M^{\prime}
is the outcome of the aforementioned multiplication.
Note that again
s(f)=s(f^{\prime})+1.

Let
\Phi
be the equivalence relation generated by
\{\phi_{d}\}_{d\in\mathcal{D}}.

We show that the sum of all monomials in a
\Phi
-equivalence class is a polynomial with nonnegative coefficients in the elements of
\widetilde{Var}_{D},
and thus prove the claim.

For
i=1,\ldots,k
define
s_{i}(f)
by

•

\mathrm{below}(c_{i}),
the number of descendants of
c_{i},
if
f(i)
belongs to the end of
c_{i}.

•

\mathrm{beyond}(c_{i}),
the number of descendants of the parent of
c_{i}
which start before
c_{i},
if
c_{i}
is not a top level chord, and
f(i)
belongs to the start of
c_{i}
’s parent.

•

\mathrm{behind}(c_{i})=k-i,
if
c_{i}
is a top chord, and
f(i)=n.

•

Otherwise
s_{i}(f)=0.

For every
d=(h,h+1)\in\mathcal{D},
define
\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f)
to be
0,
except in the following two cases in which it is
1:

•

There are
c_{i},c_{j}
which
f
-share
d,

c_{j}
is a same-end descendant of
c_{i},
and
f(j)>f(i).

•

There are
(c_{i},c_{j})
are head-to-tail chords which
f
-share
d
and
f(i)>f(j).

If
\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f)=1
we say that
f

has a contradiction at
d
. In this case, if
\varphi_{d}(f,M)=(f^{\prime},M^{\prime})
we say that
f^{\prime}
is obtained from
f
by
resolving the contradiction at
d
.

For a bijection
f
which is not cancelled by the first involution
\varphi
we have

s(f)=\sum_{i\in[k]}s_{i}(f)+\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f).

(4)

Indeed, suppose first that
f
has no contradictions, then a pair
(i,j)
where
i<j
but
f(i)>f(j),
which contributes to
s(f)
may appear if only if

•

f(i)
belongs to the end of
c_{i}
and
c_{j}
is a descendant of
c_{i}.

•

f(j)
belongs to the start of the parent of
c_{j},
and
c_{i}
is a descendant of that parent, which comes before
c_{j}
.

•

c_{i}
is a top chord, and
f(i)=n,

c_{j}
starts after
c_{i}.

These cases are enumerated exactly once in the definition of
\{s_{i}(f)\}_{i\in[k]}.

Suppose that there are contradictions in
f
. Note that if
f^{\prime}
is obtained from
f
by resolving the contradiction at
d,
then

s(f)-s(f^{\prime})=1,

\displaystyle\left(\sum_{i\in[k]}s_{i}(f)+\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f)\right)

\displaystyle-\left(\sum_{i\in[k]}s_{i}(f^{\prime})+\sum_{d\in\mathcal{D}}\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f^{\prime})\right)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f)-\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f^{\prime})=1.

Thus, (
4
) holds for
f
if and only if it holds for
f^{\prime}.

Now, let
\tilde{f}
be obtained from
f
by resolving all contradictions. From the previous case we know that (
4
) holds for
\tilde{f}.
Since
f,\tilde{f}
differ by a sequence of resolutions of contradictions, and the correctness of (
4
) is preserved under such steps, we deduce that (
4
) holds also for
f.

By Corollary
7.1
and the definition of
s_{i}
we have

(-1)^{s_{i}(f)}C_{i}^{f(i)}>0.

(5)

Every
\Phi
-equivalence class
R
has a unique element
(f_{R},M_{R})
where the bijection
f_{R}
has the maximal (with respect to inclusion) set of contradictions
D_{R}\subseteq\mathcal{D}.
Morevoer, any
(f,M)\in R
is of the form
\varphi_{d_{1}}\circ\cdots\circ\varphi_{d_{l}}(f_{R},M_{R})
for some
d_{1},\ldots,d_{l},
and any such expression is in
R.
Thus, the size of
R
is
2^{|D_{R}|}.

Set
I(D_{R})=\bigcup_{d\in D_{R}}\{l_{d},l_{d}+1\}
where
(l_{d},l_{d}+1)
are the markers of
d.
By the definition of contradictions, for each
d\in D_{R}
there are two indices
i_{d},j_{d}\in[k]
so that the chords
c_{i_{d}},c_{j_{d}}
are responsible for this contradiction, and
\{f(i_{d}),f(j_{d})\}=\{l_{d},l_{d}+1\}.
Since no
i\in[k]
can participate in two contradictions (any
c_{i}
can
f-
share at most one
d,
as explained above). Thus, the markers pairs
\{l_{d},l_{d}+1\}
for
d\in D_{R}
are all disjoint.

We will now see that summing the monomials corresponding to elements of
R
results in a positive expression, which involves the
\eta_{ij},\theta_{ij}
variables. Each of these new variables corresponds to one contradiction in
f_{R}.

Using (
4
), (
5
)
we can write
\sum_{(f,M)\in R}(-1)^{s(f)}M
as

\sum_{(f,M)\in R}\prod_{d\in D_{R}}(-1)^{\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f)}\prod_{i\in[k]}(-1)^{s_{i}(f)}C_{i}^{f(i)}=\sum_{(f,M)\in R}\prod_{d\in D_{R}}(-1)^{\mathrm{Contradiction}_{d}(f)}\prod_{i\in[k]}|C_{i}^{f(i)}|.

This sum can be reorganized to

\prod_{j\notin I(D_{R})}|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j)}^{j}|\prod_{d\in D_{R}}(|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d}+1)}^{j_{d}}||C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d})}^{j_{d}+1}|-|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d})}^{j_{d}}||C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d}+1)}^{j_{d}+1}|),

(6)

where we have used that
f_{R}
has a contradiction for each
d\in D_{R},
and the above description of
R.

All terms in the first product are clearly positive.
But also all terms in the second product are. Indeed, if
d
is the end of both
c_{i},c_{j}
where
i<j
then by the assumption
\det(C_{i,j}^{j_{d},j_{d}+1})
has sign
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}.
When we scale the
i
th and
j
th rows of the determinant by
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}
and
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})}
respectively,
we obtain

|C_{i}^{j_{d}}||C_{j}^{j_{d}+1}|-|C_{i}^{j_{d}+1}||C_{j}^{j_{d}}|<0.

(7)

Now, in this case the contradiction is obtained for
f,
such as
f_{R},
which maps
i\to j_{d},\penalty\ j\to j_{d}+1.
Thus, the left hand side of
(
7
) equals

-\left(|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d}+1)}^{j_{d}}||C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d})}^{j_{d}+1}|-|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d})}^{j_{d}}||C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d}+1)}^{j_{d}+1}|\right).

which implies that
|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d}+1)}^{j_{d}}||C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d})}^{j_{d}+1}|-|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d})}^{j_{d}}||C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d}+1)}^{j_{d}+1}|>0.

Similarly, if

d
is the ending domino of both
c_{i}
and the starting domino of
c_{j}
then
i<j,
and a contradiction will be obtained from bijections
f,
like
f_{R},
with
f(i)=j_{d}+1,\penalty\ f(j)=j_{d}.

By the assumption the determinant of
C_{i,j}^{j_{d},j_{d}+1}
has sign
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}.
We scale the
i
th row of the determinant by
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}
. The result is thus positive. Thus, the corresponding determinant which appears in (
6
) is

\displaystyle|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d}+1)}^{j_{d}}||C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d})}^{j_{d}+1}|-|C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d})}^{j_{d}}||C_{f_{R}^{-1}(j_{d}+1)}^{j_{d}+1}|\;

\displaystyle=\;|C_{i}^{j_{d}}||C_{j}^{j_{d}+1}|-|C_{i}^{j_{d}+1}||C_{j}^{j_{d}}|

\displaystyle=\;(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\det(C_{i,j}^{j_{d},j_{d}+1})>0.

All terms in the product (
6
) belong to
\widetilde{Var}_{D}.
As claimed.

The fact that the nonzero Plücker coordinates correspond precisely to the nonzero polynomials is straightforward.
Since we only used in the proof the domino form of
C
and the signs of Corollary
7.1
, every matrix in that form that satisfies these inequalities has the same nonvanishing determinants, and they are all positive.
∎

Remark 7.4
.

Showing that every point that satisfies the constraints of Corollary
7.1
lies in the positroid cell
S
also follows from the computations in Lemmas
3.22
or
3.31
, by recovering the variables of
construct-matrix
.
The argument we presented above has the advantage of giving the positive decomposition of the Plücker coordinates, a decomposition we will soon use.

The upshot of the next lemma is to show that the vanishing of an element in
\widetilde{Var}\setminus Var
implies the vanishing of at least two elements of
Var.

Lemma 7.5
.

For any chord diagram
D
,
every element in
\widetilde{Var}_{D}\setminus Var_{D}
can be represented as a
linear combination of Laurent monomials in the variables of
\widetilde{Var}_{D},

such that all coefficients are nonnegative, and at least one of the monomials appearing in this sum with a positive coefficient is of the form
\frac{P}{Q},
where
P
is a product of elements of
Var_{D},
and
Q
is a product of elements of
Var^{1}_{D}.

Proof.

Throughout the proof, since
D
is fixed, we omit
D
from the notations. We first prove the claim for elements of
Var^{1}\setminus Var.
There are two cases to consider. The first is that
c_{i}
has a same-end child
c_{j}.
In this case

\hat{\gamma}_{j},\hat{\delta}_{i}\in Var^{1}\setminus Var
and
\eta_{ij}\in Var.

From the definitions and Corollary
7.1
we have:

\eta_{ij}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}\det\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{i}&\delta_{i}\\
\gamma_{j}&\delta_{j}\end{pmatrix}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}(\gamma_{i}\delta_{j}-\gamma_{j}\delta_{i}),

and

\gamma_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\hat{\gamma}_{i},\penalty\ \delta_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\hat{\delta}_{i},\penalty\ \gamma_{j}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})}\hat{\gamma}_{j},\penalty\ \delta_{j}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})}\hat{\delta}_{j}.

Thus,
\hat{\delta}_{i}\hat{\gamma}_{j}=\eta_{ij}+\hat{\gamma}_{i}\hat{\delta}_{j},
and hence

\hat{\delta}_{i}=\frac{\eta_{ij}+\hat{\gamma}_{i}\hat{\delta}_{j}}{\hat{\gamma}_{j}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \hat{\gamma}_{j}=\frac{\eta_{ij}+\hat{\gamma}_{i}\hat{\delta}_{j}}{\hat{\delta}_{i}},
as claimed.

The second case is when
c_{i},c_{j}
are head-to-tail siblings. Then
\hat{\gamma}_{i},\beta_{j}\in Var^{1}\setminus Var
and

\theta_{ij}\in Var.

Again by Corollary
7.1
,

\theta_{ij}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\det\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{i}&\delta_{i}\\
\alpha_{j}&\beta_{j}\end{pmatrix}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}(\gamma_{i}\beta_{j}-\alpha_{j}\delta_{i}),

and

\gamma_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\hat{\gamma}_{i},\penalty\ \delta_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\hat{\delta}_{i},\penalty\ \alpha_{j}=\alpha_{j},\penalty\ \beta_{j}=\beta_{j}.

Thus,
\hat{\gamma}_{i}\beta_{j}=\theta_{ij}+\hat{\delta}_{i}\alpha_{j},
and therefore

\hat{\gamma}_{i}=\frac{\theta_{ij}+\hat{\delta}_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\beta_{j}},\penalty\ \penalty\ \beta_{j}=\frac{\theta_{ij}+\hat{\delta}_{i}\alpha_{j}}{\hat{\gamma}_{i}},
showing the claim for this case.

We now find an expression for
\eta_{il}
when
c_{l}
is a same-end descendant which is not the child of
c_{i}.

In this case
c_{i}
must have a same end child, denote it by
c_{j}.
Then
\eta_{ij}\in Var.
Write

C_{h,h+1}^{i,j,l}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{i}&\delta_{i}\\
\gamma_{j}&\delta_{j}\\
\gamma_{l}&\delta_{l}\end{pmatrix}.

By Corollary
7.1
,

\gamma_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\hat{\gamma}_{i},\penalty\ \delta_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\hat{\delta}_{i},\penalty\ \gamma_{j}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})}\hat{\gamma}_{j},\penalty\ \delta_{j}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})}\hat{\delta}_{j},

\gamma_{l}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{l})}\hat{\gamma}_{l},\penalty\ \delta_{l}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{l})}\hat{\delta}_{l},

and

\eta_{ij}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}(\gamma_{i}\delta_{j}-\gamma_{j}\delta_{i}),\penalty\ \eta_{il}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})-\mathrm{below}(c_{l})+1}(\gamma_{i}\delta_{l}-\gamma_{l}\delta_{i}),

\eta_{jl}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}(\gamma_{j}\delta_{l}-\gamma_{l}\delta_{j}).

We see that
\delta_{l}(\gamma_{i}\delta_{j}-\gamma_{j}\delta_{i}),\penalty\ \delta_{i}(\gamma_{j}\delta_{l}-\gamma_{l}\delta_{j}),\penalty\ \delta_{j}(\gamma_{i}\delta_{l}-\gamma_{l}\delta_{i})
all have the same sign

(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})+\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+\mathrm{below}(c_{l})+1},

and moreover the sum of the first two equals the third.
Thus,
\frac{\hat{\delta}_{l}}{\hat{\delta}_{j}}\eta_{ij}+\frac{\hat{\delta}_{i}}{\hat{\delta}_{j}}\eta_{jl}=\eta_{il}.
If
\hat{\delta}_{l}\in Var
then this case follows. Otherwise we use the first case to write
\hat{\delta}_{l}=\frac{\eta_{ll^{\prime}}+\hat{\gamma}_{l}\hat{\delta}_{l^{\prime}}}{\hat{\gamma}_{l^{\prime}}},
and substitute this in the expression for
\eta_{il}.

We now express
\theta_{jl}
where
c_{j}
has a same-end ancestor
c_{i}
and there is a third chord
c_{l}
which starts at
(h,h+1)
where
c_{i},c_{j}
end. We may take
c_{i}
to be a sibling of
c_{l},
and then
\theta_{il}\in Var.

Write

C_{h,h+1}^{i,j,l}=\begin{pmatrix}\gamma_{i}&\delta_{i}\\
\gamma_{j}&\delta_{j}\\
\alpha_{l}&\beta_{l}\end{pmatrix}.

By Corollary
7.1
,

\gamma_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\hat{\gamma}_{i},\penalty\ \delta_{i}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}\hat{\delta}_{i},\penalty\ \gamma_{j}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})}\hat{\gamma}_{j},\penalty\ \delta_{j}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})}\hat{\delta}_{j}

\alpha_{l}=\alpha_{l},\penalty\ \beta_{l}=\beta_{l},

and

\eta_{ij}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})-\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1}(\gamma_{i}\delta_{j}-\gamma_{j}\delta_{i}),\theta_{il}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})}(\gamma_{i}\beta_{l}-\delta_{i}\alpha_{l}),\penalty\ \theta_{jl}=(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{j})}(\gamma_{j}\beta_{l}-\delta_{j}\alpha_{l}).

We see that
\beta_{l}(\gamma_{i}\delta_{j}-\gamma_{j}\delta_{i}),\penalty\ -\delta_{j}(\gamma_{i}\beta_{l}-\delta_{i}\alpha_{l}),\penalty\ -\delta_{i}(\gamma_{j}\beta_{l}-\delta_{j}\alpha_{l})
have sign
(-1)^{\mathrm{below}(c_{i})+\mathrm{below}(c_{j})+1},
and moreover the sum of the first two equals the third.
Thus,
\frac{\beta_{l}}{\hat{\delta}_{i}}\eta_{ij}+\frac{\hat{\delta}_{j}}{\hat{\delta}_{i}}\theta_{il}=\theta_{jl},
and we finish as in the previous case.
∎

7.2
Codimension One Boundaries

Summary 7.6
.

Recall Summary
3.11
.
The stratification of the nonnegative Grassmannian was much studied in the literature
[
Pos06
, Section 18]
,
[
PSW09
, Section 6]
. Postnikov showed that the closure of a positroid cell is a disjoint union of positroid cells. Moreover, a positroid cell
S
is contained in the closure of a another positroid cell
S^{\prime}
if and only if all the Plücker coordinates that vanish on
S^{\prime}
also vanish on
S
. It was later shown that this stratification yields a CW complex structure on the closure of each positroid cell, and in particular on
\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}

[
PSW09
, Section 6]
.

Plabic graphs also carry information about gluing the boundary stratification. If a positroid cell
S^{\prime}
is a boundary stratum of a positroid cell
S,
then one can find a reduced plabic graph
G
for
S,
together with a perfect orientation, such that after omitting some of its edges, or equivalently put their weights to
0,
one obtains a reduced plabic graph
G^{\prime}
with a perfect orientation for
S^{\prime},
and the map
(0,\infty)^{d}\to S
extends smoothly to a map
[0,\infty)^{d}\to\overline{S},
where
\overline{S}
is the topological closure of
S
, and
S^{\prime}
is the homeomorphic image, and in fact the diffeomorphic image, of the subset of
[0,\infty)^{d}
obtained by setting certain coordinates to
0
(see
[
Pos06
, Section 18]
).

In the following discussion, we refer to a positroid cell as a
stratum
or
boundary stratum
of another positroid cell, if the former is a cell in the CW decomposition of the closure of the latter. Recall that Corollary
7.1
and Notation
7.2
allow translating the inequalities of Definition
2.10
to the positivity of the elements of
\widetilde{Var}_{D}.

Definition 7.7
.

Let
D
be a chord diagram, and
\star\in Var_{D}.
A matrix
C
is a
(D,\star)
-
extended domino matrix
if it is in the form of Definition
2.5
, satisfies the all inequalities of Definition
2.10
, with the exception that
\star=0.
The matrix
C
is said to be a
D
-extended domino matrix if it is in the form of Definition
2.5
, only that the inequalities of Definition
2.10
are relaxed to be weak inequalities.

For a BCFW cell
S_{D}
associated to a chord diagram
D
, we denote by
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
the subset of the nonnegative Grassmannian whose elements have a representative in the
(D,\star)
-extended domino form. We also write
\widetilde{\partial_{\star}S_{D}}\supseteq\partial_{\star}S_{D}
for the set of vector spaces which have a representative in the
D
extended domino form with
\star=0
.

Note that
\widetilde{\partial_{\star}S_{D}}\subseteq\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}^{\geq}
by Proposition
7.3
.
Recall that
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}\subseteq\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}^{\geq}
is defined as the collection of vector spaces which intersect nontrivially
\mathrm{span}(\mathrm{e}_{i},\mathrm{e}_{i{{\pluscirc}1}},\mathrm{e}_{j},\mathrm{e}_{j{{\pluscirc}1}})
for some
i,j.

We start by describing generation algorithms for these spaces, in the spirit of Algorithm
3.13
, in all cases but the case of
\partial_{\beta_{i}}S_{D},
when
c_{i}
is a sticky child. A generation algorithm for this case can also be written, but will not be needed in what follows.

Recall the algorithm
construct-matrix
in Section
3.2
. We now describe the changes needed in order to generate the boundary positroid cells
\partial_{\star},\penalty\ \star\in Var
above. Let
c_{l}=(i,i+1,h,h+1)
be a chord in
D.

•

\mathbf{\partial}_{\hat{\varepsilon}_{l}}:

Act as in
construct-matrix
, but do not perform
y_{(i+1){{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}}(s_{l})
in
end
(c_{l})
.

•

\mathbf{\partial}_{\alpha_{l}}:

Act as in
construct-matrix
, but do not perform
y_{i}(u_{l})
in
start
(c_{\ast l})
.

•

\mathbf{\partial}_{\beta_{l}}
(if
c_{l}
is not a sticky child and
\beta_{l}\in Var
),
\penalty\ \mathbf{\partial}_{\theta_{ml}}
(assuming
\theta_{ml}\in Var
):
Act as in
construct-matrix
, but instead of
end
(c_{l})
apply
{\mathrm{inc}}_{i},\penalty\ y_{i{{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}}(s_{l}),\penalty\ x_{i}(v_{l}),\penalty\ x_{h}(w_{l}),
and add
i
to
N.

In
start
(c_{\ast l})
skip
y_{i+1}(u_{l}).

•

\mathbf{\partial}_{\hat{\gamma}_{l}}
(assuming
\hat{\gamma}_{l}\in Var
):
Since
\hat{\gamma}_{l}\in Var,
then the parent of
c_{l}
ends after
c_{l},
and no sibling of
c_{l}
starts at
(h,h+1).

{\mathrm{pre}}_{h}
is skipped in
fill
(h),
and is performed in
end
(c_{l})
instead of doing
x_{h}(w_{l}).

•

\mathbf{\partial}_{\hat{\delta}_{l}}
(if
\hat{\delta}_{l}\in Var
),
\mathbf{\partial}_{\eta_{lm}}
(assuming
\eta_{lm}\in Var
):
Act as in the algorithm, omitting
x_{h}(w_{l})
in
end
(c_{l}).

Observe that omitting an
x_{h}(t)
or
y_{h}(t)
operation is equivalent to performing the corresponding operation with
t=0.
The reason the cases
\star=\beta_{l},\theta_{ml}
require a more significant change in the algorithm, is that in
construct-matrix
, the first entry that is constructed for a chord
c_{l}
is the one which corresponds to
\beta_{l},
and the algorithm substitutes there
1.
In the cases
\star=\beta_{l},\theta_{ml},
we want instead to substitute there
0,
and for this we use the entry corresponding to
\alpha_{l}
as the first entry of the chord, thus skipping the
\beta_{l}
entry for that stage in the algorithm. In particular, when
\beta_{l}\in Var,
the entry corresponding to
\beta_{l}
will remain
0
until the end of the algorithm.
Denote by
\textsc{construct-matrix}^{\star}\left(D,\,\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\}_{l=1}^{k}\right),
for
\star\in Var,
the output of the algorithm, applied with the variables
\{s_{*},u_{*},v_{*},w_{*}\}.
Note that one variable is absent, according to the above items, but in order not to make the notation heavier we do not write it explicitly.

Lemma 7.8
.

\textsc{construct-matrix}^{\star}\left(D,\,\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\}_{l=1}^{k}\right),
for a chord diagram
D
and
\star\in Var_{D},
generates positroid cells whose elements have representatives in the
(D,\star)
-extended domino form.

Proof.

This lemma is analogous to Proposition
3.21
. The proofs are obtained by minor adaptations to the proof of that proposition. The exception variable
\star=0
follows from omitting the corresponding
x_{i}
or
y_{i}
operation from the algorithm, and positive values for the other variables can be recovered in the same manner.

In the cases
\star=\hat{\varepsilon}_{l},\alpha_{l},\beta_{l},\hat{\gamma}_{l},\hat{\delta}_{l}
this is essentially the only change. We note that for
\star=\beta_{l},
the assumption
\beta_{l}\in Var
is used to guarantee that not only when the row of
c_{l}
is generated, its
i+1
entry will be zero, but also that this will not change by later
x_{i}
operations, as explained in the paragraph preceding this lemma. A similar situation holds for
\hat{\gamma}_{l}
and
\hat{\delta}_{l}
, whose appearance in
Var
guarantees they do not gain positive terms from operations by other chords.

The proof for
\eta_{lm}
and
\theta_{ml}
is also similar to the proof of Proposition
3.21
. This time, after
end
(c_{l})
in the case of
\eta_{lm},
the
h+1
entry of that row is
0.
After
end
(c_{m}),
the minor
\{l,m\}\times\{h,h+1\}
will have proportional columns. The subsequent steps will not change that. Similarly, for
\theta_{ml}
, after
start
(c_{\ast l})
the entry
i+1
of the row of
c_{l}
is
0.
After
end
(c_{\ast m})
, the minor
\{m,l\}\times\{i,i+1\}
will have proportional columns. The subsequent steps will not change that.

By repeating the argument of Proposition
3.21
it is easily seen that not only every
\star^{\prime}\in Var\setminus\{\star\}
is nonzero, it also has the expected sign of Corollary
7.1
.
∎

The generation algorithm can be translated into an algorithmic decorated permutation, which we denote by
\pi_{D,\star}
according to the recipe of Definition
3.23
(recall that
\pi_{D}
is the permutation associated with
D
). When the decorated permutation of a chord diagram
D^{\prime}
is denoted differently than
\pi_{D^{\prime}}
, for example
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(\pi_{D})
which will appear later, we shall denote
\pi_{D^{\prime},\star}
with a
\star
subscript, for example
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(\pi_{D})_{\star}
. Recall Definition
3.26
.
It will be convenient in what follows to use slightly different notations for the same permutation, and to write it in terms of the chords
c_{i},
enumerated according to their starting point, rather than
c^{\prime}_{i}
which are enumerated according to their endpoints. We will write
c_{i}=(a_{i},a_{i}+1,b_{i},b_{i}+1),
and then the permutation
\pi_{D}
reads

(p_{1}\penalty\ q_{1})\penalty\ (p_{2}\penalty\ q_{2})\cdots(p_{2k}\penalty\ q_{2k})\prod_{\begin{subarray}{c}c_{i}\penalty\ \text{is ordered according}\\
\text{to increasing order of ends}\end{subarray}}((a_{i}+1)\penalty\ b_{i}\penalty\ (b_{i}+1))

(8)

where,
p_{i},q_{i}
are as in Definition
3.26
and since we work with non commutative variables our convention is that the multiplication is from left, i.e.
\prod_{i=1}^{3}x_{i}=x_{3}x_{2}x_{1}.
In case of same-end chords, the parent is considered to end after the child.
We also recall the notation
c_{j\ast}
to denote the last sticky descendant in the sticky chain from
c_{j},
and
a_{j\ast}
is its first marker. We denote by
a^{\ast}_{j}
either
n,
if
c_{j}
is a top chord, or
a_{h}+1,
where
c_{h}
is the parent of
a_{h},
otherwise.

Observation 7.9
.

In cases
\star\in Var_{D}
for which the generation algorithm was defined, the algorithmic permutation
\pi_{D,\star}
is obtained as follows.

•

\pi_{D,\hat{\varepsilon}_{j}}
is obtained from (
8
) by omitting the transposition
((a_{j}+1)\penalty\ a^{\ast}_{j}).

•

\pi_{D,\alpha_{j}}
is obtained from (
8
) by omitting the transposition
(a_{j}\penalty\ (a_{j\ast}+1)),
where
c_{j\ast}
is the last sticky descendant in the sticky chain from
c_{j}.

•

\pi_{D,\hat{\gamma}_{j}}
is obtained from (
8
) by replacing the cycle
((a_{j}+1)\penalty\ b_{j}\penalty\ (b_{j}+1))
by
((a_{j}+1)\penalty\ (b_{j}+1)).

•

\pi_{D,\hat{\delta}_{j}},\penalty\ \pi_{D,\eta_{j,j^{\prime}}}
are obtained from (
8
) by replacing the cycle
((a_{j}+1)\penalty\ b_{j}\penalty\ (b_{j}+1))
by
((a_{j}+1)\penalty\ b_{j}).

•

\pi_{D,\beta_{j}},\penalty\ \pi_{D,\theta_{j^{\prime},j}}
are obtained from (
8
) by replacing
((a_{j}+1)\penalty\ b_{j}\penalty\ (b_{j}+1))
by
(a_{j}\penalty\ b_{j}\penalty\ (b_{j}+1)),
replacing
((a_{j}+1)\penalty\ a^{\ast}_{j})
by
(a_{j}\penalty\ a^{\ast}_{j}),
and removing the transposition
(a_{j}\penalty\ ({a}_{j\ast}+1)).

Lemma 7.10
.

Let
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
be a chord diagram. Let
\star\in Var_{D}
be an element which is not of the form
\beta_{i}
for a sticky child,
\alpha_{i}
for a chord which has a sticky child, or
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}.
Denote by
S
the positroid cell that arises from
\textsc{construct-matrix}^{\star}\left(D,\,\{s_{l},u_{l},v_{l},w_{l}\}_{l=1}^{k}\right)
. Every point in
S
is obtained from a unique choice of the
4k-1
positive variables. Hence,
S
is
4k-1
-dimensional.

Moreover, in these cases the positroid cell generated by the algorithm coincides with
\partial_{\star}S_{D},
and
\pi_{D,\star}
is the associated permutation. In addition
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
is a codimension one boundary of
S_{D}.

The restrictions we make in this statement are not always necessary, but they suffice for our needs, and simplify the proof.

Proof.

The proof follows the lines of the proof of Lemma
3.31
. The first statement and the ’Hence’ part are proven by constructing an inverse map, again in an inductive process. That is, for a given point in
S,
which must be an output of
construct-matrix
⋆
, we determine the real parameters of the
x_{l}
and
y_{l}
operations used to construct it.
Finding these values for
k=1
is straightforward.

More than one top chord:

Suppose we have shown the claim for less than
k
chords.
Assume first that
D
has at least two top chords.
For
\star\in\{\alpha_{l},\penalty\ \beta_{l},\penalty\ \hat{\gamma}_{l},\penalty\ \hat{\delta}_{l},\penalty\ \eta_{lm},\penalty\ \theta_{ml}\},

write
c_{l}=(i,i+1,h,h+1)
and denote by
c_{p}=(q,q+1,j,j+1)
the top chord from which
c_{l}
descends. Let
D_{b}
be the subdiagram made of chords which end no later than
(j,j+1).

Let
D_{c}
be the subdiagram made of the other chords, with index set
[n]\setminus[j-1].

Suppose that
D_{b}
has
k^{\prime}
chords. We consider first the case
k^{\prime}<k.

We can write

C=\begin{pmatrix}C^{\prime}\\
C^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix},

where
C^{\prime}
consists of the first
k^{\prime}
rows and
C^{\prime\prime}
of the last
k-k^{\prime}
rows. Let
V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime}
be their row spans, respectively.
The recursive structure of the algorithm

\textsc{construct-matrix}^{\star}\left(D,\,\{s_{r},u_{r},v_{r},w_{r}\}_{r=1}^{k}\right),
allows to write, as in the proof of Lemma
3.31
,
C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}
explicitly:

C^{\prime}={\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\cdots{\mathrm{pre}}_{j+2}\;\textsc{construct-matrix}^{\star}\left(D,\,\{s_{r},u_{r},v_{r},w_{r}\}_{r\in[k^{\prime}]}\right),

and

C^{\prime\prime}\;=\;{\mathrm{pre}}_{j-1}\cdots{\mathrm{pre}}_{1}\;x_{j}(w)\;\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D_{c},\,\{s_{r},u_{r},v_{r},w_{r}\}_{r\in[k]\setminus[k^{\prime}]}\right),

where
w
is the sum of
w_{r}
variables that appear in
x_{j}
operations for
C^{\prime}.

We claim that
V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime}
are uniquely determined from
V.
Indeed, by the same argument of Lemma
3.31
, we see that if this is not the case then either
V^{\prime}
or
V^{\prime\prime}
contains a vector in
\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{e}_{j},\mathrm{e}_{j+1},\mathrm{e}_{n}\}.

V^{\prime\prime}
is the row span of a standard domino matrix. Therefore, by the same argument of Lemma
3.32
,

V^{\prime\prime}\cap\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{e}_{j},\mathrm{e}_{j+1},\mathrm{e}_{n}\}=0.

We want to show that also
C^{\prime}
does not contain a vector in the span of
\{\mathrm{e}_{j},\mathrm{e}_{j+1},\mathrm{e}_{n}\}.
If
\star\in\{\alpha_{p},\beta_{p},\hat{\gamma}_{p},\hat{\delta}_{p}\},
which means
p=l,
then there is
i^{\prime}\in\{i,i+1\}
such that
(C^{\prime}_{p})^{i^{\prime}}\neq 0.
As in the proof of Lemma
3.32
we can find
I\in\binom{[j-1]}{k^{\prime}}
which intersects
\{i,i+1\}
in
i^{\prime}
for which
\det((C^{\prime})^{I})>0,
which implies, again as in that proof, that there is no linear combination of the rows of
C^{\prime}
which lies in
\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{e}_{j},\mathrm{e}_{j+1},\mathrm{e}_{n}\}.

If
\star\notin\{\alpha_{p},\beta_{p},\hat{\gamma}_{p},\hat{\delta}_{p}\},
and
\star\neq\alpha_{l}
, then
\det((C^{\prime})^{I})>0,
where
I
is be the set of first markers of each chord. This is seen by performing row operations which cancel the dominoes inherited to chords from their parent. These operations do not change the starting dominoes and transform
(C^{\prime})^{I}
to an upper triangular with nonzero diagonal.
If
\star=\alpha_{l},
then we take
I
to be the set of first markers of each chord, except for
l
and the maximal sticky chain which descends from it. For these chords we pick the second marker, and again the same reasoning shows that the determinant is nonzero. Thus, in this case we also see that
V^{\prime}
does not contain a vector in
\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{e}_{j},\mathrm{e}_{j+1},\mathrm{e}_{n}\}.

By induction we can recover from
V^{\prime}
the parameters used to construct
C^{\prime}.
We then apply
x_{j}(-w)
to
V^{\prime\prime}
and use induction again to recover the remaining parameters for
C^{\prime\prime}
.

The next case is
k^{\prime}=k.
Since we assume that there is more than one top chord,
c_{p}
must be the last top chord.
This time let
D_{c}
be the subdiagram of
c_{p}
and its descendants, and
D_{b}
the subdiagram of chords which precede
c_{p}
, with marker set
[q+1]\cup\{n\}.

Redefine
k^{\prime}
to be the number of rows in
D_{b},
and set

C=\begin{pmatrix}C^{\prime}\\
C^{\prime\prime}\end{pmatrix},

where
C^{\prime}
consists of the first
k^{\prime}
rows, and
C^{\prime\prime}
the last
k-k^{\prime}
rows.
V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime}
are the row spans.

We use the recursive structure of the algorithm again to write

C^{\prime}={\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\cdots{\mathrm{pre}}_{q+2}\;\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D,\,\{s_{r},u_{r},v_{r},w_{r}\}_{r\in[k^{\prime}]}\right)

and

C^{\prime\prime}\;=\;{\mathrm{pre}}_{q-1}\cdots{\mathrm{pre}}_{1}\;x_{q}(w)\;\textsc{construct-matrix}^{\star^{\prime}}\left(D_{c},\,\{s_{r},u_{r},v_{r},w_{r}\}_{r\in[k]\setminus[k^{\prime}]}\right),

where
w
is the sum of
w_{r}
variables that appear in
x_{q}
operations for
C^{\prime},
and
\star^{\prime}=\star

unless
c_{l}=c_{p}
is a top chord and
\star=\theta_{ml}.
In that exceptional case
\star^{\prime}=\beta_{l},
since after the algorithm finishes to construct the row of
c_{l}
its entry labeled
i+1=q+1
is
0.
It becomes non zero only by applications of
x_{q}
in later stages, whose total effect on this row is the effect of
x_{q}(w)
.

Again we show that
V^{\prime},V^{\prime\prime}
can be determined from
V.

We need to show that neither
V^{\prime}
nor
V^{\prime\prime}
contain a non zero vector in
\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{e}_{q},\mathrm{e}_{q+1},\mathrm{e}_{n}\}.
This time
V^{\prime}
is the row span of a standard domino matrix, hence for
V^{\prime}
this is done as in Lemma
3.31
. For
V^{\prime\prime},
we split as above to the two cases depending on whether
\star
belongs to
\{\alpha_{p},\beta_{p},\hat{\gamma}_{p},\hat{\delta}_{p}\}
or not, and the same conclusion holds.
As in the previous case we use induction to retrieve the parameters of the algorithm for
C^{\prime},
and then for
C^{\prime\prime}
after applying
x_{q}(-w)
to
V^{\prime\prime}.

A single top chord:

The final case to consider is that
D
has a single top chord
c_{p}=c_{1}
, where we may assume, perhaps after removing zero columns, a long chord
(1,2,n-2,n-1).

If
\star\notin\{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\hat{\gamma}_{1},\hat{\delta}_{1}\},
then as in the analogous part in the proof of Lemma
3.31

we can write

C=\begin{pmatrix}C_{1}\\
C^{\prime}\end{pmatrix}

where

C^{\prime}={\mathrm{pre}}_{n}\mathrm{rescale}_{1}(u_{1})\;x_{1}(1)\;\mathrm{rotate}_{k-1}\;\textsc{construct-matrix}^{\star}\left(D_{b},\,\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k]\setminus[1]}\right),

if
\star\neq\eta_{1m}

and

C^{\prime}={\mathrm{pre}}_{n}\mathrm{rescale}_{1}(u_{1})\;x_{1}(1)\;\mathrm{rotate}_{k-1}\;\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D_{b},\,\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k]\setminus[1]}\right),

otherwise.
The reason for this exception is that in case
\eta_{1m}=0
the missing parameter is
w_{1}
from
end
(c_{1}).

As above, the next step is to show that there is no non zero vector in the intersection between the linear span of rows
2,\ldots,k
and
V^{\prime}=\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{e}_{1},\mathrm{e}_{2},\mathrm{e}_{n-2},\mathrm{e}_{n-1}\}.

In order to show that this intersection is zero, first recall that by assumption
\star
is not of the form
\beta_{l}
, for any
c_{l}
which is a sticky child. In this case, we can find
I\in\binom{\{3,4,\ldots,n-3,n\}}{k}
such that
\det(C^{I})\neq 0,
showing that no vector in
\mathrm{span}\{\mathrm{e}_{1},\mathrm{e}_{2},\mathrm{e}_{n-2},\mathrm{e}_{n-1}\}
is spanned by
C
’s rows. We construct
I
as follows. First, we add
n
to
I.
Then for every chord in the sticky chain from
c_{1}
we take its second marker. Then, if
\star\neq\alpha_{l}
for
l\in\{2,\ldots,k\}
we add to
I
the first marker of any other chord. If
\star=\alpha_{l},
for some
c_{l},
then also for this chord, which by assumption does not have a sticky child we take the second marker, and for other chords the first. The first row of
C^{I}
has a single nonzero entry at
n.
Removing this row and the column
n
leaves us with a matrix, that like in the proof of Lemma
3.32
is upper triangular with nonzero diagonal after some row operations.

Thus, the intersection is
0,
hence
C_{1},\penalty\ V^{\prime}
can be determined from
V.
As in the proof of Lemma
3.31
we calculate
s_{1},u_{1},v_{1}
from
C_{1},
then undo
{\mathrm{pre}}_{n},\penalty\ \mathrm{rescale}_{1}(u_{1}),\penalty\ x_{1}(1),\penalty\ \mathrm{rotate}_{k-1},
use induction to recover the parameters
\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k]\setminus[1]}
and then recover
w_{1}.

We are left with the case
\star\in\{\alpha_{1},\beta_{1},\hat{\gamma}_{1},\hat{\delta}_{1}\}.
The proof of Lemma
3.32
extends to this case, and we can find a set
I\subseteq\binom{\{3,4,\ldots,n-3,n\}}{k}
with
\det(C^{I})\neq 0.
This shows as above that indeed
C_{1},
and
V^{\prime}=\mathrm{span}\{2,\ldots,k\}
are uniquely determined. Again we can recover the parameters for
c_{1},
except, possibly,
w_{1}.

If
\star\in\{\hat{\gamma}_{1},\hat{\delta}_{1}\}
then we act just as above.
If
\star=\beta_{1},
we erase the first row and
n
th column, cyclically shift the first column to be the last, possibly changing the sign of this column to preserve positivity, and re-index it as
n.

If
\star=\alpha_{1},
then by assumption
c_{1}
does not have a child which sticks to it. We erase the first row and
n
th column. Note that the first column is now a zero column. We then move the second column to be the
n
th column, possibly changing the sign of this column to preserve positivity.
In all these cases, the resulting vector space is precisely the row span of

\textsc{construct-matrix}\left(D_{b},\,\{s_{h},u_{h},v_{h},w_{h}\}_{h\in[k]\setminus[1]}\right),

we use induction to calculate its parameters and then calculate
w_{1}
if
\star\neq\hat{\delta}_{1}.

The ’More-over’ part:

Arguing exactly as in Section
3.3
, based on Summary
3.11
, we deduce from the dimension calculation above that
\pi_{D,\star}
is the associated permutation.

By definition
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
contains the (non empty) positroid cell
S
generated by the algorithm.
By Proposition
7.3
, the set of nonvanishing Plücker coordinates for each point in
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
are the same: For every
D
-extended domino matrix
C,
every Plücker coordinate
\langle C^{I}\rangle
can be written as a polynomial
Q_{I}
with nonnegative coefficients in the set
\widetilde{Var}_{D}.
Every element of
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
has a
(D,\star)
-matrix representation
C
, and a Plücker coordinate
\langle C^{I}\rangle
is non zero precisely if the polynomial
Q_{I}
does not become identically zero under the substitution
\star=0.
But since the points of
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
all have the same collection of non vanishing Plücker coordinates, and since
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
contains
S,
this collection must coincide with the corresponding collection for the positroid cell
S.
Hence the two spaces must coincide, as
S
is the subset of all points in the non negative Grassmannian with that collection of non vanishing Plücker coordinates. Moreover, by Proposition
7.3
again, the collection of non vanishing Plücker coordinates for
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
is contained in the corresponding collection for
S
. Therefore
S=\partial_{\star}S_{D}
must be a
4k-1
dimensional boundary stratum of
S_{D}.

∎

The main result of this section is the next proposition.

Proposition 7.11
.

Each boundary stratum of a BCFW cell
S_{a}
is either contained in
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
or is a boundary stratum of another BCFW cell
S_{b}.

Before we get to the proof, we define shift operations on chords in a chord diagram. These operations are crucial to the pairing of boundaries.

Definition 7.12
.

Let
c_{i}=(h,h+1,l,l+1)
be a chord in a chord diagram
D.

Left shifting the start of
c_{i}
(unobstructed):

If
h>1,
no chord starts at
(h-1,h)
or ends at
(h,h+1)
we define
\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
as the diagram obtained from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord with a new
i
th chord
(h-1,h,l,l+1).

Right shifting the start of
c_{i}
(unobstructed):

If
c_{i}
is not short, and no chord starts at
(h+1,h+2)
we define
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
as the diagram obtained from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord with a new
i
th chord
(h+1,h+2,l,l+1).

If
c_{i}
is short,
l<n-2
and no other chord starts or ends at
(l,l+1)=(h+2,h+3)
we define
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
as the diagram obtained from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord with a new
i
th chord
(h+1,h+2,h+3,h+4).

Right shifting the start of
c_{i}
(obstructed):

Suppose
c_{i}
is short and
l<n-2.

•

If no other chord ends at
(l,l+1)=(h+2,h+3)
but
c_{i}
has a sibling
(h+2,h+3,j,j+1)
then
we define
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
as the diagram obtained from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord with a new
i
th chord
(h+1,h+2,j,j+1).

•

If no other chord starts at
(l,l+1)=(h+2,h+3)
but some ancestors of
c_{i}
end there,
we define
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
as the diagram obtained from
D
by removing the
i
th chord, pushing the ends of all of its same-end ancestors one marker backwards, that is to end at
(h+1,h+2),
and adding a new short
i
chord
(h+1,h+2,h+3,h+4).

•

If there is a chord
(h+2,h+3,j,j+1)
and some ancestors of
c_{i}
end at
(h+2,h+3)
,
we define
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
as the diagram obtained from
D
by removing the
i
th, pushing the ends of all of its same-end ancestors one marker backwards, that is to end at
(h+1,h+2),
and adding a new
i
th chord
(h+1,h+2,j,j+1).

Left shifting the end of
c_{i}
(unobstructed):

If
c_{i}
is not short and it has no same-end descendant

\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
is defined from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord by
(h,h+1,l-1,l).

If
c_{i}
is short,
h>1
and no chord starts at
(h-1,h)
or ends at
(h,h+1),

\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
is defined from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord by
(h-1,h,h+1,h+2).

Left shifting the end of
c_{i}
(obstructed):

•

Suppose
c_{i}
is not short, and it has a same-end child which starts at
(j,j+1),
for
j>h+1.

In this case
\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
is obtained from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord with a new
i
th chord
(h,h+1,j,j+1).

•

Suppose
c_{i}
is not short, and it has a same-end child which starts at
(h+1,h+2),
but no chord ends at
(h,h+1)
or starts at
(h-1,h)
and
h>1.

In this case
\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
is obtained from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord with a new
i
th short chord
(h-1,h,h+1,h+2).

•

Suppose
c_{i}
is not short, it has a same end child which starts at
(h+1,h+2),
and there are chords ending at
(h,h+1).

In this case
\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
is obtained from
D
by removing the
i
th chord, pushing the endpoint of each chord whose end is
(h,h+1)
to end at
(h+1,h+2)
and add a new
i
th short chord
(h-1,h,h+1,h+2).

•

If
c_{i}
is short and some chord ends at
(h,h+1)
then
\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
is obtained from
D
by removing the
i
th chord, pushing the endpoint of each chord whose end is
(h,h+1)
to end at
(h+1,h+2)
and add a new
i
th short chord
(h-1,h,h+1,h+2).

Right shifting the end of
c_{i}
(unobstructed):

If
l<n-2,
the parent of
c_{i}
does not end at
(l,l+1)
and no sibling of
c_{i}
starts at
(l,l+1)

we define
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
as the diagram obtained from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord with a new
i
th chord
(h,h+1,l+1,l+2).

Right shifting the end of
c_{i}
(obstructed):

If the parent of
c_{i}
does not end at
(l,l+1)
but a sibling of
c_{i}
starts at
(l,l+1)
and ends at
(j,j+1),

we define
\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
as the diagram obtained from
D
by replacing the
i
th chord with a new
i
th chord
(h,h+1,j,j+1).

Note that the shift remains undefined for some combinations of direction, end and chord diagram. This is intentional for reasons that will become apparent after Lemma
7.16
. The undefined situations match the cases 2-6 in that lemma:

\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
is undefined in case
\alpha_{i}
,

\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{start}}_{i}(D)
is undefined in case
\beta_{i}
,

\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
is undefined in cases
\hat{\gamma}_{i}
and
\eta_{ij}
, and

\mathrm{lshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)
is undefined in case
\hat{\delta}_{i}
.

In all the cases where shifts are defined we call the resulting chord diagram the
shifted diagram
. By abuse of notation, we shall denote the induced shift operations on corresponding positroid cells and decorated permutations in the same way, i.e. for the right shift of the end:

\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(S_{D}):=S_{\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)},\;\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(\pi_{D}):=\pi_{\mathrm{rshift}^{\mathrm{end}}_{i}(D)}.
