∎

Remark 7.18
.

In
[
AM23
]
an interesting analysis of boundaries and their cancellation was performed for another related physical model. In that model some other class of chord diagrams were used for indexing cells. Their techniques are very different from ours, but we believe that both techniques can be used to address either problem.

8
Surjectivity

In this section we prove the surjectivity, hence showing that the images of the BCFW cells triangulate the amplituhedron.
We first prove the following lemma and two propositions. The lemma allows extending the amplituhedron map’s domain to a small neighborhood of the nonnegative Grassmannian, while the propositions concern the amplituhedron map on
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
and away from
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
.

Lemma 8.1
.

The amplituhedron map can be extended, via the same formula to a smooth submersion

\widetilde{Z}:B\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m},\penalty\ \penalty\ V\mapsto\widetilde{Z}(V)=V\cdot Z,

where
B\subset\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
is an open neighborhood of
\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}.

Proof.

The map
M\mapsto MZ
is a smooth submersion from
\mathrm{Mat}_{k\times n}
to
\mathrm{Mat}_{k\times(k+m)}
, as the restriction to rows shows.
If
M
has nonnegative
k\times k
minors then
\mathrm{rank}\,MZ=k
(see, for example,
[
Lam14
, Section 15]
). By continuity this holds in a small neighborhood of
M
in
\mathrm{Mat}_{k\times n}
as well. After modding out the left
\mathrm{GL}_{k}(\mathbb{R})
action,
M\mapsto MZ
descends to the amplituhedron map
\widetilde{Z}:B\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m},
where
B\subset\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
is some open neighborhood of
\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n},

which is a smooth submersion.
∎

Proposition 8.2
.

For every
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{n\times(k+4)}
, the set
\widetilde{Z}(S_{\partial\mathcal{A}})
is contained in the topological boundary of the amplituhedron
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
.

Proof.

By definition
\widetilde{Z}(S_{\partial\mathcal{A}})\subseteq\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z).
We need to show that every open neighborhood in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4},
of any point
p\in\widetilde{Z}(S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}),
intersects
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}\setminus\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z).

Assume that
\langle i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}\rangle
vanishes at
p.

Since
\langle i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}\rangle
has a constant sign
s
on
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)\setminus\widetilde{Z}(S_{\partial\mathcal{A}})
and vanishes on
\widetilde{Z}(S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}),
by Lemma
4.13
, it is enough to find points in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
arbitrarily close to
p
for which
\langle i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}\rangle
is of sign
-s,
since such points cannot belong to the amplituhedron.

Let
C\in S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
be a matrix representation for a preimage of
p.
For
R=(R_{i}^{j})_{i\in[k]}^{j\in[n]}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{k\times n},
write
C(R)=C+R.
The function
G(R)=\langle\widetilde{Z}(C(R))Z_{i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}}\rangle
is a multilinear function of the variables
R_{i}^{j},\penalty\ i\in[k],\penalty\ j\in[n].
Order the pairs
(a,b)_{a\in[k],b\in[n]}
arbitrarily. Let
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
be the minimal element in that order such that

G(R)|_{R_{a}^{b}=0\penalty\ \forall(a,b)>(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}\nequiv 0,

where
{}^{\prime}>^{\prime}
is the aforementioned order on pairs.
In other words,
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
is defined by the property that if we restrict
G(R)
to the domain where
R_{a}^{b}=0,
for all
(a,b)>(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
, then it is not identically
0,
but if we restrict it to the domain where
R_{a}^{b}=0,
for all
(a,b)\geq(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
then it is.
Such
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
exists since the function
G(R)
vanishes when all
R_{i}^{j}=0,
but it is not identically zero, since any element of
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
can be be represented by
C(R)
for some
R,
and
\langle i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}\rangle
is not identically zero on the whole Grassmannian.
We can write

G(R)|_{R_{a}^{b}=0\penalty\ \forall(a,b)>(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}=H(R)+R_{a^{\prime}}^{b^{\prime}}F(R),

(10)

where
F(R),H(R)
are multilinear functions in
(R_{a}^{b})
for
(a,b)<(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}).

By the choice of
(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})
it follows that
H\equiv 0
while
F\nequiv 0.
We will find a sequence of
k\times n
matrices
\{R(l)\}_{l=1,2,\ldots}
with
\lim_{l\to\infty}R(l)=0,
or equivalently
C(R(l))\to C,
for which

\mathrm{sgn}(\langle\widetilde{Z}(C(R(l)))Z_{i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}}\rangle)=-s,

thus establishing the claim.

Write

M_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}:=\{\overline{R}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{k\times n}|\overline{R}_{a}^{b}=0\penalty\ \forall(a,b)\geq(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\}\simeq\mathbb{R}^{\{(a,b)|\penalty\ (a,b)<(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\}}.

Since
F
is a multilinear function which is not identically
0,
it is non zero on a dense subset of
M_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}.
Let
\overline{R}(l)\in M_{(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})}
be a sequence of matrices on which
F(\overline{R}(l))\neq 0,
and
\overline{R}(l)\to 0
as
l\to\infty.
Define
R(l)\in\mathrm{Mat}_{k\times n}
by

R(l)_{a}^{b}=\begin{cases}\overline{R}(l)_{a}^{b},\penalty\ \penalty\ (a,b)<(a^{\prime},b^{\prime}),\\
(-s)\mathrm{sgn}(F(\overline{R}(l))/l,\penalty\ \penalty\ (a,b)=(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\\
0,\penalty\ \penalty\ (a,b)>(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})\end{cases}.

(11)

Then
C(R(l))\to C,
and, by (
10
) and (
11
),

\mathrm{sgn}(\langle\widetilde{Z}(C(R(l)))Z_{i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}}\rangle)=\mathrm{sgn}\left(\frac{(-s)\mathrm{sgn}(F(\overline{R}(l))}{l}F(\overline{R}(l))\right)=-s.

∎

Proposition 8.3
.

For every BCFW cell
S_{D}\in\mathcal{BCFW}_{n,k}
and
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{n\times(k+4)}
, the amplituhedron map
\widetilde{Z}
is injective on
\overline{S_{D}}\setminus S_{\partial\mathcal{A}},
and the inverse map is smooth. For every stratum
S\subseteq\overline{S_{D}}\setminus S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
, the image
\widetilde{Z}(S)
is an embedded smooth submanifold of
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}.

Proof.

By Lemma

7.15
and (
9
) every element of
\overline{S_{D}}\setminus S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
has an extended domino representation, hence belongs to the set
S^{\diamondsuit}_{D}
defined in Proposition
5.6
. The first claim is the a consequence of applying Proposition
5.6
. In particular
\widetilde{Z}|_{S}:S\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
is a topological embedding. It is left to show that
\widetilde{Z}|_{S}
is an immersion. We sketch the argument.
Let
p\in S
be an arbitrary point, and write
q=\widetilde{Z}(p).

Write
\mathcal{I}_{D}
for the set of Plücker coordinates which vanish on elements of
S_{D},
and
\mathcal{I}_{S}
for the set of Plücker coordinates which vanish on elements of
S.

By Lemma
8.1
the map
\widetilde{Z}
extends to a smooth map, given by the same formula, from an open neighborhood of
\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}
in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}.
In particular
\widetilde{Z}
extends smoothly, and by the same expression to a neighborhood
W
of
p
in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}.
Denote also the extended map by
\widetilde{Z}.
Similarly, since no boundary twistor vanishes on
q
, the inverse map constructed in Proposition
5.6
extends smoothly, and by the same expression, to the function
F_{D}(Y):=C_{D}(Y,Z)
, which is defined on an open neighborhood
U\subset\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
of
q,
on which no boundary twistor vanishes. The subscript
D
indicates it is calculated with respect to the
D
-domino form.
Indeed, the procedure of Proposition
5.6
assigns for any
q^{\prime}
on which no boundary twistor vanishes, a
matrix

F_{D}(q^{\prime})
that is in a
D
-domino form in the sense of Definition
2.5
, but does not necessarily satisfy the sign constraints of Definition
2.10
. Since
F_{D}
is a matrix-of-functionaries valued function it is in particular continuous and smooth, hence, if
q^{\prime}
is close enough to
q
then since
F_{D}(q)
has full rank, also
F_{D}(q^{\prime})
has. Thus,
F_{D}(q^{\prime})
represents a point in the Grassmannian, which has a
D
-domino representative only without the inequalities of Definition
2.10
, or
F_{D}(q^{\prime})\in S_{D}^{\diamondsuit}
. By shrinking
U
furthermore we may assume that
F_{D}(U)\subseteq W,
so that the amplituhedron map is well defined on
F_{D}(U).

Now, by Proposition
5.6

F_{D}:U\to F_{D}(U)
is a right inverse to
\widetilde{Z}.

Thus,
F_{D}(U)=S^{\diamondsuit}_{D}\cap\widetilde{Z}^{-1}(U),
and is a smooth submanifold of
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
of dimension
4k.
Note that Proposition
7.3
implies that for every full rank element of
S^{\diamondsuit}_{D}
, and in particular for elements of
F_{D}(U),
all Plücker coordinates contained in
\mathcal{I}_{D}
vanish.

We may assume, by making
U
smaller once more, that for every point in
F_{D}(U),
its set of vanishing Plücker coordinates is contained or equals
\mathcal{I}_{S}.

One can now pick, as explained in Summary
7.6
, a plabic graph with a perfect orientation for
S_{D},
such that after omitting edges one obtains a plabic graph for
S
(see also
[
Pos06
, Section 18]
).
This data induces a diffeomorphism (the
boundary measurement map
)
\Phi:V\to F_{D}(U)\cap\overline{S}_{D}
where
V\subset[0,\infty)^{4k}
is relatively open in
[0,\infty)^{4k},
and
\Phi^{-1}(F_{D}(U)\cap S)
is the subset of
V
where certain coordinates vanish. It is not hard to show, if
U
is small enough, that the boundary measurement map extends to a diffeomorphism
\Phi:B\to F_{D}(U),
where
B\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{4k}
is an open set with
B\cap[0,\infty)^{4k}=V.

Thus,
S\cap F_{D}(U)
is a smooth submanifold of
F_{D}(U)
of dimension
\dim(S),
obtained as the diffeomorphic image under
\Phi
of the zero locus of certain coordinate functions on
B
.

\widetilde{Z}|_{F_{D}(U)}:F_{D}(U)\to U
is a smooth bijection with a smooth inverse, hence a diffeomorphism, and its restriction to
S\cap F_{D}(U)
is therefore an immersion. Applying this argument for every
p\in S
yields the result.
∎

Remark 8.4
.

\partial_{\star}S_{D}
are defined using vanishing of matrix entries or of
2\times 2
minors. The inverse problem procedure provides a formula for each matrix entry in terms of twistor coordinates. Thus, the image of
\partial_{\star}S_{D}
under the amplituhedron map is contained in the zero locus of some function of the twistors. This function is of constant sign on
S_{D}.

Proposition 8.5
.

Let
f:B\to N
be a smooth submersion between two manifolds (
without boundary
)
B,N,
where the dimension of
N
is
n.
Let
L
be a connected open subset of
B
with a compact closure
\overline{L}\subset B.
Denote
f(\overline{L})
by
K.

Let
\{S_{a}\}_{a\in A}
be a collection of
n
-dimensional submanifolds
without boundary
of
B,
which are contained in
\overline{L}
and satisfy the following properties:

1.

f(S_{a})\cap f(S_{b})=\emptyset,\penalty\ a\neq b.

2.

\overline{S_{a}}
is compact. The (topological) boundary of each
S_{a}
has a stratification
\partial S_{a}=\bigcup_{i=1}^{k_{a}}S_{a;i}
where each
S_{a;i}
is a submanifold (
without boundary
) of
B
of dimension at most
n-1.
We further assume that for each
S_{a;i},
its topological boundary is contained in the union of strata
S_{a;j}
of smaller dimension.

3.

Every
f(S_{a;i})
is either contained in
\partial K,
the topological boundary of
K,
or it is a smooth submanifold of
K.
Moreover, if
S_{a;i}
is of dimension
n-1,
and
f(S_{a;i})\varsubsetneq\partial K,
then
S_{a;i}=S_{b;j}
for some other
b,j.
Boundaries of the latter type are called
shared boundaries
.

4.

For every
D_{a},

f
is injective on the union
S_{a}
and its shared boundaries, and the inverse map is continuous.

Then

f(\bigcup_{a\in A}\overline{S_{a}})=K.

Moreover,
f(S_{a;i})\cap\partial K=\emptyset
, for every shared boundary
S_{a;i},
and
\partial K
is precisely the union of
f(\overline{S_{a;i}}),
taken on
S_{a;i}
of dimension
n-1
which are not shared boundaries.

Our proof uses the following transversality result, whose proof is postponed to the end of the section.

Lemma 8.6
.

Let
M
be a smooth connected manifold of dimension
n
and
N_{1},\ldots,N_{r}
a finite collection of smooth submanifolds of dimensions at most
n-1.

Suppose that the closure of each
N_{i}
(inside
M
) is contained in the union of
N_{i}
with other submanifolds from
\{N_{1},\ldots,N_{r}\},
of dimensions smaller than
\dim(N_{i}).

Then for every

p,q\in M\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}N_{i}\right)

there exists a smooth path connecting
p,q
which
intersects each
N_{i}
finitely many times, and, if
\dim(N_{i})<n-1
then it does not intersect
N_{i}
at all.

Proof of Proposition
8.5
.

Since
L
is open and connected, and
f
is a submersion,
f(L)
is open and connected.
Since
\overline{L}
is compact and connected, so is
\overline{f(L)}=K.
Thus,
\text{int}(K),
the interior of
K,
which is an open set containing
f(L)
but contained in its closure, is connected as well.
Since
\overline{S_{a}}
are all compact, so are
f(\overline{S_{a}}).
By the third assumption and the smoothness of
f,
any
S_{a;i}
which does not map to
\partial K,
maps to a smooth submanifold of dimension at most the dimension of
S_{a;i}.

By the last item,
f
restricts to a homeomorphism of manifolds
with boundary
from the union of
S_{a}
and its shared boundaries, to its image. In particular, since
\text{dim}(S_{a})=\text{dim}(N),
each restriction
f|_{S_{a}}
is an open map.
The remaining boundaries of
S_{a}
either map to
\partial K
or to smooth submanifolds of codimension at least
2.

Assume by way of contradiction that the images of
(\overline{S_{a}})_{a\in A}
do not cover
K.

Then one can find a point
p\in\text{int}(K)
which is not in this union.
Take an arbitrary point
q
in the image of some
S_{a}.
Then

q\in\text{int}(K),
since
f|_{S_{a}}
is open and
f|_{S_{a}}(S_{a})=f(S_{a})\subset K.

We can apply Lemma
8.6
with
M=\text{int}(K),
which is a connected submanifold of
N,
and with
N_{1},\ldots,N_{r}
being the images
f(S_{a;i}),
for strata
S_{a;i}
which do not map to
\partial K.
The second and third items above guarantee that the assumptions of the lemma are satisfied. Thus, we can find a path which does not pass through any
f(S_{a;i}),
for boundary strata
S_{a;i}
of codimension two or more, and passes only finitely many times through images of
n-1
-dimensional boundary strata, which must be shared boundaries.
Denote this path by
u:[0,1]\to\text{int}(K),
with
u(0)=q,\penalty\ u(1)=p.

Let
t^{*}\in[0,1]
be the last time
u(t)
lies in the image of
\bigcup\overline{S_{a}}
. Then
t^{*}<1
since this union is closed. Moreover,
u(t^{*})
cannot belong to any
S_{a}
, since the image of any point in such a cell has a neighborhood in
\text{int}(K)
which is also contained in the image of the cell. So
u(t^{*})
must lie in the image of a shared boundary
S_{a;i}=S_{b;j},\penalty\ b\neq a.
Let
q^{*}
be the unique preimage of
u(t^{*})
in that boundary, let

W\subset{S_{a}}\cup S_{a;i}\cup{S_{b}}
be an open neighborhood of
q^{*}.
Write

W_{a}=W\cap({S_{a}}\cup S_{a;i}),\penalty\ W_{b}=W\cap({S_{b}}\cup S_{b;j}).

Then

W_{a}\cap S_{a;i}=W_{b}\cap S_{b;j}.

Write
U_{a}=f(W_{a}),\penalty\ U_{b}=f(W_{b})\subset K
.
Then
u(t^{*})
lies in the interior of
U_{a}\cup U_{b}.
Indeed, let
\mathbb{R}^{n}_{\pm}
be the half spaces of
\mathbb{R}^{n}
defined by first coordinate being non negative or non positive, and consider
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
as their subspace where the first coordinate is
0.

By making
W
smaller if necessary, we may assume that there are homeomorphisms of manifolds
with boundary

g:(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\to(W_{a},S_{a;i}\cap W_{a}),\penalty\ h:(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{-},\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\to(W_{b},S_{b;j}\cap W_{b})\penalty\ \text{such that }g|_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}=h|_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}},\penalty\ g(0)=q^{*}.

Composing with the map
f
we obtain maps

G:(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{+},\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\to(U_{a},\widehat{\partial U}),\penalty\ H:(\mathbb{R}^{n}_{-},\mathbb{R}^{n-1})\to(U_{b},\widehat{\partial U})\penalty\ \text{such that }G|_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}}=H|_{\mathbb{R}^{n-1}},\penalty\ G(0)=u(t^{*}),

where

\widehat{\partial{U}}=U_{a}\cap f(S_{a;i})=f(W_{a}\cap S_{a;i})=f(W_{b}\cap S_{b;j})=U_{b}\cap f(S_{b;j}).

We now perform a
gluing procedure
: we can glue
G
and
H
along the boundary
\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
, to obtain a map from
\mathbb{R}^{n}
to
U_{a}\cup U_{b}.
This map is continuous. It is injective since it is injective on each closed half space, by the previous paragraph, and also on
\mathbb{R}^{n}\setminus\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
by the first assumption. Its inverse is continuous since it is continuous on the image of each closed half space, by the fourth assumption again. Thus, it is a homeomorphism onto its image, which must be open, and contains
u(t^{*}).

But then for small enough
t^{\prime}>t^{*},\penalty\ u(t^{\prime})
must still be in
U_{a}\cup U_{b},
hence in the union of the images of
\overline{S}_{a},\overline{S}_{b}.
A contradiction to the definition of
t^{*}.

We turn to the ’More-over’ part.
Every point in a shared boundary
S_{a;i}
maps to an internal point of
K,
by the gluing procedure above. Thus the shared boundaries in the sense of this proposition indeed map to the interior of
K.

Since
f(\bigcup_{a\in A}S_{a})\subseteq\text{int}(K),
and
f(\bigcup_{a\in A}\overline{S_{a}})=K,
which is what we just showed,

\partial K\subseteq f(\bigcup_{a\in A}\partial S_{a}).

Write

S^{\prime}=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S_{a;i}\penalty\ \text{is not an internal}\\
\text{ boundary of $S_{a}$}\end{subarray}}S_{a;i},\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ S=\bigcup_{\begin{subarray}{c}S_{a;i}\penalty\ \text{is not a shared boundary}\\
\text{of $S_{a},$\penalty\ \penalty\ $\dim S_{a;i}=n-1$}\end{subarray}}S_{a;i}.

Then
S^{\prime}\setminus S
is the union of boundary components of dimensions at most
n-2.

Assume by way of contradiction that there exists
p\in\partial K\setminus f(\overline{S}).

Take a small neighborhood
U\subset N
of
p
which does not intersect
f(\overline{S})=\overline{f(S)}.

Then

U\cap\partial K\subseteq f(S^{\prime}\setminus S).

Since
p
is a boundary point, and
K
is the closure of an open set, we can find
q_{1}\in\text{int}(U\cap K),
and
q_{2}\in U\setminus K.

As in the proof of the previous part of the proposition, Lemma
8.6
, with
M=U
and
N_{1},\ldots N_{r}
are
f(S_{a;i})\cap U,
for the different strata
S_{a;i}
(note that for the strata which compose
S,

f(S_{a;i})\cap U=\emptyset
), guarantees the existence of a path in
U
which connects
q_{1},q_{2}
and does not pass through
f(S^{\prime}\setminus S).
This path cannot escape from
K,
as can be seen by repeating the argument from the previous part of the proposition. But this is a contradiction, since
q_{2}\notin K.

∎

Remark 8.7
.

The proof of the above proposition shows that the first item, the separation, can be weakened to just assuming that
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
are
locally separated
near their shared boundary, for every pair of cells that have share a boundary component, where by local separation we mean that the outward normals of the shared boundary, with respect to
S_{a}
and
S_{b},
are pointing oppositely.

Proof of Theorem
1.5
.

The proof follows from Proposition
8.5
, once we verify its assumptions.
More precisely, we verify that for
f=\widetilde{Z},
the amplituhedron map,
B\subset\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
being some open neighborhood of
\overline{L}
for
L=\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,n},

N=\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
and
\{S_{a}\}_{a\in A}
being the BCFW cells, the assumptions of Proposition
8.5
hold. The conclusion is that the closure of images of the BCFW cells covers
K=\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}.

We first verify the assumptions on
f=\widetilde{Z},L=\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,n},
and its neighborhood
B.

The open space
\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,n}
and its compact closure
\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}
are connected. We take
B\subset\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
to be the open neighborhood of
\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}
guaranteed by Lemma
8.1
. Hence the amplituhedron map extends to a smooth submersion
\widetilde{Z}:B\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+m}.

Next, we need to verify the assumptions of Proposition
8.5
for the BCFW cells. Their dimensions indeed equal
\dim(\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4})=4k
. The first property is Theorem
1.4
. The second property is true for any positroid cell. The third property follows from Proposition
8.2
and Proposition
7.11
. Proposition
8.3
shows the fourth property, that
\widetilde{Z}
is injective on the union of each BCFW cell and its
shared boundaries
(in the sense of Proposition
8.5
), those codimension one boundary strata which do not map to the boundary, and that
\widetilde{Z}^{-1}
is injective on the image of this union.
The theorem follows.
∎

We have the following corollaries.

Corollary 8.8
.

The boundary of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
equals
\widetilde{Z}(S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}),
which is, by Remark
4.17
, precisely the union of the intersections of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
with the hyperplanes

\langle i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}\rangle=0,

for
i,j\in[n]
such that
|\{i,i{{\pluscirc}1},j,j{{\pluscirc}1}\}|=4.

Thus,
\text{int}(\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z))=\widetilde{Z}(\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}\setminus S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}).

Proof.

The ’More-over’ part of Proposition
8.5
, applied to the amplituhedron, as in the first paragraph of the proof of Theorem
1.5
, whose conditions were verified in the course of proof of Theorem
1.5
, describes the topological boundary of
\mathcal{A}_{j,k,4}(Z):
it is the union of
\widetilde{Z}(S),
over all codimension
1
boundary strata
S
of BCFW cells, which are not shared boundaries (in the sense of Proposition
8.5
). By Proposition
7.11
all codimension
1
boundaries strata of BCFW cells which are not internal are contained in
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}.
Since
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
is closed this implies

\partial\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}\subseteq\widetilde{Z}(S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}).

On the other hand, Proposition
8.2
implies the other containment

\partial\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}\supseteq\widetilde{Z}(S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}).

∎

Corollary 8.9
.

The image of each codimension-one stratum of a BCFW cell that does not map to the boundary, is also the injective image of a codimension-one boundary of another BCFW cell.

Proof.

From the previous corollary every codimension
1
boundary stratum of a BCFW cell that does not map to the boundary does not intersect
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
. From Proposition
7.11
every such boundary stratum is also a boundary stratum of another BCFW cell. By Proposition
8.3
the amplituhedron map is injective on these boundaries.
∎

We conclude this section with the proof of Lemma
8.6
. The proof relies on the notion of transversality and on Thom’s Parametric Transversality Theorem
[
Tho54
]
that we now recall. A proof of Thom’s theorem can be found, for example, in
[
GP10
]
. We assume that all manifolds of interest have a countable atlas.

Definition 8.10
.

Let
f:X\to Y
be a smooth map between a smooth manifold with boundary
X
and a smooth manifold
Y.
Let
Q
be a smooth submanifold of
Y.
We say that
f
is
transverse
to
Q,
and write
f\pitchfork Q
if for every
x\in f^{-1}(Q)

df_{x}(T_{x}X)+T_{f(x)}Q=T_{f(x)}Y,

where
T_{b}B
denotes the tangent space of
B
at
b\in B,
and
df_{x}
is the differential map at
x,
which maps
T_{x}X
into
T_{f(x)}Y.

We denote by
\partial f
the restriction of
f
to
\partial X.

Definition 8.11
.

Let
X
be an
n
dimensional manifold with an atlas
\{(U_{\alpha},\phi_{\alpha}:U_{\alpha}\to\mathbb{R}^{n})\}_{\alpha\in A},
we say that a set
C\subseteq X
is of measure
0,
if for every
\alpha\in A,
the set
\phi_{\alpha}(M\cap U_{\alpha})
is of Lebesgue measure
0
in
\mathbb{R}^{n}.

If
Y\subset X
is the complement of a measure
0
subset, we say that almost every
x\in X
belongs to
Y.

Theorem 8.12

(Thom’s Parametric Transversality Theorem)
.

Let
X
be a smooth manifold with boundary, let
B,Y
be smooth manifolds and let
Q
be a submanifold of
Y
. Let
F:X\times B\to Y
be a smooth map.
Suppose that
F\pitchfork Q
and that
\partial F\pitchfork Q.

Then for almost every
b\in B
the map

F(-,b):X\times\{b\}\to Y

is transverse to
Q.

Proof of Lemma
8.6
.

Fix
p,q\in M\setminus\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{r}N_{i}\right)
and
\epsilon>0.

Take an arbitrary smooth embedding
u_{0}:(-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)\to M
with
u(0)=p,u(1)=q.

Extend
u_{0}
to a diffeomorphism
U
between
(-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)\times B_{1},
where
B_{1}\subset\mathbb{R}^{n-1}
is the open unit ball, and a tubular neighborhood
W
of
u_{0}((-\epsilon,1+\epsilon)).

Define
F:[0,1]\times B_{1}\to M
by

F(t,x)=U(t,t(1-t)x).

We claim that
F
satisfies the assumptions of Theorem
8.12
with
X=[0,1],\penalty\ B=B_{1}
and
Q=N_{i},
for any given
i.
Since
F(0,x)=p\notin N_{i},\penalty\ F(1,x)=q\notin N_{i},
it follows that
\partial F\pitchfork N_{i}.
It is left to verify
F|_{(0,1)\times B_{1}}\pitchfork N_{i}.
Since
U
is a submersion, also
F
is a submersion when
t\neq 0,1.
Thus,

dF_{(t,x)}\left(T_{(t,x)}\left((0,1)\times B_{1}\right)\right)=T_{F(t,x)}M.

Applying Theorem
8.12
we see that for each
i,
for almost all
b\in B_{1},
the path
F(-,b)\pitchfork N_{i}.
Thus, we can find
b
for which

F(-,b)\pitchfork N_{1},\ldots,N_{r}.

Write
u(t)=F(t,b).

Then
u:[0,1]\to M
is a smooth path which satisfies

u(0)=p,\penalty\ u(1)=q,\penalty\ u\pitchfork N_{i}\penalty\ \text{for }i=1,\ldots,r.

For any
i
with
\dim(N_{i})<n-1,
the definition of transversality, and the fact that
du_{x}(T_{x}(0,1))
is one dimensional implies that
u([0,1])\cap N_{i}=\emptyset.

Suppose that
\dim(N_{i})=n-1,
but assume, towards contradiction, that
u([0,1])
intersects
N_{i}
infinitely many times. Let
t_{1},t_{2},\ldots
be a converging sequence of such times, and let
t_{*}
be its limit.
Since
u
is continuous,

u(t_{*})=\lim_{j\to\infty}u(t_{j})\in\overline{N_{i}}.

Since
p,q\notin\overline{N_{i}},
by the assumptions,
t_{*}\in(0,1).
Since
\overline{N_{i}}
is contained in the union of
N_{i}
with submanifolds
N_{j}
of smaller dimension, which are known not to intersect
u([0,1])
we can deduce that
u(t_{*})\in N_{i}.

Because
u
is smooth, and the sequence
\{t_{i}\}_{i=1}^{\infty}
converges to
t_{*},
we can calculate the tangent to
u((0,1))
at
u(t_{*})
along the sequence
\{u(t_{i})\}_{i=1}^{\infty}\subset N_{i},
which shows that this tangent belongs to
T_{u(t_{*})}N_{i},
contradicting the transversality. The proof follows.
∎

9
Consequences

In this final section we present two consequences of our results and methods to the topology and the structure of the amplituhedron. First, the interior of the amplituhedron
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
is homeomorphic to an open ball, for every positive
Z
. So far, this has been known for one particular
Z
, based on a general-
m
result by Galashin, Karp and Lam
[
GKL22
]
, see also
[
BK23
]
.

Our second result gives a decomposition of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
as a union of
\widetilde{Z}
-images of a certain type of products of two positive Grassmannians. This kind of decomposition is less refined than the BCFW triangulation that we define in Section
2
following the work of Karp, Williams, and Zhang
[
KWZ20
]
, based on Arkani-Hamed et al.
[
ABC+16
]
. However, this high-level decomposition more transparently reflects the outcome of one step in the BCFW recurrence, see Bai and He
[
BH15
]
. A consequence of this decomposition and other tools developed in this work is a mechanism to obtain triangulations of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
from various triangulations of amplituhedra of smaller
k
and
n
. This includes many more collections of cells beyond those discussed in this work, such as those obtainable from different ways to apply the BCFW recursion. These further results will appear in a separate paper
[
ELP+23
]
.

9.1
The Interior is a Ball

Galashin, Karp and Lam
[
GKL22
]
proved the following result

Theorem 9.1
.

For every
n,k,m
there is a special choice of a positive matrix
Z_{*}=Z_{*}^{n,k,m}
such that the amplituhedron
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,m}(Z_{*})
is homeomorphic to a closed ball.

An immediate conclusion is that for the same
Z_{*}
the interior of the amplituhedron is homeomorphic to an open ball. We now show that when
m=4,
this last fact generalizes for every positive
Z.

Theorem 9.2
.

Let
Z
be an arbitrary positive
n\times(k+4)
matrix. Then the interior of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
is homeomorphic to an open ball.

The proof relies on the following lemma, which is interesting in its own right.

Lemma 9.3
.

The amplituhedron map is a homeomorphism from
M=\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}}\overline{S_{D}}\setminus S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}
onto its image
\text{int}(\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}).

Proof.

\widetilde{Z}
is continuous on
M,
and surjects on its image
\text{int}(\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4})
by Theorem
1.5
and Corollary
8.8
.
We need to prove it is also injective and open.

M
is endowed with the topology induced from the Grassmannian
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}.

Write
M_{i}
for the open subset of
M
defined as the union of all dimension
4k-j
strata of
\overline{S_{D}}\setminus S_{\partial\mathcal{A}},
over all BCFW cells and
j=0,\ldots,i.
Note that
M=M_{4k}.

We prove the following three claims, whose combination immediately implies the lemma:

1.

\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{0}}:M_{0}\to\widetilde{Z}(M_{0})
is a homeomorphism, and
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{1}}:M_{1}\to\widetilde{Z}(M_{1})
is open.

2.

If for
i\geq 1,

\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{i}}
is open, then
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{i}}
is also injective.

3.

If for
i\geq 2,

\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{i-1}}:M_{i-1}\to\widetilde{Z}(M_{i-1})
is a homeomorphism, then
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{i}}
is an open map.

We start with the first claim.
For each point of
p\in S_{D}\subseteq M_{0}
the proof of Theorem
1.3
shows that
\widetilde{Z}
maps a small open neighborhood of
p
in
S_{D}
homeomorphically on its image, which is open in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
. Thus
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{0}}
is open.
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{0}}
is injective by Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
.

M_{1}\setminus M_{0}
is the union of shared boundaries of BCFW cells. Consider
p\in S,
where
S
is a shared boundary of a BCFW cell
S_{D}
. Then, by Proposition
7.11
it is a codimension
1
boundary of another BCFW cell
S_{D^{\prime}}.
By Proposition
8.3
and Theorems
1.3
and
1.4
,
\widetilde{Z}|_{S_{D}\coprod S\coprod S_{D^{\prime}}}
is injective. The proof of Proposition
8.5
uses a gluing procedure to construct, for every point
q^{*}\in S,
a neighborhood in
S_{D}\coprod S\coprod S_{D^{\prime}}
which maps homeomorphically under
\widetilde{Z}
to an open neighborhood of
\widetilde{Z}(q^{*})
in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}.
Thus
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{1}}
is open.

For the second claim, assuming
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{i}}
is open, if
\widetilde{Z}(p_{1})=\widetilde{Z}(p_{2})
for some
p_{1}\neq p_{2}\in M_{i},
then for any two neighborhoods
B_{1},B_{2}\subset M_{i}
of
p_{1},p_{2}
respectively,
\widetilde{Z}(B_{1})\cap\widetilde{Z}(B_{2})
contains an open ball
B\subset\widetilde{Z}(M_{i})
around
\widetilde{Z}(p_{1})=\widetilde{Z}(p_{2}).
By making
B_{1},B_{2}
smaller we may assume
B_{1}\cap B_{2}=\emptyset.
Since
\widetilde{Z}
is a smooth map, the images of strata of positive codimension are nowhere dense, hence there are
q_{1}\in B_{1}\cap\left(\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}}{S_{D}}\right)=B_{1}\cap M_{0}
and
q_{2}\in B_{2}\cap\left(\bigcup_{D\in\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}}{S_{D}}\right)=B_{2}\cap M_{0}
respectively, with
\widetilde{Z}(q_{1})=\widetilde{Z}(q_{2}),
in contradiction to the first claim, which shows that
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{0}}
is injective.

Finally, for the third claim,
suppose that
\widetilde{Z}
is a homeomorphism on
M_{i-1},
for some
i\geq 2,
but assume towards contradiction that for some point
p\in M_{i}
we cannot find a small enough neighborhood of it in
M_{i}
which is mapped under
\widetilde{Z}
to an open set. Then
p\in M_{i}\setminus M_{i-1},
since
M_{i-1}
is open in
M_{i}
and
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{i-1}}
is open. Let
S
be the positroid cell containing
p.

Take a smooth metric on
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n}
and restrict to
\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n}.
For each point
x\in\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,n},
and every
r>0
small enough, the ball of radius
r
around
x
only intersects positroid cells that contain
x
in their closures. Moreover, the metric can be chosen so that the intersection of the ball with any stratum is path connected.
Restrict the metric to
M.
Take an open ball
B
in
M
around
p,
which is small enough to be path connected and that its closure intersects only
positroid cells
S^{\prime}
with
p\in\overline{S^{\prime}}.
By Proposition
8.3

\widetilde{Z}
is injective on
S\cap B,
and moreover, for every
x
which belongs to a positroid cell
S^{\prime}\neq S
with
S\subseteq\overline{S^{\prime}},\penalty\ S^{\prime}\subseteq M_{i-1}

\widetilde{Z}(x)\notin\widetilde{Z}(S).

By the assumption,
\widetilde{Z}
is also injective on
B\setminus S\subset M_{i-1},
and hence
\widetilde{Z}
is injective on
B.

By its construction
B
is a connected and path connected component of
M\setminus\partial B,
and
\widetilde{Z}(\partial B),\widetilde{Z}(\overline{B})
are compact subsets of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4},
which do not intersect
\partial\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4},
as
B\cap S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}=\emptyset.

Every connected component of
\text{int}(\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4})\setminus\widetilde{Z}(\partial B)
is open. We will therefore finish by showing that
\widetilde{Z}(B)
is a path connected component of
\text{int}(\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4})\setminus\widetilde{Z}(\partial B).

Since
B
is path connected, also
\widetilde{Z}(B)
is. Let
C
be the connected component of
\text{int}(\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4})\setminus\widetilde{Z}(\partial B)
which contains it.
C
is an open manifold. It is enough to show that there is no
x\in C\setminus\widetilde{Z}(B).
If there were such
x,
then since
x\notin\widetilde{Z}(\partial B)
as well, then
x\notin\widetilde{Z}(\overline{B}).
Since
\widetilde{Z}(\overline{B})=\overline{\widetilde{Z}(B)}
is compact,
x
has an open connected neighborhood
U\subseteq C
with
U\cap\widetilde{Z}(\overline{B})=\emptyset.

Since
U
is open it contains a point
\widetilde{Z}(q_{1})
for
q_{1}
which belongs to a BCFW cell. Let
q_{2}\in B
be another point of a BCFW cell. We can apply Lemma
8.6
, with
C
as the smooth connected manifold
M
from the statement of the lemma, and
N_{i},\penalty\ i=1,2,\ldots,r
are the intersections of
C
with the different strata of the different
\overline{S_{D}}\setminus S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}.
The assumptions of the lemma are easily verified, exactly as in the proof of Proposition
8.5
. Thus, we can find a path in
C
which connects
\widetilde{Z}(q_{1})
and
\widetilde{Z}(q_{2}),
and does not intersect
C\setminus M_{1}.
This path is therefore contained in
C\cap M_{1}.

Since
\widetilde{Z}|_{M_{1}}
is a homeomorphism, this path lifts to a path in
M,
which connects
q_{1},q_{2}
but does not intersect
\partial B.
This is a contradiction, since
q_{1}\in B,\penalty\ q_{2}\notin B
and
B
is a path connected component of
M\setminus\partial B.
And the proof follows.
∎

Proof of Theorem
9.2
.

Let
Z_{*}
be as in Theorem
9.1
.
By Lemma
9.3
and Theorem
9.1
,
\widetilde{Z}_{*}
maps
M
homeomorphically on
\text{int}(\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z_{*})),
which is an open ball. In particular
M
is homeomorphic to an open ball. Now, let
Z
be an arbitrary positive matrix. By using Lemma
9.3
for this
Z,

\widetilde{Z}(M)=\text{int}(\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z))
is also homeomorphic to an open ball.
∎

9.2
A High-Level Decomposition

In this section we describe the decomposition of the amplituhedron corresponding to one step in the BCFW recurrence, which is stated in Theorem
9.5
below. It is given in terms of the following definition.

Definition 9.4
.

Let
k_{1},k_{2},j
and
n
be nonnegative integers satisfying

k_{1}+k_{2}\leq n-5,\penalty\ k_{1}\leq j-2,\penalty\ k_{2}\leq n-j-4.

For
s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{+},
and two matrices

L\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}L_{1}^{1}&\cdots&L_{1}^{j+1}&L_{1}^{n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots\\
L_{k_{1}}^{1}&\cdots&L_{k_{1}}^{j+1}&L_{k_{1}}^{n}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{k_{1}\times([j+1]\cup\{n\})},\penalty\ \penalty\ R\;=\;\begin{pmatrix}R_{1}^{j}&\cdots&R_{1}^{n-1}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots\\
R_{k_{2}}^{j}&\cdots&R_{k_{2}}^{n-1}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathrm{Mat}_{k_{2}\times([n-1]\setminus[j-1])}

we define the
middle embedding

{\Upsilon}_{j}(s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2},L,R)
as the matrix

\left(\begin{array}[]{*{11}{c}}L_{1}^{1}&\cdots&L_{1}^{j-1}&L_{1}^{j}{+}\frac{s_{1}}{s_{2}}L_{1}^{j+1}&L_{1}^{j+1}&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\!(-1)^{k_{2}}L_{1}^{n}\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
L_{k_{1}}^{1}&\cdots&L_{k_{1}}^{j-1}&L_{k_{1}}^{j}{+}\frac{s_{1}}{s_{2}}L_{k_{1}}^{j+1}&L_{k_{1}}^{j+1}&0&\cdots&0&0&0&\!(-1)^{k_{2}}L_{k_{1}}^{n}\\[2.5pt]
0&\cdots&0&s_{1}&s_{2}&0&\cdots&0&(-1)^{k_{2}}t_{1}&(-1)^{k_{2}}t_{2}&(-1)^{k_{2}}\\
0&\cdots&0&R_{1}^{j}&\!\!R_{1}^{j+1}{+}\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}}R_{1}^{j}&R_{1}^{j+2}&\cdots&R_{1}^{n-3}&R_{1}^{n-2}{+}\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}R_{1}^{n-1}&R_{1}^{n-1}&0\\
\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\ddots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots&\vdots\\
0&\cdots&0&R_{k_{2}}^{j}&\!\!R_{k_{2}}^{j+1}{+}\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}}R_{k_{2}}^{j}&R_{k_{2}}^{j+2}&\cdots&R_{k_{2}}^{n-3}&R_{k_{2}}^{n-2}{+}\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}}R_{k_{2}}^{n-1}&R_{k_{2}}^{n-1}&0\end{array}\right)

In other words,

{\Upsilon}_{j}(s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2},L,R)=\begin{pmatrix}L^{\prime}\\
v\\
R^{\prime}\end{pmatrix},

where
L^{\prime}
is the
k_{1}\times[n]
matrix

L^{\prime}={\mathrm{pre}}_{j+2}\ldots{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}y_{j}(\frac{s_{1}}{s_{2}})L

R^{\prime}
is the
k_{2}\times[n]
matrix

R^{\prime}={\mathrm{pre}}_{1}\ldots{\mathrm{pre}}_{j-1}{\mathrm{pre}}_{n}x_{j}(\frac{s_{2}}{s_{1}})y_{n-2}(\frac{t_{1}}{t_{2}})R

and
v
is the vector

(0,\ldots,0,s_{1},s_{2},0\ldots,0,(-1)^{k_{2}}t_{1},(-1)^{k_{2}}t_{2},(-1)^{k_{2}}),

where the non zero entries are at positions
j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n.

This map descends to a rational map

\mathrm{Gr}_{k_{1},[j+1]\cup\{n\}}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{1,5}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{k_{2},[n-1]\setminus[j-1]}\dashrightarrow\mathrm{Gr}_{k,n},

and preserves positivity when applied to nonnegative Grassmannians.

Define the set
S_{j,n;k_{1},k_{2}}
to be the middle embedding
{\Upsilon}_{j}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4},\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k_{1},[j+1]\cup\{n\}},\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k_{2},[n-1]\setminus[j-1]}).
We extend the definition to the case
k_{1}=0,j=1
by setting

S_{1,n;0,k_{2}}=\overleftrightarrow{\;\textsc{upp}\;}_{1}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\times\mathrm{Gr}_{k_{2},[n]\setminus\{1\}}).

The middle embedding operation was considered implicitly in
[
AT14
]
, and explicitly in
[
BH15
]
as part of their explicit interpretation of the BCFW recursion. In matrix formulation it appeared in
[
Bou10
]
.
The lower embedding can be seen to be the special case
k_{2}=0,\penalty\ j=n-4.

Each
S_{j,n;k_{1},k_{2}}
is a positroid cell. The simplest way to show that is by using plabic graphs formalism, see
[
ELP+23
, Proposition 5.8]
for a proof.
Lemma
9.6
below provides an equivalent description of these sets, which manifests them being positroid cells. This latter description is the one we shall use in what follows.

Theorem 9.5
.

For all
k\geq 0,\penalty\ n\geq k+4
, and positive
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{n\times(k+4)}
, the sets

\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}}),\;\;\;\;\;\;\widetilde{Z}(S_{1,n;0,k-1}),\;\;\;\;\;\;\left\{\widetilde{Z}(S_{j,n;k_{1},k_{2}})\right\}_{\begin{subarray}{c}k_{1}\geq 0,\,k_{2}\geq 0,\,k_{1}+k_{2}=k-1\\
k_{1}+2\leq j\leq n-k_{2}-4\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\end{subarray}}

are disjoint and their union is dense in

\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z).

The following lemma provides alternative descriptions for the middle embedding, which are in the spirit of the constructions of Section
3
.

Lemma 9.6
.

For
(k_{1},j)\neq(0,1)
the space
S_{j,n;k_{1},k_{2}}
can be constructed in the following two ways.

1.

Start with
\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k_{2},[n]\setminus[j]}.
Apply

•

Upper embedding
\overleftrightarrow{\;\textsc{upp}\;}_{j}

to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k_{2},[n]\setminus[j]}.

•

Perform
{\mathrm{inc}}_{1},\ldots,{\mathrm{inc}}_{k_{1}},
and then
{\mathrm{pre}}_{k_{1}+1},\ldots,{\mathrm{pre}}_{j-1}.

•

For each
i=1,\ldots,k_{1}
in that order apply
y_{i{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}(t_{i;1}),\penalty\ t_{i;1}>0.

•

For each
h=1,\ldots,j+1-k_{1},
apply for
i=k_{1},\ldots,1,
in that order, the operation
x_{i+h-1}(t_{i;h+1})
with
t_{\bullet;\bullet}>0.

2.

Start with
\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k_{1},[j+1]\cup\{n\}}.
Apply

•

Lower embedding
\overleftrightarrow{\;\textsc{low}\;}_{n-2}

to
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\times{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k_{1},[j+1]\cup\{n\}}.

•

Perform
{\mathrm{inc}}_{j+2},\ldots,{\mathrm{inc}}_{k_{2}+j+1},
followed by
{\mathrm{pre}}_{k_{2}+j+2},\ldots,{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-3}.

•

For each
i=1,\ldots,k_{2},
in that order apply
y_{j+1+i{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}1}(t_{i;1}),\penalty\ y_{j+1+i{\,\;\mathclap{\circ}\mathclap{\relbar}\;\,}2}(t_{i;2}),\penalty\ t_{i;1},t_{i;2}>0.

•

For each
h=1,\ldots,n-2-k_{2}-j,
apply for
i=k_{2},\ldots,1,
in that order
x_{j+i+h}(t_{i;h+2})
with
t_{\bullet;\bullet}>0.

Sketch of proof.

The two constructions in the statement of the lemma are positroid cells by their construction. As mentioned above, by
[
ELP+23
, Proposition 5.8]
, also each
S_{j,n;k_{1},k_{2}}
is a positroid cell. The dimensions of these three positroid cells are easily seen to be
(j+2-k_{1})k_{1}+(n-j-k_{2})k_{2}+4.

We claim that for each of these three positroid cells, the Plücker coordinate
P_{I}
is non vanishing precisely if
I
can be written as

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\},\penalty\ I_{1}\in\binom{[j+1]\cup\{n\}}{k_{1}},\penalty\ I_{2}\in\binom{[n-1]\setminus[j-1]}{k_{2}},\penalty\ a\in\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}.

This is easily checked for
S_{j,n;k_{1},k_{2}}.

In order to analyze the two constructions, note that the effect of
x_{i}(t),\penalty\ t>0
on the set of non vanishing Plücker coordinates is as follows:
P_{I}\neq 0
on
x_{i}(t)S
precisely if
P_{I}\neq 0
on
S,
or
\{i+1\in I\}
and
P_{I\setminus\{i+1\}\cup\{i\}}\neq 0
on
S.
The same rule holds for
y_{i}
with the roles of
i,i+1
interchanged.

Let
S_{0}
be the output of the first two steps, i.e. the upper embedding and the sequence of
{\mathrm{pre}}_{h},{\mathrm{inc}}_{h}.
Then it is easily seen that the non vanishing Plücker coordinates for
S_{0}
are precisely sets
I
of the form

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\},\penalty\ I_{1}=[k_{1}],\penalty\ I_{2}\in\binom{[n-1]\setminus[j-1]}{k_{2}},\penalty\ a\in\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}.

After the third step, i.e. the sequence of
y_{h}
operations, by the above rule, the non vanishing coordinates
P_{I}
are those with

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\},\penalty\ I_{1}\in\binom{[k_{1}]\cup\{n\}}{k_{1}},\penalty\ I_{2}\in\binom{[n-1]\setminus[j-1]}{k_{2}},\penalty\ a\in\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}.

Similarly, after the
h
th part of the fourth step, i.e. the applications of

x_{i+h-1}(t_{i;h+1}),\penalty\ i=k_{1},\ldots,1,
the set of non zero coordinates grows to

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\},\penalty\ I_{1}\in\binom{[k_{1}+h]\cup\{n\}}{k_{1}},\penalty\ I_{2}\in\binom{[n-1]\setminus[j-1]}{k_{2}},\penalty\ a\in\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}.

In the end of the fourth step, when
h=j+1-k_{1},
the resulting set of non vanishing Plücker coordinates is as stated.

The argument for the second positroid cell constructed above is similar. After the first two steps the non vanishing Plücker coordinates are
P_{I}
for

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\},\penalty\ I_{1}\in\binom{[j+1]\cup\{n\}}{k_{1}},\penalty\ I_{2}=\{j+2,j+3,\ldots,j+k_{2}+1\},\penalty\ a\in\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}.

After the third step the set of non zero coordinates becomes

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\},\penalty\ I_{1}\in\binom{[j+1]\cup\{n\}}{k_{1}},\penalty\ I_{2}\in\binom{[j+k_{2}+1]\setminus[j-1]}{k_{2}},\penalty\ a\in\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}.

After the
h
th part of the fourth step this set further grows to be

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\},\penalty\ I_{1}\in\binom{[j+1]\cup\{n\}}{k_{1}},\penalty\ I_{2}\in\binom{[j+k_{2}+1+h]\setminus[j-1]}{k_{2}},\penalty\ a\in\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\},

and in the end of the fourth step, when
h=n-2-k_{2}-j,
it becomes

I=I_{1}\cup I_{2}\cup\{a\},\penalty\ I_{1}\in\binom{[j+1]\cup\{n\}}{k_{1}},\penalty\ I_{2}\in\binom{[n-1]\setminus[j-1]}{k_{2}},\penalty\ a\in\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}.

Thus, the cells are all equal.
∎

Lemma 9.7
.

Let
Z
be a positive
n\times(k+4)
matrix. At every point in
\widetilde{Z}(S_{i,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1})
,

•

The twistors
\langle i+1,n-2,n-1,n\rangle,\penalty\ \langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle,\penalty\ \langle i,i+1,n-2,n\rangle
have constant signs
(-1)^{k-k_{1}-1},\penalty\ (-1)^{k-k_{1}}\penalty\
and
-1,
respectively, on
\widetilde{Z}(S_{i,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1}).

•

The functionaries

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i,i+1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}

are negative for
j\in\{i+1,\ldots,n-4\},
while

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j,j+1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}

are positive for
j\in[i-1].

Proof.

The proof of the first item is completely analogous to the proof of Lemma
5.2
,(
1
).
Every point of
S_{i,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1}
contains a vector supported on positions
i,i+1,n-2,n-1,n,
as seen from Definition
9.4
. As in Lemma
5.2
,(
1
), its
i
th,
i+1
th and
n-1
th entries are proportional to

(-1)^{k-k_{1}-1}\langle i+1,n-2,n-1,n\rangle,\penalty\ (-1)^{k-k_{1}}\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle,\penalty\ (-1)^{k-k_{1}}\langle i,i+1,n-2,n\rangle

respectively.
The first item follows from showing that these twistors are nonzero.
As in the proof of Lemma
5.2
,(
1
), when a twistor coordinate
\langle Z_{\{i,i+1,n-2,n-1,n\}\setminus\{p\}}\rangle,\penalty\ p=i,i+1,n-1,
is expanded using Cauchy-Binet, all summands
\langle C^{I}\rangle\langle Z_{I\cup(\{i,i+1,n-2,n-1,n\}\setminus\{p\})}\rangle
have the same sign. We therefore finish by showing that at least one such
\langle C^{I}\rangle,
for
I\cap(\{i,i+1,n-2,n-1,n\}\setminus\{p\})=\emptyset,
is nonzero. The existence of such
I
was shown for every chord diagram in
\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}
that contains a top chord
(i,i+1,n-2,n-1)
with
k-k_{1}-1
descendants in Lemma
3.32
. Since every such chord diagram is contained in the closure of
S_{i,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1},
the Plücker coordinate
P_{I},
for the same
I,
is non vanishing also on
S_{i,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1}.

The proof of the second item when
(k_{1},j)=(0,1)
is just Example
4.46
. For
(k_{1},j)\neq(0,1)
the proof is completely analogous to the proof of Proposition
6.2
, where any usage of Corollaries
3.47
-
3.49
is replaced by Lemma
9.6
.
For the case
j>i
we use the first construction of Lemma
9.6
. After its first step, the upper embedding of
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{4}\times\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k-k_{1}-1,[n]\setminus[i]},
using Corollary
4.44
we deduce that the functionaries

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,i,i+1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}=-{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j,j+1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}

for
j\in\{i+1,\ldots,n-4\}
are all negative. The remaining steps involve applications of
{\mathrm{inc}}_{h},\penalty\ {\mathrm{pre}}_{h},\penalty\ x_{h},\penalty\ y_{h}
which do not affect the functionary, or its sign, by Lemmas
4.20
,
4.22
,
4.26
.

Similarly, for
j<i,
we use the second construction of Lemma
9.6
. After the lower embedding step the functionary

{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j,j+1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}

is positive on the resulting cell, by Corollary
4.45
. The same reasoning as in the previous case shows that the functionary and its sign are preserved under the following steps of the construction.
∎

Notation 9.8
.

Denote by
\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}
the subset of
\mathcal{CD}_{n,k_{1}+k_{2}+1}
which consists of chord diagrams with a rightmost top chord
(i,i+1,n-2,n-1)
which has
k_{2}
descendants.

Proposition 9.9
.

For every
Z\in\mathrm{Mat}^{>}_{n\times(k+4)}
,

\widetilde{Z}(S_{i,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1})
is triangulated by the images of the BCFW cells that correspond to the chord diagrams in
\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1}.

Proof.

Write
k_{2}=k-k_{1}-1.

Every cell
S_{D}
which corresponds to a chord diagram
D\in\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}
is clearly in the closure of
S_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}.
Thus,
\widetilde{Z}(S_{D})\subseteq\overline{\widetilde{Z}(S_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}})}.
Each such
S_{D}
maps injectively into
\overline{\widetilde{Z}(S_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}})},
by Theorem
1.3
, and the images of every two cells
S_{a},S_{b}
corresponding to a pair of different diagrams
D_{a},D_{b}\in\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}
are disjoint, by Theorem
1.4
.

Write
S=\bigcup_{D_{a}\in\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}}S_{a}
, where
S_{a}:=S_{D_{a}}
. We would like to analyze codimension
1
boundaries of
S.
These boundaries come from codimension
1
boundaries of cells
S_{a}
in the union. By the treatment of Section
7
, and in particular Proposition
7.11
, we know that some of them belong to two cells in the union, some map to the boundaries, and the remaining are codimension
1
boundaries of both
S_{a}
and
S_{b}
where the former is in the union, and the latter is not. In the last situation the diagram
D_{a}
contains has a top chord
(i,i+1,n-2,n-1)
with
k_{2}
descendants, while the diagram
D_{b}
does not have such a top chord. Since
S_{a},\penalty\ S_{b}
share a boundary,
D_{a},D_{b}
must differ by a shift. There are three possibilities for the nature of this shift:

1.

It either moves the chord
c_{l}=(i,i+1,n-2,n-1),
or

2.

the shift makes
c_{l}
a descendant of another chord, or

3.

the shift changes the number of chords which descend from the top chord
(i,i+1,n-2,n-1).

The first possibility may happen in one of three ways. It is either the result of a left shift of
c_{l}
’s start, or of an unobstructed right shift of
c_{l}
’s start or of a left shift of
c_{l}
’s end.
The first two ways, and the unobstructed version of the third way, correspond to the vanishings of
\beta_{l},\alpha_{l},\hat{\delta}_{l},
or equivalently the twistors

\langle i,n-2,n-1,n\rangle,\penalty\ \langle i+1,n-2,n-1,n\rangle,\penalty\ \langle i,i+1,n-2,n\rangle,

respectively.

The left shift of the end of
c_{l}
is obstructed precisely when
c_{l}
has a same end child
c_{h}=(j,j+1,n-2,n-1).
On the corresponding boundary
\eta_{l,h}
vanishes. By adding the appropriate multiple of the row
C_{l}
to the row
C_{h}
that cancels the inherited domino, the resulting new
h
th row is supported on
j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n,
and its entries can be solved in terms of twistors, as in the inverse problem procedure of Section
5
. If we denote by
C^{\prime}
the resulting matrix, then

(C^{\prime})_{h,l}^{n-2,n-1}=\begin{pmatrix}&\langle i,i+1,n-1,n\rangle&-\langle i,i+1,n-2,n\rangle\\
&\langle j,j+1,n-1,n\rangle&-\langle j,j+1,n-2,n\rangle\end{pmatrix}.

The vanishing of
\eta_{l,h}
is the vanishing of the determinant of the minor
C_{h,l}^{n-2,n-1},
which is equivalent to the vanishing of the determinant of
(C^{\prime})_{h,l}^{n-2,n-1},
which can thus be written as

\det\begin{pmatrix}&\langle i,i+1,n-1,n\rangle&-\langle i,i+1,n-2,n\rangle\\
&\langle j,j+1,n-1,n\rangle&-\langle j,j+1,n-2,n\rangle\end{pmatrix}={\left\langle\!\left\langle\,n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;j,j+1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}=0.

By Lemma
4.10
the last equality is equivalent to
{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j,j+1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}=0.
Note that in this case
j>i.

We proceed to the second possibility, the situation where under the shift from
D_{a}
to
D_{b}
the chord
c_{l}
ceases to be a top chord. This possibility happens precisely when in
D_{a}

c_{l}
has a sibling
c_{h}=(j,j+1,i,i+1),
and the shift under consideration is the right shift of
c_{h}
’s end. In this obstructed case
\theta_{h,l}
vanishes, and it is easy to see, as in the previous case, that this implies the vanishing of
{\left\langle\!\left\langle\,j,j+1\;{\big|}\;i,i+1\;{\big|}\;n-2,n-1\;{\big|}\;n\,\right\rangle\!\right\rangle}.
Note that in this case
j<i.

Finally, the third possibility for the shift is impossible, since
(i,i+1,n-2,n-1)
is a rightmost chord.

Any unmatched codimension
1
boundary of
S

maps to
\partial\widetilde{Z}(S_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}).

This is clear for boundaries of
S
which are contained in
S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}.
The
\widetilde{Z}
-image of an unmatched boundary
\partial_{\star}S_{a}
of some
S_{a},\penalty\ D_{a}\in\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}},
which is also a boundary of
S_{b},\penalty\ D_{b}\notin\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}},

is contained in the zero locus of a twistor or functionary, by the above analysis. These twistors and functionaries are exactly those which appear in the statement of Lemma
9.7
and that are shown to have a constant sign on
\widetilde{Z}(S_{i,n;k_{1},k-k_{2}}).
But every neighborhood of every
p\in\partial_{\star}S_{a}
also intersects
S_{b},
and the twistor or functionary which vanishes on
\partial_{\star}S_{a}
has the opposite sign on
\widetilde{Z}(S_{b}),
showing that indeed
\widetilde{Z}(\partial_{\star}S_{a})\subseteq\partial\widetilde{Z}(S_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}).

We are now in the setting of Proposition
8.5
, with
L=S_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}},
and subspaces
S_{a}
being the BCFW cells which correspond to
D_{a}\in\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}.
We have verified the additional assumptions required for applying the proposition in the proof Theorem
1.5
. Thus we can deduce that

\widetilde{Z}(\overline{S_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}})=\bigcup_{a:D_{a}\in\mathcal{CD}_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}}\overline{S_{a}}.

∎

A corollary of the proof, and of Proposition
8.5
is that the boundary of the image of
\overline{S_{i,n;k_{1},k_{2}}}
is the union of zero loci of the boundary twistors
\langle i,i+1,j,j+1\rangle,
and of the twistors and functionaries in the statement of Lemma
9.7
above.

Proof of Theorem
9.5
.

Let
Z^{\prime}
be the matrix obtained from
Z
by omitting the
n-1
th row.
By Theorems
1.3
-
1.5

\mathcal{BCFW}_{[n]\setminus\{n-1\},k},
the BCFW cells for the chord diagrams with
k
chords on the marker set
[n]\setminus\{n-1\},
triangulate
\mathcal{A}_{k,4,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}}(Z^{\prime}).
Clearly

\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}})=\mathcal{A}_{k,4,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}}(Z^{\prime}).

Moreover,
\mathcal{CD}_{[n]\setminus\{n\},k},
the chord diagrams which correspond to
\mathcal{BCFW}_{[n]\setminus\{n-1\},k},
are in natural bijection with chord diagrams on the marker set
[n],
without a chord ending at
(n-2,n-1).

{\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}
maps homeomorphically every BCFW cell of
\mathcal{BCFW}_{[n]\setminus\{n-1\},k}
to the cell of
\mathcal{BCFW}_{n,k}
corresponding to it under this bijection. Thus, the latter cells triangulate
\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}}).

The remaining cells triangulate the different
\widetilde{Z}(S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}}),
by Proposition
9.9
.
Theorem
1.5
implies that the union of closures of these sets covers the amplituhedron.

Regarding disjointness, we first show that
\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}})
and every
\widetilde{Z}(S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}})
are open. For
\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}})
this follows since

\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}})=\widetilde{Z^{\prime}}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}}),

where
\widetilde{Z^{\prime}}
is the amplituhedron map with respect to
Z^{\prime},
and amplituhedron maps are submersive when restricted to the positive Grassmannian, by Lemma
8.1
.

For
\widetilde{Z}(S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}})
we argue as follows. We assume
(k_{1},j)\neq(0,1),
but the treatment for this case is similar. Denote by
W_{L},W_{c},W_{R}
the following subspaces of
\mathbb{R}^{k+4}:

W_{L}=\mathrm{span}(Z_{1},\dots,Z_{j+1},Z_{n}),\penalty\ W_{c}=\mathrm{span}(Z_{j},Z_{j+1},Z_{n-2},Z_{n-1},Z_{n}),\penalty\ W_{R}=\mathrm{span}(Z_{j},\ldots,Z_{n-1}).

Let
M
be space of triplets
(Y_{L},\ell,Y_{R})\in\mathrm{Gr}_{k_{1}}(W_{L})\times\mathrm{Gr}_{1}(W_{c})\times\mathrm{Gr}_{k-k_{1}-1}(W_{R}),
which satisfy that, if we write
Y=Y_{L}+\ell+Y_{R}
, then
\dim(Y)=k,
and

Y+W_{R}=Y+W_{L}=Y+W_{c}=\mathbb{R}^{k+4}.

(12)

M
is open in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k_{1}}(W_{L})\times\mathrm{Gr}_{1}(W_{c})\times\mathrm{Gr}_{k-k_{1}-1}(W_{R}),
hence is a manifold.
We can write

\widetilde{Z}|_{S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}}}=\Phi\circ\Psi,

where
\Phi:M\to\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
takes
(Y_{L},\ell,Y_{R})
to
Y_{L}+\ell+Y_{R},

and
\Psi:S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}}\to M
takes, in the notations of Definition
9.4
, the vector space represented by
\Upsilon_{j}(s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2},L,R)
to
(L^{\prime}Z,vZ,R^{\prime}Z),
thought of as a triplet of vector spaces.
It is easy to verify that this map is well defined and independent of the choices of representatives, but it requires explanation why (
12
) is satisfied.
To see this, note that in order to show that
Y+W_{R}
(
Y+W_{L},Y+W_{c}
resp.) spans
\mathbb{R}^{k+4},
it is enough to find a non zero twistor
\langle Yi_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\rangle\neq 0
where
i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}\in\{n,1,2,\ldots,j+1\}
(
\{j,j+1,\ldots,n-1\},

\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}
resp.). For
W_{L},W_{R}
we can just take
i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}
to be four cyclically consecutive indices in

\{n,1,2,\ldots,j+1\}
or
\{j,j+1,\ldots,n-1\},
respectively.
By Lemma
4.13
, and the easily checked fact that
S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}}\cap S_{\partial\mathcal{A}}=\emptyset,
we deduce that
\langle Yi_{1},i_{2},i_{3},i_{4}\rangle\neq 0.
For
W_{c}
we can take
i_{1},\ldots,i_{4}
to be any subset of size four of
\{j,j+1,n-2,n-1,n\}
and deduce the same, this time using Lemma
9.7
.

We now argue that
\Phi,\Psi
are submersions, and hence also
\widetilde{Z}|_{S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}}},
and this will imply that
\widetilde{Z}({S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}}})
is open. Both claims are simple, but somehow lengthy. We now sketch the proof for
\Psi;
the proof for
\Phi
is an exercise in linear algebra, which we omit. Fix
p\in S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}}.
We need to show that

d\Psi:T_{p}S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}}\to T_{\Psi(p)}M

surjects. If
p
has a representative of the form
\Upsilon_{j}(s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2},L,R)
then it is easy to see, even in the level of matrix representatives of the triplets of vectors spaces which form elements of
M,
that variations at the
L-
directions, while keeping
R,s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2}
fixed, surject on the
Y_{L}-
directions of
T_{\Psi(p)}M
and map to
0
on the
\ell,Y_{R}
directions. An analogous claim holds for the
R-
directions. Variations at the
(s_{1},s_{2},t_{1},t_{2})
directions, while keeping
L,R
fixed, surject on the
\ell-
directions of
T_{\Psi(p)}M.
Thus
d\Psi
is a surjection at
p
.

Thus
\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{>}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}})
and every
\widetilde{Z}(S_{j,n;k_{1},k-1-k_{1}})
are open (in
\mathrm{Gr}_{k,k+4}
) and hence contained in the interiors of their closures.
As a consequence, if two such spaces
\widetilde{Z}(S_{1}),\widetilde{Z}(S_{2})
for
S_{1}\neq S_{2}
intersect, then their intersection contains an open ball.
Each
\widetilde{Z}(S_{i})\subseteq\bigcup_{D\in A_{i}}\overline{\widetilde{Z}(S_{D})},
where the union is taken over a collection
A_{i}
of chord diagrams, where
A_{i}=\mathcal{CD}_{j,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1}
if
S_{i}=S_{j,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1}
and otherwise
A_{i}=\mathcal{CD}_{n,k}\setminus\bigcup_{j,k_{1}}\mathcal{CD}_{j,n;k_{1},k-k_{1}-1},
and these set are all disjoint. Hence we can find BCFW cells
S_{a_{1}},S_{a_{2}}
for
D_{a_{1}}\in A_{1},D_{a_{2}}\in A_{2}
with a non empty intersection, contradicting Theorem
1.4
.
∎

As a final comment we note that
\widetilde{Z}({\mathrm{pre}}_{n-1}\mathrm{Gr}^{\geq}_{k,[n]\setminus\{n-1\}})
is precisely the subspace of
\mathcal{A}_{n,k,4}(Z)
on which all the twistors
\langle i,i+1,n-2,n\rangle
are nonnegative.
